# Chapter 1

[T]{.dropcap}he steam table at Deerbrook Falls Middle School held four hundred and twelve trays of chicken and rice by six forty-five in the morning, and Deb Hale had learned a long time ago that you could tell everything about a day by how the rice looked at six forty-five. Today it looked fine. A little dry at the edges of the pan, nothing a splash of broth wouldn’t fix. She fixed it.

“You’re doing the thing,” Marisol said, from the sink.

“I’m not doing a thing.”

“You’re doing the thing where you fix something that wasn’t broken because you don’t want to think about the thing that is.”

Deb set the ladle down harder than she meant to. Behind her the steam rose off the rice in a soft, unbothered column, like it had nowhere else to be. Outside the high square windows the parking lot was the color of a nickel, salt-stained and half-frozen, and the sky over Route 4 hadn’t fully committed to morning yet.

“April third,” Marisol said, when Deb didn’t answer. “You’ve had it circled since before Christmas.”

“I don’t have it circled.”

“You have it circled in red pen with a box around it, Deb. I’ve seen your calendar. You keep it on the fridge.”

She did keep it on the fridge. She’d looked at that box more times than she’d admit to, the way you touch a bruise to see if it still hurts, except this bruise hadn’t happened yet. Eighty-seven days out. She hadn’t done the math on purpose. It had just arrived one morning while she was making coffee, the number sitting there fully formed, the way numbers did to her sometimes, and she hadn’t been able to get rid of it since.

“He’s got school today,” she said instead. “He’s got the Bio test. And a short shift after.”

“That’s not what I asked.”

“I know what you asked.”

Marisol let it go, because that was the deal between them, twelve years of working a lunch line together teaching a person exactly how far to push and when to hand somebody a rag instead. Deb took the rag. Wiped down a counter that also didn’t need it. The cafeteria smelled like it always did this early, bleach and browning butter and the particular metal smell of four hundred trays waiting to be filled, and there was something almost holy about it, doing the same motions in the same order at the same hour, five days a week, for going on twenty years. Nobody in this building needed her to be anything other than reliable. She had gotten very good at reliable.

The rice went out at eleven-fifteen and was gone by twelve-fifty and the pan came back with about a cup left in it, which was the correct amount and which Deb noted with the flat satisfaction of a woman whose ordering had been right.

She had a system for the rice and had had it since 2011. You made eleven percent more than the count suggested, because the count was based on trays and not on sixth graders, and sixth graders ate rice the way water finds a drain.

Bev said, at the pass, “You did eleven percent again.”

“I always do eleven percent.”

“One of these days you’ll do ten and we’ll all find out what happens.”

“We know what happens,” Deb said. “It happened in 2014.”

“It was not that bad in 2014.”

“There were four hundred children and we ran out at twelve-forty and I have never fully recovered.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

At home, three miles east, Eli Vance was standing in front of the bathroom mirror doing the thing he did before school, nothing dramatic, just a slow, careful check, shirt collar, hair, the small scar above his eyebrow that had healed pale and thin and that he touched sometimes without noticing he was doing it. He counted his shirt buttons under his breath, out of some old habit that had started at the parts counter, where you counted the same box of spark plugs three times before you trusted the number, and he did the same thing now with buttons, with steps to the bus stop, with the exact number of days he had left in this house, though he tried not to let that last one turn into an actual count. It turned into one anyway. Fifteen weeks. Give or take.

He didn’t tell Deb he counted things. It wasn’t a secret so much as it was nobody’s business, the way his mother’s name wasn’t anybody’s business, the way the three houses before this one weren’t, the way a lot of things about the first fourteen years of his life had quietly filed themselves under nobody’s business and stayed there. Deb had never asked him to unfile any of it. That was one of maybe six things about her he’d trust with money, if it came to that.

He grabbed his bag. Down the stairs two at a time, the fourth one creaking as it always did, and into the kitchen where Deb had left a plate covered in foil like she did most mornings even though she was already gone to the school by the time he was up, egg and toast, the toast slightly overdone the way he liked it and she pretended not to remember was a preference.

There was a Post-it on the foil. *Bio test. You got this. Fridge has extra OJ. D*

He ate standing up, and did not let himself think about the box on the calendar, the one he’d also seen, the one he assumed she thought he hadn’t.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The kitchen at Deerbrook Falls Middle School had been built in 1968 and remodeled once, badly, in 1994, and Deb Hale knew every failure in it the way you know the sounds your own house makes at night.

The number two oven ran forty degrees hot and had since 2016 and the district’s position was that it was within tolerance. The floor by the dish pit had a low spot that held water, which was a slip hazard she had reported fourteen times in writing. The walk-in’s compressor was original. There was a light over the prep table that had never worked in twenty years and that she had personally given up on in 2011.

And it all worked. That was the thing she was proud of and had never once said out loud to anybody, including Marisol: that four hundred and twelve children ate at eleven-fifteen every single school day out of a room with a hot oven and a wet floor, and had done for two decades, and the reason it worked was that a woman had built a system on top of the failures instead of waiting for the failures to be fixed.

You put the rolls in number two and pulled them four minutes early. You kept the yellow cone by the dish pit permanently, not as a warning but as furniture. You did the walk-in inventory yourself on Fridays because the compressor would go one day and when it went you would need to know exactly what was in there within ninety seconds.

That was the job. Not the cooking. The cooking was the least of it.

Marisol came past with a hotel pan and said, “Two’s hot.”

“Two’s always hot.”

“I know two’s always hot. I’m telling you the rolls are in two.”

“Then pull them at nine minutes.”

“That’s what I’m doing. I’m narrating.” Marisol slid the pan onto the rack. “I like to say it out loud so that when the district finally replaces that oven there’ll be a record that we’ve been compensating for ten years.”

“There’s a record. It’s eleven emails.”

“Twelve,” said Marisol. “I sent one in November you don’t know about.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The bus was late, which put him at the school with four minutes instead of ten, which he minded more than he’d have admitted to anyone. The Civic stayed home on school days; gas was for work. Eli liked margins. He liked knowing where the edge of a thing was before he got to it. The Bio test he wasn’t worried about; Ms. Alvarez ran a fair room and he’d actually studied, actually understood the Punnett squares this time instead of just pattern-matching his way through them, and there was a small clean satisfaction in that, in a thing making sense because you’d made it make sense.

He got a ninety-four. He knew it before she handed it back, because he could feel when he’d gotten something right, a kind of quiet in his chest that didn’t need confirming, and getting the paper back with the number on it was less like finding something out and more like watching someone else agree with what he already knew.

At the parts counter that afternoon, a short shift straight from school, he sold a man from Springfield a serpentine belt and told him, correctly, that the tensioner pulley was the actual problem and the belt alone wouldn’t fix it, and the man looked at him the particular way adults looked at him sometimes, surprised that someone seventeen could be right about something mechanical, and Eli didn’t say anything about that look because there wasn’t anything worth saying. He just wrote up the second part.

The Punnett square thing was worth a minute of his attention on the walk to the bus, because it had done something to him he hadn’t expected.

He’d been failing at it for a week. Not failing the class; failing the concept, which was worse, because the class he could pass by pattern-matching and he knew he could and it made him feel like a fraud in a way he had no interest in examining.

The specific problem had been that he could produce the right answer without knowing why, and he had been able to do that with a great many things for a great many years, and it had gotten him through three schools and four houses, and he had a private rule about it which was: *anything you can only do by pattern is a thing that will fail you the first time the pattern changes.*

He’d sat at the kitchen table two weeks ago getting quietly furious about it, and Deb had made him put the pencil down and eat something.

That was all she’d done. She hadn’t explained it. She could not have explained it; she’d have been the first to say so. She’d said “eat something” and put a plate down and gone back to what she was doing, and forty minutes later he’d come back to the diagrams with a full stomach and it had opened like a door.

He got a ninety-four.

And what he’d thought, sitting in Ms. Alvarez’s room with the paper in his hand, was not *I’m good at this.* It was: *there is a house I can be stupid in for forty minutes without it costing me anything.*

He had never had one of those, and there was no way to say it to anybody at all.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

His manager, Gary, fifty-something and built like a filing cabinet, came by partway through the shift with two coffees, one of which he set down in front of Eli without a word, which was as close as Gary came to saying anything kind out loud, and said, “Heard you’re graduating in June.”

“Yeah.”

“Good. You want more hours after, come talk to me. I mean that.”

“I know you do.”

Gary looked at him a second longer than the sentence needed, like he was deciding whether to say something else, and then didn’t, which Eli appreciated more than he’d have known how to explain. Some people, when they found out what you were, tried to say something about it. Gary just handed you a coffee and offered you hours. Eli had decided a while back that the second kind of person was worth more than the first, by a wide margin.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The afternoon at that school ran on a schedule so fixed that Deb could have told you, at any given moment between six-thirty and two, what she would be doing and where her hands would be.

Eleven-fifteen: sixth grade. Eleven-forty-five: seventh. Twelve-fifteen: eighth, who ate like men and complained like lawyers. Twelve-fifty: the line closed and the second half of the job started, which was the half nobody saw.

Wipe-down. Dish pit. Counts. Temperature logs, a legal document, which she had never falsified and had been asked to twice, both times in 2013, by a district employee who no longer worked there. Waste log. Tomorrow’s thaw pulled from the freezer to the walk-in, which had to happen by one-thirty or the thing would not be thawed by six-thirty and the whole of tomorrow would be an improvisation.

Then the paperwork, which had grown every single year since 2006 and which nobody in the district office had ever once acknowledged was growing.

At one-forty Andre the dishwasher put his head around the office door and said, “The disposal’s making the noise.”

“Which noise?”

“The bad one.”

“Is it the bad one or is it the other bad one?”

“It’s the one from October,” Andre said.

Deb put her pen down. “That’s the other bad one. That’s a spoon.”

It was a spoon. It was always a spoon. She had been retrieving spoons from that disposal since the second Bush administration, and she got it out in ninety seconds with the water off and her sleeve rolled, and Andre said “how do you always know” and Deb said “because in twenty years there has never once been anything in there except a spoon,” and went back to the temperature logs.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Deb got home at five forty, later than usual because the walk-in cooler at school had thrown a fit and she’d had to wait for the repair guy, and found the house smelling faintly of somebody else’s dinner, which meant Eli had gotten home before her and eaten something and cleaned up after himself well enough that she couldn’t tell what, exactly, which was its own small miracle for a seventeen-year-old.

He was at the kitchen table with the biology test in front of him, not looking at it exactly, just having it there the way you keep a good grade around for a minute before you put it away.

“Ninety-four,” he said, without her asking.

“Look at you.”

“Punnett squares.” Like that explained something, and to her it did, a little, because she’d sat with him at this same table two weeks ago while he’d tried to get the diagrams to make sense and gotten quietly furious with himself in the specific, contained way he got furious, no yelling, just his jaw working and his pencil pressing too hard into the paper, and she’d made him put the pencil down and eat something and come back to it, and apparently that had worked, or something had.

“I’m making the chicken thing,” she said. “The one with the lemon.”

“You always make too much.”

“I like leftovers.”

He looked at her for a second in the particular way he had, assessing, like he was deciding whether to let something go or not, and then let it go, because they both knew she cooked for four in a house of two and had for three and a half years, and neither of them had ever said out loud what that was actually about.

She made the chicken. He did his English reading at the table while she cooked, and somewhere in there Marisol texted her a picture of her own dinner disaster, something involving a casserole and a smoke detector, and Deb laughed out loud in her kitchen for no reason Eli could see, and he looked up.

“Marisol set something on fire again.”

“Just a little.”

“She’s set something on fire like six times this school year.”

“Seven, if you count the Crock-Pot at Thanksgiving.”

He almost smiled. Deb counted that as a win, the almost, the way you’d count a coin you found on the sidewalk, small, unearned, worth bending down for anyway.

They ate at six-thirty, the television off, the window over the sink gone dark at a quarter past five the way it does in the first week of January, and it was, on the surface, an entirely ordinary Tuesday, a woman and a boy who was not legally hers eating chicken with lemon in a kitchen that smelled like garlic and dish soap, and neither of them mentioned the calendar on the fridge, the one with April third boxed in red, eighty-seven days out, eighty-six by morning, closing in the same unhurried way it had been closing since before Christmas.

Deb washed the dishes. Eli dried, because that was the arrangement, had been since the first month, back when he still flinched slightly if she reached past him too fast and she’d learned to always tell him where her hands were going before they went there. He didn’t flinch anymore. She still told him anyway. Some habits you kept not because they were needed but because of what it meant that you’d started them.

“I got the schedule for the Bridges thing,” she said, drying her hands on the dish towel, casual, the way you’d mention the weather, though she’d rehearsed saying it exactly like that in the car on the way home. “Renee sent it over. Said if you want to look at it before, then…”

“I saw it.”

“Okay.”

“I’m not doing it, Deb.”

“I know. I’m not saying you have to look at it to do it. I’m saying you can look at it.”

He set the last plate in the rack, careful, the way he did everything with his hands, like the plate had a right to be handled gently regardless of what he currently thought about anything else in his life.

“I already know what I want,” he said, and it wasn’t sharp, wasn’t a door closing, just a fact laid down flat on the counter between them, without heat, without asking her to feel any particular way about it.

“Okay,” she said again, and meant it as far as it went, and did not say the rest of what was sitting behind her ribs, which had no good shape yet, which she wasn’t sure she had a right to say to a boy who owed her, by any honest accounting, absolutely nothing at all.

Outside, the last of the salt-gray snow along the curb had gone soft at the edges, the first real thaw of the year, not that either of them was watching for it.


# Chapter 2

[T]{.dropcap}he house on a Thursday afternoon in January before a home visit had a particular quality that Deb had spent four years trying to eliminate and had never quite managed.

Nothing was different. She had learned that lesson in the first year and had kept to it since. She did not clean specially, she did not bake, she did not tell Eli to be anywhere in particular.

And still, from about two in the afternoon, the house waited.

She could not describe it any other way and had once tried, to Marisol, and had failed. It was not fear. There was nothing to fear; Renee had been coming for four years, and the file said what the file said, and there had never been an issue. It was that for two hours a month, a woman with a folder was going to sit at her table and make a written record of whether a child was all right in her house, and no matter how many times that record came out fine, the two hours before it had a temperature.

She had asked Renee about it once, in the third year.

“Does it ever stop feeling like an inspection?”

“No,” Renee had said. “And if it does, tell me, because that’s when I start worrying about the placement.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Renee Ostrowski drove a maroon Dodge Caravan with a hundred and ninety thousand miles on it and a passenger door that had to be pulled twice, and she arrived at Deb’s house on the second Thursday of January with a folder under her arm and road salt to the ankles of both boots.

“Don’t take your shoes off,” Deb said. “The floor’s older than both of us.”

“I’m taking them off. I’ve got a hole in the left one and I’d rather you didn’t see it.”

She took them off. Deb made coffee. This was the fourth year of these visits, monthly, the same kitchen table, the same three questions in slightly different order, and somewhere along the way the visits had started to feel, underneath the inspection, like a woman coming over. Not a friend, exactly. Renee had been careful about that line, and Deb respected it. But something.

Deb had made the coffee before Renee arrived, which she did every time, and had put out the good mugs, which she also did every time and which Renee had commented on once, in 2022, saying “you don’t have to do the mugs,” and Deb had said “I know,” and had continued doing the mugs.

There was a whole grammar to the visits and it had taken about a year to learn.

You did not clean the house specially. That was the first rule and Deb had broken it for eight months before working out that a house cleaned specially for a caseworker reads exactly as a house cleaned specially for a caseworker.

You did not hover during the private portion, when the caseworker talks to the child alone, and you did not go stand somewhere you could hear. Deb went out to the garage for those, every time, for four years, and stood by the workbench doing nothing for ten minutes.

You did not ask afterward what he’d said.

You did not, under any circumstances, use the visit to raise something you’d been avoiding raising directly, which was a thing Deb had watched a foster parent do at a support group in 2021 and had found so appalling that she had never gone back to the group.

And you told the truth about the bad weeks. That was the one that had taken longest and cost the most. In the first year Deb had presented every month as fine, because fine was what you presented, and Renee had sat in that kitchen and accepted fine, and then in the fourteenth month there had been a bad two weeks with a kid and Deb had had nowhere to put it because she had spent a year establishing that nothing was ever hard.

She had learned. By the third year, when Renee asked how it was going, Deb answered the actual question, and it had changed the entire nature of the visits, and it was the most useful thing she had figured out about the work.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Eli came down at ten past four, already in his work polo because his shift started at five, and sat where he always sat for these, at the end of the table, hands flat, answering everything in complete sentences. Yes, school was fine. Yes, he’d seen the dentist. No, nothing had happened that he wanted to report. He had been doing this since he was fourteen and he was extremely good at it, and it had taken Deb about a year and a half to understand that being good at it wasn’t a good thing.

Renee went through her list. Then she set her pen down, which she didn’t usually do.

“It’s confirmed,” she said. “April third, nine forty in the morning, Magistrate Meehan. Deb, I gave you the date in December and I know it’s been on that fridge since. This is me giving it to both of you with a time on it, because you’ll get formal notice in the mail and I’d rather you hear it from a person than an envelope.”

“April third,” Eli said. Not a question.

“Sixteen days before your birthday. That’s just how the docket fell.”

He nodded, once. Nothing on his face at all, and Deb watched him do it and thought about how a person learns that. Nobody’s born able to hear a date like that and give a room nothing back.

“One more thing,” Renee said. “I’ll say it once and then leave it. You’re both adults and I don’t do pressure.” She looked at Eli when she said it, not Deb, which Deb noticed and appreciated more than she could have said. “The agency would support an adoption petition. If that’s something either of you wanted. There’s time to file, barely, and Magistrate Meehan will ask you directly at the hearing whether you consent, because at your age she has to ask you, not us, and not Deb. Your answer is the answer. That’s the law and it’s a good law.”

The refrigerator hummed. Out in the street a plow went by, scraping, throwing a wave of gray slush over the curb.

“Okay,” Eli said.

“That’s not a question I need answered today.”

“Okay,” he said again, and stood up, and put his mug in the sink and rinsed it, because he always rinsed it, and picked up his keys. “I’ve got five o’clock.”

“Go,” Deb said.

He went. The door did its thing where it stuck and then gave, and then the house was two women and a folder.

Renee didn’t say anything for a second. She turned her coffee cup a quarter turn on the table, the way people do when they’re deciding what they’re allowed to say.

“He’ll say no,” Deb said.

“He might.”

“He’ll say no. I’ve known that for about a year.” She was surprised by how level it came out. “He’s told me in about nine ways that aren’t sentences.”

“That’s usually how they tell you.” Renee closed the folder. “For what it’s worth, and I’m not supposed to say this part, it’s not about you. I’ve watched a lot of kids get to seventeen and a half in this county. The ones who say no at that age are almost never saying no to the person asking.”

“Then what are they saying no to?”

“Being fixed,” Renee said. “At the end. By somebody who’s been kind to them the whole way and now needs them to accept a thing so it can be finished properly. They can smell it, Deb. It’s the single thing they’re best at smelling.”

Deb laughed, one hard breath through the nose, not because it was funny.

“You think that’s what I’d be doing.”

“No.” Renee stood and started the long process of getting back into her boots. “I think you’d be doing it because you love him and adoption’s the only form they hand you for that. And I think he’d be saying no for reasons that have nothing to do with whether he loves you back. Both. That’s the job. That’s why I’m gray.”

“You’re not gray.”

“I’m forty-six and I’ve got a stripe over this ear like a badger.”

She got her coat on. At the door she stopped, hand on the frame.

“Ask him anyway,” she said. “Not for the answer. He should get to hear the offer out loud from you, once, in a room where it’s just the two of you and there’s no magistrate involved. Kids like Eli spend their whole lives being offered things by institutions. It’s different when it’s a person.”

Then she went out to the Caravan and the Caravan did not start.

It made the sound. That flat, unhopeful *rr-rr-rr* that means the day is going to be longer than planned, and Renee said a word Deb had never heard her say, and put her forehead on the steering wheel for exactly two seconds before getting out to look under the hood, which she plainly did not know how to do.

Eli’s car came back up the drive four minutes later. He’d forgotten his name tag.

He looked at the Caravan. He looked at Renee, who was holding the hood open and frowning at the engine with tremendous authority.

“Battery terminal’s corroded,” he said, from twelve feet away, without moving.

“You can see that from there?”

“It’s green. It’s supposed to not be green.”

He got his name tag from the house, came back out, went into his trunk for a wire brush and a bottle of Coke that had been in there since October, and did something to the terminal that took under four minutes and involved pouring soda on a car engine, which Renee narrated to Deb the entire time in a tone of genuine outrage.

“He’s pouring pop on it. Deb. He is pouring pop on a vehicle I have to drive to Springfield.”

“It works,” Eli said.

“That cannot possibly work.”

It worked. The van turned over on the first try and Renee sat there with her hands on the wheel looking betrayed by the entire field of automotive science, and Eli shut the hood, and wiped his hands on a rag from his pocket, and said, “You need a new battery, though. That’s a patch. Two, three weeks maybe.”

“How much is a battery?”

“Depends. Come by the store, I’ll do the math for you.”

“You’ll do the math for me,” Renee repeated. “You know I’ve been doing this job since before you were born.”

“Yeah,” said Eli, “but you poured no pop on anything,” and got in his car and backed down the drive, and Renee turned to Deb with her mouth open, and Deb was still laughing about it forty minutes later while she scrubbed a pot that was already clean.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Renee Ostrowski had carried a caseload of between nineteen and thirty-one children continuously since 2003, and Deb had worked out fairly early that the visits to her house were the easy ones, and that this was not a compliment so much as a description of a person’s Tuesday.

She’d learned things by accident over four years. That Renee’s caseload had a boy in it who had been in nine placements in six years and that this fact lived somewhere behind her eyes in every conversation. That she drove between forty and ninety miles a day. That the agency had lost four caseworkers in eighteen months during 2022 and that Renee had absorbed a portion of each departure and had never mentioned it as a hardship, only as a logistics problem.

That she had, at some point, been very good at something else. There was a piano in a photograph on her phone that she’d shown Deb by accident while looking for a form, an upright in a room with a window, and when Deb had asked about it Renee had said “that’s my mother’s” and then, four seconds later, “I played until I was twenty-six,” and had gone back to the form.

Nobody asked caseworkers anything. Deb had figured that out and had made a small private policy of asking Renee one question per visit that was not about a child, and Renee had noticed the policy inside of three months and had never acknowledged it, and had answered every single question.

This visit’s question, asked while Renee was getting her boots back on, was: “Do you get a lunch break?”

Renee looked up from the boot.

“That’s your one?”

“That’s my one.”

“No,” Renee said. “I eat in the car. I’ve eaten in the car since 2011. There’s a spot behind the library in Miamisburg where I park, and I have twenty minutes, and I do not answer the phone.”

“Every day?”

“Every day I’m not in court.” She got the boot on. “Twenty-two years in child protection and what I’ve got is a parking spot behind a library.”

“That’s not enough.”

“No,” Renee agreed, standing up, “it is not,” and picked up her folder, and went out to the van.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She asked him that night. Not the way she’d planned, because the way she’d planned had a beginning and this didn’t.

He came in at eight-twenty smelling like the store, that specific cold-rubber-and-cardboard smell, and got the plate out of the fridge himself, and she was at the table with the crossword she wasn’t doing.

“Renee say anything else after I left?”

“She said her van’s a disgrace.”

“It is.” He put the plate in the microwave. Punched the numbers. “Anything else?”

Here it was. The whole afternoon had been leaning toward this and she still wasn’t ready, and she thought, distantly, that you’re never ready, that being ready is a thing people say about conversations they didn’t actually have.

“I want to say something and I want to say it once,” she said. “And then I’m not bringing it up again unless you do.”

He didn’t turn around. The microwave hummed and turned.

“If you wanted me to file,” Deb said, “I would file. Tomorrow. It’s not too late, and it wouldn’t be because anybody told me to, and it wouldn’t be to fix anything, and it wouldn’t be a favor I was doing you. I’d do it because I want to. That’s the only reason.”

The microwave beeped. He didn’t open it.

“Okay,” he said.

“You don’t have to answer.”

“I know.”

“I mean it, Eli. Not tonight, not this week, not at all if you don’t want to. I just wanted you to have heard it from me and not from a woman in a robe with a docket.”

He opened the microwave then. Steam came up off the chicken. He stood there looking into it with his back to her, and his shoulders did something, once, a small tightening and release that she would think about later, that she would turn over and over for weeks after, trying to decide what it had been.

“The rice is dry,” he said.

“I know. The pan sat.”

“You always say that.”

“It’s always true.”

He took the plate to the table and sat down across from her and ate, and they talked about the Caravan, and about whether Coke was an acceptable engineering solution, and about a man who’d come into the store that evening and tried to return an alternator he had visibly hit with a hammer, and it was, again, on the surface, entirely ordinary, and she let it be, because the thing she had needed to put in the room was in the room now, and he had not picked it up, and she had known he wouldn’t, and she had done it anyway.

That was the whole of what she had. She was fifty-four years old and she ran a lunch line and she could not adopt a boy who did not want adopting, and what she could do was set a thing on the table and not require him to take it.

Later, upstairs, she heard him counting. Very quietly, through the wall, the way he did. She couldn’t make out the numbers. She never could.


# Chapter 3

[T]{.dropcap}he guidance office at Deerbrook Falls High had four motivational posters on its walls, and Eli had ranked them privately by how much he hated them. Fourth place was a kitten on a branch. Third was a mountain with a word under it. Second was a rowing team, eight men in a boat, above a sentence about how nobody wins alone, which he objected to on the grounds that it was factually untrue in a large number of cases. First place, undefeated for three years, was a photograph of an open road at sunset with the words YOUR FUTURE STARTS TODAY, which he considered an actively hostile thing to hang in a room where seventeen-year-olds were told what their transcripts looked like.

“You’re staring at the road one again,” Mrs. Kadeem said.

“It’s a good poster.”

“You’ve never meant that.”

“I mean it deeply.”

Mrs. Kadeem had been a counselor for twenty-nine years and had a laminated sign on her desk that said I AM NOT YOUR MOTHER, BUT, which was the only piece of decor in the room he had any respect for. She pulled his file up on the computer, and then, because she was the kind of person who did this, closed the laptop halfway so it wasn’t between them.

“Okay,” she said. “June. You graduate. Then what.”

“I’ve got it worked out.”

“Everybody’s got it worked out. Boy came in here yesterday with a plan to become a professional streamer. Had a folder and everything.”

“I have a folder.”

“Eli.”

He took the folder out of his bag and put it on her desk. It was a real folder, green, with tabs, and she looked at it the way people look at a thing they were prepared to be gentle about and now have to take seriously.

She opened it.

Inside, in order: a printout of the AutoWorks pay scale with the counter-to-parts-manager progression circled and the wage at each step written in the margin. A listing for a room in a house on Pryor Street, four hundred a month including utilities, with two other listings behind it as backup, both cheaper, both worse. A one-page budget with a column for what he made now and a column for what he’d make on full hours, and a line at the bottom marked SLACK that had a hundred and ten dollars in it. A community college course catalogue with the automotive technology certificate program flagged, spring intake, with the cost per credit hour and a note about the education and training voucher he’d be eligible for as a former foster youth, the asset limit written out, ten thousand, underlined twice, with a small dry note next to it in his handwriting: *not a problem.*

A photocopy of his birth certificate. A photocopy of his social security card. A list, handwritten, headed DOCUMENTS I NEED AND WHERE THEY ARE.

Mrs. Kadeem read all of it. It took a while. She didn’t say anything the whole time, and Eli sat there with his hands flat on his knees and made himself not fill the silence, which was a discipline he’d been working on.

“How long have you been building this,” she said finally.

“Since I was fifteen.”

“Fifteen.”

“You don’t get to seventeen in the system without noticing there’s an eighteen.” He shrugged, one shoulder. “It’s not sad. Everybody makes it sad. It’s just a date. You can plan around a date.”

She sat back. Took her glasses off, which was, he had learned, the tell for a woman about to say something she hadn’t prepared.

“You know what the failure mode is here,” she said. “Not the money. The money’s fine, this is better money planning than most of the adults in this building could do. It’s that this folder has one name in it. Yours. There’s not a single line in here that assumes anybody helps you.”

“Because nobody has to.”

“That’s not the same as nobody will.”

“It’s the same as far as the folder’s concerned.”

Mrs. Kadeem put her glasses back on.

“I want you to add a page,” she said. “Not now. Sometime before June. A page of people. Names and numbers, of people you’d actually call. And I want it to have more than one name on it, because I’ve watched a lot of kids leave this building with a green folder, and the ones who come back to tell me about it are never the ones with the best budget. They’re the ones who had somebody to call at eleven at night about a thing that turned out to be nothing.”

“That’s a poster,” Eli said.

“That’s absolutely a poster, and it’s still true. Add the page.”

He put the folder back in his bag.

At the door she said, “Vance. This is very good work. I don’t say that.”

“You say it constantly.”

“I say it about essays. That’s different.” She was already opening the laptop again. “Go to class.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

He had come into that office with the folder in his bag and had not planned to take it out.

That was worth being honest about, because he was fairly rigorous with himself and had been since about fifteen. The plan had been to say he had it worked out, absorb four minutes of a woman being kind at him, and leave.

What changed it was that she closed the laptop.

Not all the way. Halfway, so it wasn’t between them, and it was a small thing and he had watched adults do the opposite of it several hundred times: the screen up, the eye contact over the top of it, the typing while you talk. A person can be entirely sincere and still be conducting you through a workflow.

Mrs. Kadeem closed the laptop halfway and put her hands on the desk, and Eli Vance, seventeen, took a folder out of his bag that nobody had ever seen.

He thought about that on the walk to third period. It was a useful piece of information about himself and he filed it, the way he filed things: *four minutes of undivided attention gets more out of me than four years of being told I can talk about anything.*

He wondered, briefly, whether Deb had figured that out about him deliberately or by accident.

He decided, walking into American history, that it did not actually matter which.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The rest of the day was a Tuesday, the first one back after Christmas.

Third period was American history with Mr. Dunleavy, who had taught in that building for thirty-one years and had a voice like a man reading a phone book to a jury, and who was, in Eli’s private assessment, the most underrated teacher in the school. Dunleavy did not care whether you liked him. He did not do activities. He wrote four things on the board at the start of class and by the end of it you understood all four, and if you didn’t, he would stay after and explain it again in the same voice with no visible resentment whatsoever.

Eli had a lot of time for Mr. Dunleavy.

Fourth was gym, which he had successfully avoided caring about for four years. Fifth was lunch.

He sat where he sat, which was the end of the third table with Trevor and a kid called Amos Reyna who spoke perhaps twenty words a week and who had, over two years, become a genuine friend by a process neither of them could have described. Trevor talked. That was the arrangement. Trevor talked for approximately twenty-two minutes about a video game, a girl in his chemistry class, his mother’s opinion about the girl in his chemistry class, and a truck he wanted, and Eli and Amos ate and made noises at intervals, and it was one of the two or three most restful periods in Eli’s week.

“You’re doing the thing,” Trevor said, at minute nineteen.

“What thing?”

“You go quiet in the middle of March every year and then you’re fine in May. You did it last year.”

Eli looked up.

“You noticed that?”

“I’ve known you four years, man.” Trevor was working his way through a sandwich the size of a brick. “It’s January. You’re about two months early. And the year before you were quiet the whole spring but that was because of the thing with your knee.”

“It wasn’t my knee.”

“You said it was your knee.”

“I know what I said.”

Trevor chewed. Amos, who missed nothing and said nothing, looked at neither of them.

“Anyway,” Trevor said, “if it’s a thing, it’s a thing, and if it’s not, it’s not.” And then, in exactly the same tone: “You want to hear about the truck?”

“No.”

“It’s a 2011 Silverado.”

“I said no.”

“It’s got a hundred and forty thousand on it and the guy wants six.”

“Six is too much,” Eli said, defeated, and Trevor grinned, because that was the entire objective, and talked about the Silverado for ten minutes.

Later, walking to sixth period, Amos Reyna said, “March.”

“What?”

“You’re early,” Amos said.

And then he went into a classroom and that was the entirety of what Amos Reyna said to Eli Vance that week, and Eli stood in the hallway for a second with his bag on one shoulder and found that he did not entirely mind it.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

He thought about it on the bus and decided he’d add the page, eventually, and privately admitted that he already knew what the problem with the page was, which was that he could think of exactly four names for it, and one of them was Deb, and putting her on a list of people he’d call at eleven at night felt like a thing he had to be careful about, because you could get used to a name being on a list. You could get so used to it that when it came off, the list stopped working entirely.

The other three were Gary, Mrs. Kadeem, and a kid named Trevor Pham whose garage currently held two boxes of Eli’s things and who had asked no questions about it whatsoever, which was the single greatest thing anyone had ever done for him.

Four was probably fine. Four was more than a lot of people had.

He did not add the page that week, or the next one.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Mrs. Kadeem called Deb that afternoon, which she had been doing roughly once a quarter for four years and which Eli had never known about.

“He came in.”

“How was he?”

“He had a folder.” There was a sound of a chair. “Deb, he had a laminated pay scale from an auto parts store with the manager progression circled.”

“That sounds right.”

“He’s seventeen.”

“I’m aware of how old he is.”

“I’ve got two hundred and eighty seniors,” Mrs. Kadeem said. “Nine of them have a plan. Four of those plans are realistic. His is the only one with a contingency in it.” A pause. “There’s a line at the bottom of his budget called slack.”

Deb sat down on the bench outside the school office.

“I know about the slack line.”

“He put a hundred and ten dollars a month in it and he explained to me, unprompted, that it wasn’t savings, it was for the month something goes wrong, and that it goes back to zero if he uses it and he starts again.” Kadeem exhaled. “I’ve been doing this twenty-nine years. That’s a forty-year-old’s line.”

“Yes.”

“So here’s why I called.” Her voice changed slightly. “I told him to add a page of people. Names and numbers. And I want you to know I did that, because he’ll either do it or he won’t, and if he does it I want somebody in his life to know why there’s suddenly a page of people, and if he doesn’t do it I want somebody in his life to know that too.”

Deb looked at the floor of the hallway.

“How many kids do you say that to?”

“All of them. Every year. I’ve said it about four hundred times.”

“Does it work?”

“Almost never,” Mrs. Kadeem said. “About one in nine. But it’s free and it takes forty seconds and the one in nine is worth it, and I’d have retired in 2019 if I thought about it any other way.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

At home, Deb had discovered the pool catalogue.

It had come in the mail on a Wednesday, addressed to a Mr. R. Halstead who had not lived at that address since before Deb bought the house, and it was forty pages of above-ground swimming pools photographed in backyards that did not appear to be in Ohio.

“Look at this one,” she said, at dinner, holding it up. “It’s got a slide.”

“Why do you have that.”

“It came. It’s mine now. I’m considering my options.”

“You don’t have a yard.”

“I have a yard.”

“You have a driveway and an opinion about the neighbor’s fence.”

“That is a yard, legally.” She turned a page. “This one comes with a filter system rated for four thousand gallons. Do you know how much four thousand gallons is?”

“Yes. It’s four thousand gallons.”

“It’s a lot, Eli.”

“That’s what I said.”

She read him the specifications of a pool called the Bayside Deluxe for a good ten minutes while he ate, and he objected to individual claims in the copy on technical grounds, and by the end of it the catalogue had a place on top of the microwave where it would stay for the next three months, and both of them had, without discussing it, begun to treat the acquisition of an above-ground pool as an ongoing household project that neither of them intended to complete.

She put the catalogue up there. He didn’t move it. Later that would matter, in the way objects matter once the person who argued with you about them has moved out, but on that Wednesday in January it was only a stupid pool with a slide, and the two of them arguing about gallons, and the dishes done in the usual order.


# Chapter 4

[J]{.dropcap}anuary the tenth was the first Saturday of the year that either of them had free, and Marisol had been proposing it since the second.

“I’m going Saturday.”

“I know you’re going Saturday. You’ve said.”

“I’m saying it again because last year you agreed to come and then a boy needed a coat.”

“A boy did need a coat.”

“A boy needed a coat in the middle of a Saturday I had cleared,” Marisol said, “and I want it noted that I have never complained about it and am not complaining now, I am establishing a pattern.”

So they went.

It had been a ritual for about six years and it had a structure, and the structure was not negotiable, and Deb had once suggested doing books first and had been looked at.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The Deerbrook Falls Goodwill on a Saturday morning in January was the warmest building in the county, and Marisol Ferreira treated it as a competitive sport.

“Housewares first,” she said, in the parking lot, like a general. “Then books. We do not do clothes today, I have no discipline about clothes.”

“You have no discipline about anything.”

“I have tremendous discipline. I have simply chosen not to apply it to a coat rack.”

They went in. The smell was the same as every thrift store on earth, dust and detergent and other people’s houses, and the radio behind the counter was playing something with a lot of saxophone in it. Deb liked it here for reasons she’d never bothered to examine. Six years of a foster license meant a lot of Saturdays acquiring things fast: a winter coat for a kid who’d arrived without one, sheets, a desk lamp, a duffel bag for a boy who’d been moving his life in trash bags and hadn’t said anything about it because he was fourteen and didn’t yet know he was allowed to mind.

She’d bought that duffel bag right there, second aisle, eleven dollars, three and a half years ago.

“You’re doing the face,” Marisol said.

“I don’t have a face.”

“You’ve got a whole face. You’ve had it since the parking lot.”

Deb picked up a gravy boat shaped like a rooster and put it down again.

They worked the aisles. Marisol found a cast-iron pan for four dollars and became insufferable about it. There was a rack of coffee mugs that Deb went down slowly, and a bin of extension cords, and a shelf of small appliances with their cords wound and taped, waffle irons and blenders and one enormous stand mixer that Marisol tried to lift and immediately regretted.

And then, on the end of the housewares aisle, a boxed set of dishes.

They were not nice. Stoneware, off-white with a thin blue band, eight place settings, three of them chipped, in a cardboard box that had been opened and re-taped by somebody at the store. Sixteen dollars for the lot. There was a hand-lettered sign on it that said AS IS.

Deb stood in front of it for long enough that Marisol came back down the aisle to find her.

“You have dishes.”

“I know.”

“You have the dishes we ate off of on Thanksgiving. Which were fine. Which had no chips in them.”

“I know what dishes I have.”

Marisol looked at the box. Then at Deb. Then, in a slightly different voice, “Deb.”

“They’re sixteen dollars.”

“That’s not the part I’m asking about.”

Deb picked up the box. It was heavier than she expected, and the cardboard gave slightly under her hands, and she stood there in the middle of a thrift store on a Saturday holding sixteen dollars’ worth of somebody else’s dishes with her jaw set.

“He’ll need dishes,” she said.

The saxophone kept going behind the counter. Somebody two aisles over dropped something plastic and said sorry to nobody.

“He hasn’t asked you for dishes,” Marisol said carefully.

“He won’t.”

“Deb.”

“He’s got a folder.” Deb heard herself say it and kept going anyway. “I found it on the table Monday night, he’d left it out. There’s a room on Pryor Street, four hundred a month, and a budget with a line at the bottom called slack, and a hundred and ten dollars in it. He’s had this thing since he was fifteen years old. Fifteen.” She shifted the box against her hip. “There’s nothing on the list about dishes. There’s nothing on it about a can opener either, or a mattress pad, or the fact that the first month you live alone you will absolutely get sick and have nothing in the house.”

“So say that to him.”

“I can’t say that to him.”

“Why can’t you say that to him?”

“Because he’d say thank you,” Deb said, “and then he’d carry it around.”

Marisol didn’t have anything for that immediately, which was rare.

Deb put the box in the cart.

“I’m not going to give them to him,” she said. “That’s the whole point. They’ll go in the basement, and if he needs them he’ll have them, and if he doesn’t he’ll never know they existed. That’s allowed. That’s not a crime.”

“Nobody said crime.”

“You’ve got a face too, you know.”

“I’ve got a beautiful face,” Marisol said, and took the cart handle, and started walking, and after a second added, without turning around, “Get the can opener too. The one on the pegboard, by the register. It’s a dollar and it’s the good kind with the handle.”

Deb got the can opener.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The books were next, because that was the order and Marisol did not deviate from the order.

Deb bought nothing. She went down the paperbacks with her hands behind her back like a woman in a museum, which was a habit she’d developed to stop herself buying more of them, and she read the spines of a dozen Nora Roberts novels and four copies of the same John Grisham and a shelf of the specific 1990s hardcovers that populate every thrift store in America in identical proportion.

There was a cookbook section. There always was. Church cookbooks, spiral bound, from congregations in Preble County and Darke County and one from a Methodist church in Deerbrook Falls itself, dated 1987.

She picked that one up.

It was the kind of thing she never bought and always opened. Recipes typed on a typewriter by somebody’s mother, three hundred of them, each with a name under it. *Marjorie Kepner’s Hot Chicken Salad. Eileen Sturgis’s Refrigerator Rolls.* Somebody had gone through this copy with a pen and put small checkmarks next to about forty of them and a single word next to one: *good.*

Deb stood in the aisle and looked at that for a while.

Then she put it back, and then took it out again, and bought it for a dollar fifty, and it went on the shelf over the microwave at home and she never once cooked out of it and she has it still.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

They had lunch after at a place on Fourth that did a decent Reuben and a terrible cup of coffee, and Marisol told a story about her sister’s third wedding that involved a rented tent and a raccoon, and Deb laughed until she had to put her sandwich down, actually laughed, the kind that hurts your face, and for twenty minutes there was nothing else in the world but a bad Reuben and a raccoon in a tent.

Then Marisol said, stirring the terrible coffee, “You know what you’re doing, right?”

“Eating lunch.”

“With the dishes.”

“Buying dishes is not a psychological event, Marisol. It was sixteen dollars.”

“I didn’t say a word about psychology.” Marisol tapped the spoon twice on the rim, set it down. “My mother did it. When my brother went into the Navy. Bought him a whole set of sheets he never slept on, and they sat in the hall closet at her place for nineteen years, and when she died I found them, still in the plastic, and I sat on the floor of that closet for a solid hour.”

Deb turned her cup a quarter turn.

“That’s a horrible story.”

“It’s not a horrible story, it’s a completely normal story, that’s what makes it horrible.” Marisol shrugged. “I’m not telling you not to buy the dishes. Buy the dishes. I’m telling you the dishes are for you.”

“I know they’re for me,” Deb said.

It came out fast and flat and truer than she’d intended, and it sat there on the table between the plates.

Marisol nodded slowly. Picked her sandwich back up.

“Okay,” she said. “Good. As long as one of us said it.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

“Can I tell you the thing about the duffel bag,” Deb said, in the car, in the Goodwill lot, before either of them had put a key in anything.

“You’ve never told me a thing about a duffel bag.”

“Because you’ve never sat in a parking lot with me.”

Marisol put her seatbelt back off.

“Go on then.”

“He came with a garbage bag,” Deb said. “Fourteen years old, September 2022, and he came up my driveway with a black garbage bag with everything he owned in it and a backpack. And it wasn’t a scandal, that’s the thing everybody gets wrong, it wasn’t neglect, the placement moved fast and there wasn’t a bag and somebody in an office did the best they could at four in the afternoon.”

“Okay.”

“And he carried it up the drive like it was a completely normal thing to be carrying, because for him it was, and he’d done it three times before.” She had her hands on the wheel of a parked car. “And I stood in the doorway and watched a child carry his whole life up my driveway in a bag you put grass in, and I said ‘come in, I’ve got soup,’ and I did not react in any visible way whatsoever, which took everything I had.”

“And the duffel.”

“I came here that Saturday. Eleven dollars. Second aisle, right there where the extension cords are.” Deb nodded at the store. “And I did not give it to him and say anything. I put it on the floor of his closet on a Tuesday when he was at school, and he never mentioned it, and it’s been on the floor of that closet for three and a half years, and it’s the bag he’ll leave with.”

Marisol looked out the windshield for a moment.

“You bought him a bag to leave with,” she said.

“I bought him a bag so that when he left, whenever that was, he wouldn’t do it with a garbage bag.” Deb put the key in. “That’s the reason. I did it in the first week. I’ve never once pretended to myself it was anything else.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The dishes went in the basement, on the shelf under the stairs, next to the Christmas things. Deb put a folded towel over the box, which she told herself was about dust.

She went down to the basement to do it, which meant going down the stairs with a cardboard box against her hip and pulling the light chain with two fingers, and standing in a basement that smelled the way basements smell in Ohio in January, concrete and cold and the ghost of a hundred loads of laundry.

The shelf under the stairs held, in order: four bins of Christmas, one bin marked HALLOWEEN/MISC that contained no Halloween items whatsoever, a box of Danny’s school things she had not opened since 2021, a broken humidifier she kept meaning to take somewhere, and a gap.

She put the dishes in the gap.

Then she stood there in the cold for a minute, looking at the box of Danny’s things, which was brown and taped and had DANNY - SCHOOL written on the side in her own handwriting from a decade ago.

She did not open it. She had opened it twice, once in 2019 and once in 2021, and both times had put it back inside of four minutes.

She got the folded towel off the dryer and put it over the dishes and went back upstairs.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Marisol texted her at nine that night: *did you put a towel on them*

Deb looked at the phone for a while.

*Yes.*

*i knew it. i said to myself in the car, she is going to put something over them.*

*It’s a basement. There’s dust.*

*sure,* Marisol sent, and then, four minutes later, *im not being funny. i think its fine. my mother did the sheets. everybody does a version.*

*What did you do?*

The dots came up and went away twice.

*my sons snowboard,* she sent eventually. *he wanted one when he was 16 and we couldnt do it that year and by the time we could he’d stopped asking. i bought it in 2019. its in my garage. hes 34.*

Deb sat with the phone in her hand at her own kitchen table.

*Marisol.*

*dont.*

*I’ve been in your garage.*

*you have not been in the back of my garage,* Marisol sent. *nobody has been in the back of my garage. see you tomorrow, bring the good coffee.*

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Eli came home at four from Trevor’s, and the house smelled like the good soup, the one with the little pasta in it, and he ate two bowls standing at the counter reading something on his phone, and said, around the second bowl, “There’s a raccoon story I need to hear about, apparently. Marisol texted me a picture of a tent.”

“She texted you?”

“She texts me a lot. She sent me a video of a goat last week.”

“Why is she texting you about goats.”

“I have no idea,” Eli said. “I’ve never encouraged it,” and went upstairs, and Deb stood in her kitchen with a ladle in her hand, smiling at a wall, in a house that had eight extra dinner plates in the basement that nobody was ever going to mention.


# Chapter 5

[A]{.dropcap}utoWorks on Route 4 had a fluorescent tube over aisle three that had been failing since October, and the sound it made was somewhere between a hum and an insect, and Gary refused to replace it on the grounds that it wasn’t dead yet.

“It’s dying,” Eli said.

“Dying’s not dead.”

“It’s making a noise.”

“So do I,” Gary said, and went back to the register.

The store on a Thursday evening in January was mostly empty and smelled like rubber and cardboard boxes and the particular cold that comes off a concrete floor no matter how high you run the heat. Eli liked the emptiness. He liked restocking, the small clean order of it, the way a shelf that was wrong could be made right with about ten minutes of attention and nobody had to have a feeling about it.

He was two-thirds down the wiper blade section when Gary came around the endcap with a clipboard, which meant something official, because Gary conducted all unofficial business without paper.

“You got a minute.”

“I’ve got the whole aisle.”

“Sit down.”

There was nowhere to sit, so they both stood by the oil filters, and Gary looked at the clipboard for a second the way people look at notes they wrote so they wouldn’t say it wrong.

“June,” he said. “You graduate. I said come talk to me. You didn’t come talk to me, so I’m coming to you, which is not how I like to do things.”

“Noted.”

“Thirty-eight hours to start. Not forty, because forty means benefits and Corporate has opinions about that at your age, and I’ll fight them on it in September when you’ve got three months in and I’ve got a leg to stand on.” He turned the clipboard around. There were actual numbers on it, printed, with a wage per hour and a line under it. “That’s what it looks like. That’s not a starting-a-conversation number, that’s the number. If you tell me yes I’ll put you on the June schedule tonight.”

Eli looked at it.

It was fifty cents an hour more than the figure in his folder. He had built the entire budget on the figure in his folder. Fifty cents an hour across thirty-eight hours came to nineteen dollars a week, which came to something over nine hundred a year, which meant the line at the bottom marked SLACK went from a hundred and ten dollars a month to a hundred and ninety-two, and he did the math standing there in under four seconds and felt something in his chest do a thing he had no interest in examining.

“Why fifty cents,” he said.

“Because you’re worth fifty cents.”

“That’s not an answer.”

“It’s the only one you’re getting.” Gary tapped the clipboard. “You upsold a tensioner to a man from Springfield who came in for a belt. You were right, he came back and said so, and he bought his brake work here instead of at the place on Second, which he told me, out loud, at that register. That’s the answer. You cost me fifty cents and made me four hundred, and I’d rather have you than the fifty cents. This is not a favor. If you ever say the word favor to me about this I’ll take it back.”

“I wasn’t going to say favor.”

“You were absolutely going to say favor. It was all over your face.”

Eli looked at the clipboard a second longer.

“Yes,” he said.

“That’s it?”

“That’s it. Yes. Put me on the June schedule.”

“Well, hell,” Gary said, mildly disappointed, “I had a whole other part prepared,” and went back to the register, and did not mention it again, and Eli stood there in aisle three under the dying fluorescent tube with his hands in his pockets and let himself have about ten seconds of something before he went back to the wiper blades.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Gary Prewitt had managed that store since 2006 and had worked in it since 1991, which meant he had been standing behind that counter when the road out front was still two lanes and the Kroger was a Big Bear and the whole stretch of Route 4 had four fewer traffic lights on it.

Eli had learned all of this in pieces over eighteen months, never in a conversation that was about it. Gary did not tell you things. Gary mentioned things while doing something else, which meant that in order to learn anything about the man you had to be present for a large number of hours doing inventory.

What Eli had assembled, over those hours:

Gary had been married. There was no ring and there was no mention, but there was a woman’s handwriting on a laminated card taped inside the cabinet under the register that listed the four suppliers’ after-hours numbers, and the card was old, and Gary had never replaced it with a printed one, and when a supplier changed a number in 2023 he had written the new one on a piece of masking tape and stuck it over the old one rather than redo the card.

Gary had a daughter somewhere. This Eli knew because a customer had asked after her once, by name, and Gary had said “she’s good” in a voice that closed a door politely.

Gary read. That one had been the surprise. There was a paperback in the cab of his truck at all times, face down on the dash, and they were not the books you would guess. Eli had seen a Cormac McCarthy in there and a book about the Dust Bowl and, on one memorable occasion, a very battered copy of something in Latin, which Gary had refused to explain and had then explained, forty minutes later, unprompted, over a shipment of oil filters: two years of seminary in his twenties, abandoned, and he still did the Latin because it was the only school thing he’d ever been good at.

“You were going to be a priest,” Eli had said.

“I was going to be a lot of things.”

“Gary.”

“Count the filters.”

That was as far as it had ever gone, and Eli understood the shape of it exactly: a man had told you a true thing about himself and the entire condition of him telling you was that you did not make him do it twice.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The store between six and eight on a Thursday got a particular kind of customer, and Eli had built a taxonomy for them.

There was the guy who knew what he needed and had the part number written on his hand. Four minutes, transaction complete, everybody happy.

There was the guy who did not know what he needed and was prepared to say so, who was the best customer in the world and who Eli would spend twenty minutes on cheerfully.

There was the guy who did not know what he needed and was not prepared to say so, who would describe a noise, and who required a specific delicacy, because the transaction could not proceed until he had been given a way to not know something in front of another man.

And there was the woman, usually somewhere between thirty and seventy, who came in braced for an argument because she had had that argument in an auto parts store before, sometimes many times, and who would put a part on the counter and say what she wanted in a voice with an edge already on it.

Eli had learned about eighteen months in that the most useful thing you could do with that last customer was nothing at all. No reassurance, no visible kindness, no signaling that he was one of the good ones. You just sold her the part, competently, at speed, the way you’d sell it to anyone, and answered what she asked and did not answer what she hadn’t, and by the third visit the edge was gone and by the fifth she asked for him by name.

He had four customers like that now. Gary had noticed and had never said a word about it, and had simply started routing them to Eli’s register.

That Thursday it was a woman called Yvette with a Corolla and a persistent squeal, and it took ten minutes, and it was a belt after all, and she paid and said “thanks, Eli,” and left.

Gary, from the next register, not looking up: “That’s the fourth one this week.”

“Is it.”

“Mm,” said Gary, and went back to the screen.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

There had been a Thursday earlier in the month.

He had wanted a Saturday. Reggie had the Saturdays, all of them, by seniority, and Gary had said no about that in October in six words and had not restated it since, and Eli had asked once in November and been told no again and had gone back to the wiper blades. The no was reasonable. That was not the problem.

A Saturday was ninety-one dollars.

He knew what asking straight would get him, because he had already done it, and he knew four other ways in, and he had them ranked by Tuesday.

They did inventory on Thursdays after eight, the two of them, in the back with the roll door down and the radio on the country station Gary refused to admit he liked. It was the only stretch of the week when Gary talked without a register in front of him. Everything Eli knew about the man he had learned in that room, in pieces, none of it on purpose.

The paperback on the dash that week was the Latin one.

He saw it on the way in and filed it on the way in, and by the third rack he knew he was going to use it.

“You’re doing the Latin again.”

“Count.”

“I’m counting.” He counted. “Forty-one. You said it was the only school thing you were ever any good at.”

Gary didn’t say anything.

That was the moment, and Eli was aware of it the way you are aware of a stair you have already put your weight on. There was a version where he said forty-one again and moved to the next rack and Gary went on not saying anything and the room stayed what it was.

“Was that the seminary or before it?”

And Gary told him.

It took about six minutes and it was more than the first time. There was a woman called Adele who had taught the class. There was a room in Dayton with radiators that banged. There was the fact that Gary had been twenty-three and had been good at exactly one thing in his life up to that point, and had walked away from it in March of 1994 for a reason he gave in one sentence and then stopped.

He stopped in the middle of a box. Eli watched him lose his place in a case of oil filters and start it again from the top, out loud, at forty-one, which was where they had been eight minutes ago.

Then Gary said, “You want one of Reggie’s Saturdays.”

“I’d take one.”

“Take the seventeenth,” Gary said, and wrote it on the clipboard, and did not look up, and they finished the filters with the radio on and neither of them said anything else for twenty minutes.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

He got in the Civic at ten past nine with a Saturday on the schedule, and sat in the lot with the key in and did not turn it, because the other thing had arrived and he wanted to look at it while it was still there.

It felt good.

That was the whole of it and there was no way to make it into anything else. A man had told him a true thing in November on the condition that nobody ever made him do it twice, and Eli had made him do it twice, and it had taken one question, and the question had been chosen on a Tuesday out of four.

He drove home doing arithmetic. Ninety-one dollars was two thirds of a set of tires he was going to need by June. It was correct. He did it twice on Route 4 and it came out the same both times and it did not do anything at all.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The coffee stopped for nine days.

Gary had been setting one down in front of him at six since about the second month, without a word, which was how Gary said things. The Thursday after, there was no coffee. There was none the Tuesday after that either, or the Thursday after that.

Nothing else changed. Gary gave him the Saturday and two more after it, routed the women who came in braced for an argument to his register the same as ever, and said nothing about any of it to anybody.

Eli counted the nine days and did not mention it and did not fix it, because there was no version of fixing it that was not the same equipment running again.

On the tenth day there was a coffee on the counter at six and Gary was at the far register with his back turned, and Eli drank it and did the wiper blades and got the shelf right.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

He came home at eight-forty. Deb was at the table with the checkbook and a stack of mail and the reading glasses she claimed were not reading glasses, and she looked up and said, “Anything happen?”

And he said, “A guy tried to return an alternator he hit with a hammer.”

“You told me that one already. That was two weeks ago.”

“It’s a different guy.”

“Two guys hit an alternator with a hammer?”

“It’s a very common repair strategy.”

She laughed and went back to the checkbook, and he got the plate out of the fridge, and the whole time there was a sentence sitting in his mouth, fully formed, which was *Gary put me on the June schedule at thirty-eight hours*, and it would have taken four seconds to say, and she would have been glad, that was the thing, she would have been so glad, and something in him closed a hand around the sentence and would not let it out.

He thought about why later, upstairs, lying on top of the covers with his shoes still on.

It wasn’t a secret. There was nothing in it to keep. In eight days she’d know anyway, because he’d have to give her the June dates for the calendar on the fridge, the calendar with the box on it.

It was that if he told her tonight she’d take it and she’d hold it and she’d turn it into a thing they had together, and it wasn’t a thing they had together, it was a thing he’d built by himself at a counter in a store on Route 4, and he needed one item in the entire structure of his life to be load-bearing without her name on it.

That was the honest version, and he knew it was the honest version, and he also knew it was a little bit cruel, and he lay there in the dark and held both of those at once and did not enjoy either.

Through the floor he could hear her moving around the kitchen. The tap. A drawer. The specific double-thump of the dishwasher door.

He counted the ceiling tiles, which was a stupid thing to count because there were twelve and there had always been twelve, and got to twelve, and started again.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Downstairs, Deb finished the checkbook and paid the water bill and put a stamp on it, and then sat for a minute with her hands flat on the table.

He’d been up there twenty minutes and hadn’t come back down for the second helping he always came back down for.

She thought about calling up to ask if he was all right, and didn’t, because he was seventeen and he was allowed to have a Thursday, and because the last time she’d gone up and knocked on a closed door about nothing he’d been perfectly polite about it in a way that had taken her three days to shake off.

So she wiped the counter instead. Put the mail in the recycling. Got the pool catalogue down off the microwave and read four pages of it standing up, the Bayside Deluxe with the slide, and then a smaller one called the Meadowbrook that had a filter she privately considered underpowered, and put it back.

The kitchen at nine at night with the overhead off and just the light above the stove was the best room in the house, and always had been, and she’d bought this house twelve years ago for reasons that had a lot more to do with this exact hour than with the square footage. Frank had wanted the one on Kettering with the two-car garage. She’d wanted a kitchen you could stand in at nine at night.

She’d gotten it. Nobody had told her at the time that you could get exactly the thing you wanted and still end up standing in it alone, listening for a floorboard upstairs, cataloguing whether the quality of the quiet was the ordinary kind or the other kind.

The floorboard went. Once, over by the closet.

She turned off the light above the stove and went to bed.


# Chapter 6

[T]{.dropcap}he call came at five-fifteen in the morning, the automated one, a woman’s recorded voice reading the closure list in the flat way of a machine that had never had a snow day itself, and Deb lay in the dark with the phone against her ear and heard *Deerbrook Falls City Schools, all buildings, closed* and felt eleven years old.

It never wore off. Twenty years of working in a school and the closure call still did the same thing to her that it had done in 1982.

She got up. Made coffee. Stood at the window over the sink in her robe and watched it come down, which it was doing seriously, no wind, big slow flakes stacking on the fence rail in a way that meant it had been going since about two. Six inches on the car already, maybe seven, and the plows hadn’t been down her street and wouldn’t be until nine at the earliest because hers was a residential and she’d learned where that fell in the priority order.

The whole day, unclaimed, sitting there.

Eli came down at ten past eight, which for him was late, in sweatpants and a hoodie with the hood actually up, which she had seen maybe five times in three and a half years.

“School’s closed,” she said.

“I know. Trevor texted me at five-thirty. He was hysterical.”

“Store?”

“Gary called at seven. He’s not opening till noon and he said if I show up before then he’ll send me home and dock me for the insult.” He got a mug. Considered the coffee. “So I’ve got nothing.”

“Me neither.”

They stood there in the kitchen, the two of them, with a whole free Wednesday, and something in the situation was almost funny, the way neither of them seemed to know the procedure.

“I’m not shoveling till it stops,” she said.

“It’s not going to stop till two.”

“Then I’m not shoveling till two.”

“Okay,” he said, and sat down, and that was it, that was the whole negotiation, and the day began.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

What they did with it, in order:

He fixed the drawer in the bathroom that had been sticking since November, which took forty minutes and a screwdriver and involved two trips to the basement, and Deb found herself following him down the second time for no reason at all and then having to invent an errand once she was there. She stood in front of the shelf under the stairs with a folded towel on a box of dishes and asked him if he needed the flashlight.

“There’s a light.”

“There’s a bad light.”

“It’s fine, Deb.”

She went back upstairs.

They ate breakfast at ten, the big kind, eggs and the good bacon and toast, and he ate an absurd amount of it, and she watched him do it with the specific satisfaction of a woman who had spent her entire working life feeding people who didn’t eat enough.

Breakfast took an hour because neither of them had anywhere to be, which on a weekday happened about once a winter.

She did the eggs the way she did them for herself, slower than she did them on a school morning, and Eli noticed and said so.

“These are different.”

“They’re the same eggs.”

“They’re not the same eggs. These are…” He considered them. “They’re wetter.”

“They’re not wetter, they’re not overcooked. You’ve been eating overcooked eggs for three and a half years because I make them at six in the morning while doing four other things.”

“So these are the good ones.”

“These are what eggs are,” Deb said. “You’ve been getting the ones I can make while packing a lunch.”

Eli ate for a minute.

“That’s sort of bleak,” he said.

“It’s a Wednesday morning, it’s not bleak. Nobody gets the good eggs on a Wednesday.”

“No, I mean.” He gestured with the fork. “There’s a version of this house where you’re doing everything at six in the morning while doing four other things, and there’s this one, and I’ve been in the first one for three and a half years and today’s the first time I’ve been in the second one.”

Deb got up to get the coffee, which she did not need, in order to have somewhere to put her face.

“That’s a snow day,” she said, from the counter. “That’s the entire point of a snow day. Everybody gets the good eggs.”

“How many have there been?”

“Snow days? Since you’ve been here?” She thought about it. “Four. Two in 2022, one last year, this one.”

“Four days in three and a half years,” Eli said, “where the eggs were right,” and Deb stood at her own counter with a coffee pot in her hand and had to wait a second before turning around.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Around eleven the power flickered twice and then held, and she said something about candles, and he said something about how she had a dozen flashlights and no batteries, which was true and which she disputed for ten minutes on principle.

At noon they watched forty minutes of a documentary about the construction of a bridge, which she put on as a joke and which he became absorbed in, to the point of objecting out loud to a claim about load distribution, and she looked over at him arguing with a television about a bridge and had to look away again.

At one-thirty he taught her a card game.

“What’s it called?”

“It doesn’t have a name.”

“Every game has a name.”

“This one doesn’t. Trevor’s cousin taught it to us. You have to get rid of your cards but the eights are wild only if somebody’s already played a red seven.”

“That’s not a rule. You just made that up.”

“That’s the rule.”

“Eli.”

“That’s the rule.”

She lost four hands in a row, at which point she became certain, and said so, that the rules were being adjusted between hands to ensure her defeat, and he denied this with a straight face and a level of formality that constituted a confession, and then she won the fifth hand and accused him of letting her, and he said, “I would never,” and she said, “You absolutely would,” and he said, “I would, but I didn’t,” and that was the closest thing to an argument they had all day.

At two-fifteen the power went out for real.

Not a flicker. The whole house dropped, the refrigerator’s hum cutting off so completely that the silence afterward had a shape to it, and both of them stopped mid-sentence and looked at the ceiling, as though the ceiling would know.

“Ha,” said Eli.

“Don’t.”

“I said a dozen flashlights and no batteries.”

“We have batteries.”

“Where are the batteries, Deb?”

They did not have batteries. They had, it emerged over the following twenty minutes, four flashlights in three locations, of which one worked; a box of tea lights from a Christmas she could not identify; and a headlamp belonging to Eli that he produced from his room without comment and that had a full charge, because of course it did.

“Why do you have a headlamp?”

“Because I work on cars.”

“You work on cars in daylight.”

“I work on cars whenever the car breaks,” Eli said, “which is a thing that has never happened at a convenient hour, and I would like to point out that I am currently the only person in this house providing light.”
