The power came back at three-oh-five, fifty minutes later, with a thump and a chorus of appliances resetting themselves, and by then they had eaten most of a bag of pretzels by headlamp at the kitchen table and Deb had told him about the ice storm of 2004, when the power had been out for four days and Frank had insisted on cooking a chicken on a charcoal grill in nine degrees, on principle, and had produced a chicken that was black on the outside and refrigerated in the middle and had eaten it anyway.

“He ate it?”

“He ate a piece of it. He got very quiet afterward and he never mentioned it again and neither did I.”

“That’s the whole story?”

“That’s the whole story. That’s most stories about Frank.”

Eli turned the headlamp off and set it on the table.

“You don’t talk about him.”

“There’s not much to say. He was funny. He was funny for about twelve years and then he was a man in a house who was somewhere else, and then he was actually somewhere else, and the second part took a lot less time than you’d think.” She stacked the pretzel bag into a fold. “That’s not a tragedy. It’s a very ordinary thing that happens in a very large number of houses, and I’ve never once been able to make it sound like anything when I’ve tried to tell it.”

“You just told it.”

“I told you the chicken.”

“Yeah,” Eli said. “I know.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

At three it stopped snowing and they shoveled.

Eight inches, and heavy, the wet kind, and they did the driveway together in the blue late-afternoon light with the whole street quiet the way it only is after a real snow, and did the walk, and then, without discussing it, went down and did Mrs. Petrosyan’s walk two doors down, because she was eighty-one and had a hip, and Eli did the steps and Deb did the flat and it took ten minutes with two people and would have taken Mrs. Petrosyan until Friday.

She came out in a housecoat and a man’s parka to tell them they shouldn’t have and to try to give Eli five dollars, and he refused it, and she got insistent about it in Armenian and English simultaneously, and eventually he took the five dollars because it was clearly the only way anybody was going back inside.

On the way home he handed it to Deb.

“What’s this for.”

“Household fund.”

“It’s your five dollars.”

“It’s the pool fund,” he said. “Bayside Deluxe. Four thousand gallons. We’re a hundred percent of the way to five dollars.”

She put the five under the pool catalogue on top of the microwave and it stayed there.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Walking back up the street with the shovels over their shoulders, the light going that particular blue it goes at four-forty in January when the sky is clear after a snow, Eli said, “Can I ask you something.”

“Go ahead.”

“How many were there? Before me.”

Deb shifted the shovel.

“Three.”

“I know it’s three. I mean how long.”

“Malik was seven months. He went to an aunt in Michigan, which was the right outcome and I’d do it again.” She watched her boots. “Then there was a girl, Alyssa, she was eleven, she was with me for ten months and then she went back to her mother, which was also the right outcome, and which was the single hardest thing I have ever done in my life, and I would also do it again.”

“And the third?”

“Jaylen. Eight months. He’s twenty-two now, he’s in Cleveland, he texts me on my birthday.”

“Every year?”

“Every year since he left. Nine words, roughly. Same nine words.” She smiled at the road. “Last year he added an exclamation point and I thought about it for a week.”

Eli walked.

“Do you know where all of them are?”

“I know where two of them are. I don’t know where Alyssa is.” Deb’s breath went up in front of her. “Her mother stopped answering after about eight months, which she was entitled to do, and I have made a decision not to go looking, which I re-make about twice a year, and I’ve never once been sure it’s the right one.”

They got to the driveway. The house was dark, because they’d left in daylight and neither of them had thought about it.

“For what it’s worth,” Eli said, “that’s the first time you’ve told me any of that.”

“You never asked.”

“That’s not why.”

Deb stopped with her hand on the storm door.

“No,” she said. “It’s not why.”

She thought about it standing there in the cold with a shovel.

“I don’t tell you about them,” she said, “because when you’re in a house like this, there’s a thing where you can start feeling like one of a series. Like there’s a chair and people take turns in it. And I decided a long time ago that whatever else I got wrong, no kid was going to sit at my table and hear about the last one.”

Eli looked at the door frame.

“Yeah,” he said. “That was the right call.”

“Was it?”

“Yeah.” He took the shovel off her and stood both of them against the wall of the house, blades down, where they went. “But you can tell me now. If you want. I’m not in the chair anymore, or I’m about to not be, and I’d rather know that there’s a guy in Cleveland who texts you nine words.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

At five-fifteen, with the light almost gone, Eli said, “You want to see something stupid?”

He had the laptop open on the kitchen table and turned it around, and it was a satellite photo of the street, the whole block from above, gray and dead in whatever month the imagery was from.

“That’s our roof.”

“That’s our roof, and that’s Petrosyan’s roof, and that’s the Klausners’, and that’s the empty lot.” He zoomed. “Now watch.”

He clicked to a different year, and the image slid, and the empty lot had a house on it.

“When’s that?”

“2007.”

“There was a house on that lot in 2007?”

“There was a house on that lot until 2011,” Eli said. “I looked it up in about September. There’s a whole thing, it’s on the auditor’s site, every parcel’s got a history and it’s all public. That house was built in 1948 and torn down in 2011 and the lot’s been empty for fourteen years and it’s owned by somebody in Florida who pays four hundred dollars a year in taxes on it and has never done anything with it.”

Deb pulled a chair around.

“How long have you known this?”

“About four months.”

“You’ve been sitting on a demolished house.”

“I’ve been sitting on a lot of things, Deb, you’ve never asked me about any of them.” He clicked back. “Your house is 1952. Petrosyan’s is 1949. The whole street went up between ’48 and ’54 and it was all one builder, which is why every single one of these houses has the same bad cabinet hinges.”

“That’s why the hinges are bad?”

“That’s why the hinges are bad. It’s not you. It’s a guy in 1952.”

“Everything’s a guy,” Deb said.

“Everything’s a guy,” Eli agreed, and they spent forty minutes on the auditor’s website looking at the ownership history of every house on that street, and found that the Klausners’ place had been sold four times in six years in the 2010s and that Mrs. Petrosyan and her husband had bought theirs in 1971 for nineteen thousand dollars, and that Deb had paid ninety-four for hers in 2014, and that the whole street’s worth of human sorting and arriving and leaving was sitting there in a public database in a font nobody had chosen.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

They had soup at six because there was nothing else and neither of them wanted to go out, and the kitchen windows had that black-mirror thing they do at night in winter, the room doubled and floating out there in the yard.

He said, out of nowhere, “You ever think about moving?”

“Out of the house?”

“Yeah.”

“Not seriously.” She turned her spoon over. “I thought about it in the divorce, because everybody tells you to. Fresh start, all that. And then I stood in this kitchen at nine o’clock at night and thought, no, I like this room, and that was the end of the thinking.”

“That’s a whole house for one room.”

“It’s a good room.”

He looked around at it, actually looked, the ceiling and the bad cabinet hinges and the window over the sink.

“It’s a good room,” he agreed.

Then he took both bowls to the sink without being asked and started the water, and she got up to dry, and the day ended the way it had gone all day, which was easily.

She thought about it that night, lying in bed with the house ticking as it cooled.

There was nothing in the day. That was what she kept coming back to. Nothing had happened. A drawer got fixed. A woman got her walk shoveled. A card game with invented rules and a documentary about a bridge, and eight inches of snow, and fourteen dollars’ worth of bacon.

Seventy-two days.

She had a horrible clear thought, then, lying there, which was that in some number of years this would be the day she’d want back, not any of the big ones, not the day he got his license or the graduation that was coming in June, but a Wednesday in January when the schools were closed and nobody had anywhere to be. And that she’d known it while it was happening, which she was not sure was a mercy.

She rolled over and told herself to go to sleep, and did, eventually, and down the hall Eli lay awake for a while doing the ceiling tiles again, twelve and twelve and twelve, until they stopped meaning anything and he went under.


# Chapter 7

[T]{.dropcap}he last week of January had nine inches of snow in it and a two-hour delay on the Thursday, and the whole month ended in the kind of gray that makes a person in southwest Ohio uncertain whether the sun is a thing that happened or a thing she was told about.

Deb ran four hundred and twelve trays through all of it.

That is the fact about that winter that would be missing from any account of it, including her own: that underneath the calendar and the folder and the boy at the counter on Route 4, a woman got up at five-forty every weekday and fed four hundred and twelve children, and did it well, and did it on a budget last adjusted in 2017, and that this occupied roughly sixty percent of her waking life and appears in none of the important conversations.

Eli sold parts. That occupied twenty-four hours of his week and he was good at it and it is likewise missing.

The pancake supper got planned. Two staff meetings, one of which ran long over a dispute about the syrup station that had been running since 2019.

The compressor on the walk-in was inspected and found to have four years left in it, which it had also had in 2019.

That was January.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

February came in warm and dishonest. Two days in the fifties, the whole town out without coats like it had been let out of something, water running down every gutter and the snow going gray and porous along the curbs, and Marisol declared spring in the cafeteria at seven in the morning and was corrected by four separate people.

“It’s February second.”

“I don’t accept that.”

“The groundhog’s today.”

“I don’t accept the groundhog either. I have never accepted the groundhog.” She was stress-eating croutons out of the bag, which she did approximately once a week and denied every time. “It’s fifty-four degrees. I have a right to feelings.”

“You’re eating croutons.”

“These are for the salad bar.”

“They’re in your hand.”

“They’re on their way to the salad bar,” Marisol said, with dignity, and ate another one.

Deb ran the line. Four hundred and twelve trays. A sixth grader informed her that the chicken had a weird shape today and Deb informed him that all chicken has a weird shape if you think about it too long, which ruined his afternoon and possibly his month, and Marisol laughed so hard she had to go stand by the freezer.

Renee called at one-forty, on Deb’s break, when she was sitting in the Escape in the lot with the windows down because it was fifty-four degrees in February and that was worth something.

“Is this a bad time.”

“I’m sitting in a parking lot eating a sandwich I made at six in the morning. It’s the best time I’ve got.”

“I need ten minutes and I’d rather do it on the phone than at the house, because if I do it at the house he’s in the room and I don’t want him in the room for the procedural part. He’ll get the procedural part from me directly at the visit next week. This part’s yours.”

Deb put the sandwich down.

“Go ahead.”

“April third, Magistrate Meehan. Here’s what actually happens in that room, so nothing surprises you.” Renee’s voice went into the flatter register she used for facts, and Deb was grateful for it. “I’ll present the agency’s report. It’ll say Eli’s placement has been stable for three and a half years, which it will say in about nine words, because that’s the format, and I want you to hear me say that the nine words are not what it was. Then the guardian ad litem speaks. Then Meehan asks Eli directly whether he consents to the filing of an adoption petition. She has to. He’s seventeen, and in this state a child over twelve has to consent to their own adoption. There’s a line in the statute that lets a court go around it if the court decides that’s what’s best for the child, and I want you to hear me say that no magistrate in that county has ever once used it on a seventeen-year-old and Meehan is not going to be the first. It’s his answer, Deb. That’s the whole of it.”

“I know.”

“I know you know. I’m saying it because it’s the difference between knowing it and having heard it said out loud in a car.” A pause. “She’ll also ask him about Bridges. The voluntary agreement, extended care to twenty-one. That one’s a signature, and it’s his signature, and he can sign it that day in that room, or in June, or in September, or the week before he turns twenty-one. That door doesn’t shut on April third. I want you to hold on to that, because a lot of people in your position hear the hearing date and think of it as a wall.”

“Is it a wall?”

“No,” Renee said. “It’s a Friday with paperwork. The wall’s April nineteenth, and that one’s got a handle on it too.”

Deb watched a kid in the far corner of the lot trying to skateboard through a puddle, failing, doing it again.

“He’ll say no to both.”

“Probably.”

“He’ll say it politely.”

“He will say it,” Renee said, “so politely that everybody in that room will feel fine about it for about four hours, and then two of us will be up at eleven at night.”

Deb laughed. It was not a good laugh but it was real.

“Renee.”

“Yeah.”

“Nine words.”

“I’ll make it eleven,” Renee said. “I’ll do what I can. The report’s a form, Deb. It’s got boxes. There’s no box for a kid who fixes a caseworker’s van with a bottle of pop and won’t take five dollars off an old woman.” Something shifted in her voice, briefly, and then went back. “That’s what the hearing is. It’s a form. The rest of it happened in your kitchen for three and a half years and no magistrate’s ever going to see it, and I’ve been doing this twenty-two years and that’s still the part I can’t get comfortable with.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She sat in the car for a while after Renee rang off.

Fifty-four degrees on the second of February, and the whole lot dripping, and the kids’ voices coming faint from the field where somebody had let a class out early because of the weather. She had a few minutes of break left and half a sandwich she was not going to eat.

*It’s a Friday with paperwork.*

She turned that over. It was, she recognized, the most useful sentence anybody had said to her since Christmas, and it was also completely inadequate, and both of those were true at once.

Because it was a Friday with paperwork. That was factually correct. Nothing would happen in courtroom 4B on the third of April that had not already happened; the hearing would ratify a set of facts that were already true, and would create nothing, and would take under half an hour.

And she had drawn a red box around it in December.

She thought about why, sitting in the car.

She decided, eventually, that it was because it was the only date in the whole business that had a room attached to it. Everything else was diffuse. He would turn eighteen in a bedroom or a parking lot or standing at a counter selling somebody a serpentine belt, and it would happen the way birthdays happen, which is not at any particular moment. But the third of April had a room, and a time, and a woman in a robe, and if you were a person who needed something to point at, that was the only thing on the calendar shaped like an event.

*You want a wall,* she thought, *and there isn’t one, so you’ve made a Friday do it.*

She ate the other half of the sandwich after all, and went back in, and did the afternoon.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Deb told Marisol at four, in the walk-in, which was where they told each other things because the walk-in had a door that closed and nobody followed you into a walk-in.

“So she has to ask him.”

“In front of everybody.”

“And he says no.”

“He says no.”

Marisol leaned against the shelving with a case of shredded cheese against her hip and thought about it, and did not say any of the four things Deb was braced for. No *maybe he’ll surprise you.* No *you never know.* No *he loves you though.*

What she said was, “What are you going to do with your face.”

“What?”

“In the room. When he says it.” Marisol shifted the cheese. “Because he’s going to look at you. Not on purpose, maybe, but he will, right after. They always do. And whatever’s on your face is what he takes out of that building and keeps.”

Deb stood in the cold with her arms crossed.

“That’s a horrible thing to say to a person.”

“It’s the only useful thing I’ve got and I’ve been thinking about it since January.” Marisol put the cheese down. “My mother did the face wrong once. Not about anything this big. About my brother and the Navy, actually, same thing, different year. He told her at the table and she did about a half-second of something before she got hold of it, and he saw the half second, and he was forty-one years old before he told me he’d seen it. Forty-one. He carried a half second for twenty-three years.”

“So what do I do.”

“You practice,” Marisol said. “That’s my whole advice. It’s not noble. You practice in the car like a lunatic until the face is ready, so when it happens he gets the one you chose and not the one that happens to you.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

“There’s a thing I want to ask you,” Deb said, before Marisol could get out of the walk-in, “and I want a real answer.”

“Those are my worst ones.”

“Do you think I’m doing this for him or for me?”

Marisol stopped with her hand on the door.

“Which this?”

“All of it. The hearing. Going. The thermos. Sitting in the back of a room where I’m not a party to anything and have no standing and nothing I do will make one particle of difference to the outcome.” Deb had her arms folded. “Because I’ve been telling myself it’s so he’s not in there with four professionals and nobody who’s eaten dinner with him. And I’ve been telling myself that for three weeks and I’ve started to hear how much I like saying it.”

Marisol let go of the door.

“You want the real answer.”

“I asked for it.”

“It’s both, and the proportions don’t matter.” She said it without any particular gentleness. “Half of it’s for him and half of it’s so you can be the woman who went, and if you sit in that cooler trying to separate them you’ll be in here till June.”

“That’s not helpful.”

“It’s extremely helpful, you just don’t like it.” Marisol got the door open. “Deb, every good thing anybody’s ever done for me was about eighty percent for them. My sister sat with me for four days after Ray and half of it was that she couldn’t stand being in her own house. It didn’t come out of my end any different.”

She held the door.

“Go to the hearing,” she said. “Take the thermos. Do it for whatever mix of reasons you’ve got, because the mix is not available for inspection and never will be, and the boy gets a woman in the back row either way.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She practiced in the car.

Not that afternoon. That afternoon she thought about it and felt ridiculous. But the next day, in the school lot at seven in the morning with the engine running and the heat on her hands, she looked at herself in the rearview mirror and said, out loud, in an empty car, “That’s all right.”

It came out wrong. It came out like a woman being brave in a movie about a woman being brave.

She tried it again. Worse.

She sat back and looked at the crack in the windshield, which ran from the lower left up toward the middle and had been there since a truck threw a stone at her on Route 4 in October of 2018, and which she kept meaning to get fixed, and which had gotten about an inch longer since Christmas the way they do.

“Okay,” she said to the empty car. “Okay.”

And then she went in and did four hundred and twelve trays, and made a joke to Marisol about the groundhog, and did not think about it again until nine that night, standing in the kitchen with the light on over the stove, when she found she could say it without her voice doing anything at all.

“That’s all right.”

It sounded like nothing. It sounded like a woman saying a normal sentence in a kitchen.

She said it twice more, to the window and the black yard beyond it, and put the pan away, and went up to bed.


# Chapter 8

[T]{.dropcap}he house did a thing in early February. Every year. The snow went porous and the light came back a little and there was a Tuesday around the third or fourth where you could stand in the driveway at five in the afternoon and it was not dark, and every year that day arrived and did something to her that was not happiness and was not grief.

Anticipation, maybe, except of nothing.

She had felt it in 2018 when she was alone in the house and it had nearly finished her, that specific February afternoon light with nobody in the building. She had felt it in 2022 with Jaylen upstairs and it had been fine. She felt it now with a court date on the calendar and a boy at a parts counter on Route 4, and she stood in the driveway with the recycling bin at ten past five on the third of February and thought: *fifty-nine days.*

Then she took the bin out and went in and started dinner.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The envelope came the Tuesday after that, and it was not dramatic, which was somehow the worst thing about it.

It came in the same bundle as a utility bill, a flyer for a roofing company, a card from Deb’s sister in Toledo who sent cards for occasions that did not exist, and the pool catalogue’s monthly successor, addressed again to Mr. R. Halstead, who Deb had by now developed a full imaginary relationship with.

Eli got the mail. He always got the mail; it was on the way in from the driveway and he’d been doing it since the first month.

He stood at the counter and went through it, and the envelope was fourth from the top, and it had the county seal in the corner and a case number in the window and his own name printed in a font that had been chosen by nobody.

He knew what it was before he opened it. He opened it anyway, standing up, with his coat still on.

It was one page. It said that a permanency review hearing had been scheduled in the matter of a case number, before Magistrate P. Meehan, on April 3 at 9:40 a.m., in courtroom 4B, and that his attendance was required, and that he had the right to be heard by the court, and that he had the right to counsel, and it listed a phone number for the guardian ad litem’s office, and it was signed with a stamp.

Nine forty in the morning. Renee had said that in this kitchen in January and he had filed it and here it was again with a case number on it, and the case number was the thing, actually, the case number was the part that did it, because he had been a case number since he was twelve and he had made a kind of peace with that, and the peace was that nobody printed it and mailed it to the house where he ate dinner.

He read it twice.

Then he did a thing he had not decided to do, which was that he did not put it in the drawer with the school forms and the insurance papers, where it belonged, where he put everything.

He put it on the counter. Face up. Squared to the edge, because his hands did that automatically, but face up, where she would see it the second she came through the door.

Then he went to his shift.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

There were two hours between him leaving the house and her coming home to it, and she spent them at work without knowing anything at all.

That was the part she would go over afterward. That the envelope had been sitting on the counter since three-forty in the afternoon, and she had been at work until five-fifteen, and for one hour and thirty-five minutes there had been a piece of paper in her kitchen with a case number on it and she had been in a school cafeteria arguing with a delivery driver about a pallet of frozen corn.

The delivery driver’s name was Wes and he had been bringing that pallet on Tuesdays for six years and he was, on this particular Tuesday, wrong about something.

“It says eight cases.”

“It says eight cases on your sheet. There’s six on my truck.”

“Then there’s two cases somewhere between your truck and this door, Wes.”

“There’s no two cases, Deb.”

“There are always two cases.”

There were, in the end, two cases. They were behind a stack of paper goods on the truck where they had been since Springfield, and Wes found them and said nothing about it and Deb said nothing about it either, because they had been doing this on Tuesdays for six years and the not-saying was the entire relationship.

Marisol, watching from the doorway with a clipboard: “You enjoyed that.”

“I did not enjoy that.”

“You enjoyed that more than anything else that’s happened to you this month.”

Which was, Deb thought later, standing in her kitchen with a county seal in her hand, probably true.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Deb saw it at five-fifty.

She’d come in with grocery bags on both arms and set them on the counter and the envelope was right there next to the bread, and she read it standing up with her coat still on and a bag of frozen peas going soft against her hip.

She read it three times.

Then she put the peas away and the milk away and the rest of it away, in order, the way you do, and folded the bags and put them in the drawer with the other bags, and washed her hands, and stood at the sink for a while.

Face up.

He’d left it face up. That was not nothing. Eli Vance had never in three and a half years left a piece of paper anywhere but exactly where it went, and this one was on the counter, squared to the edge, turned toward the door.

She was fifty-four and she’d been doing this long enough to know the difference between a boy who doesn’t want to talk about something and a boy who has put something where you’ll trip over it. The second kind is asking. He’d have died before asking out loud, and he’d left it face up on the counter next to the bread, and those were the same act performed by different people.

So she left it there.

That was the decision she made, standing at the sink with the water running over her hands. She didn’t file it. She didn’t tuck it into the drawer to spare him seeing it again. She left the envelope on the counter where he’d put it and got out the pan and started dinner.

He came in at eight-thirty. She heard the door, the coat, the boots.

He saw it. She watched him see it, from the stove, without turning her head.

He stopped for about a second and a half. Then he came the rest of the way in and got a glass and filled it from the tap.

“You saw it,” he said.

“I saw it.”

“Okay.”

She turned the heat down under the pan. “Nine forty’s a bad time for a hearing.”

“Why.”

“Because it’s not early enough to get it over with and it’s not late enough to do anything with the morning first.” She stirred. “You’ll sit in that hallway from nine-oh-five like everybody else. There’s no coffee in that building that’s worth anything. I’ll bring a thermos.”

He didn’t say anything for a second.

“You don’t have to come.”

“I know I don’t have to come.”

“It’s not, like, a thing where they need you there.”

“Eli.” She turned around then. “I’m coming to the hearing. That’s not a negotiation and it’s not a favor and it’s not me making a point about anything. You’ll be in a room with a magistrate and a caseworker and a guardian ad litem, and every one of them is a professional doing a job, and every one of them is good at it, and none of them has eaten dinner at this table. So I’m going to be in the back with a thermos. That’s all that is.”

He looked at her for a second longer than the sentence needed.

“Okay,” he said.

“Okay.”

“The coffee in that building is genuinely bad,” he added. “I’ve been in there four times. It’s got a taste.”

“It’s the machine. They’ve had that machine since the nineties. Marisol’s cousin worked in that building, it’s a whole thing, there’s a petition.”

“There’s a petition about the coffee machine.”

“There’s been a petition about the coffee machine since 2011,” Deb said. “It has nineteen signatures. Her cousin’s on it twice.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

“Can I ask you what happens in there,” Deb said, at the sink. “In the room. I’ve never been in one.”

“You’ve never been to a hearing?”

“Not one. Six years, four kids, and every hearing I’ve ever been connected to happened without me in it, because a foster parent’s not a party. You get a notice if you’re lucky and Renee tells you the outcome by phone.” She rinsed the pan. “I’ve been sent about a dozen pieces of paper about children who lived in my house, after the fact.”

Eli was quiet for a second.

“That’s insane,” he said.

“That’s the design. And there’s a reason for it, and the reason is sound, because the whole point is that the child’s not mine and a court shouldn’t be making decisions with a foster parent in the room applying pressure.” She set the pan in the rack. “I’ve thought about it a lot. I think it’s correct. It’s correct and it’s a very strange thing to be on the end of, both.”

“Okay.” He turned the envelope over on the table. “It’s a small room. It’s smaller than you’d think, it’s about the size of this kitchen and a half. There’s chairs at the back, like eleven of them. There’s a table for the agency and a table for the lawyers and the magistrate’s up front but not, like, up on a thing. It’s not a movie courtroom. Nobody’s in robes except her.”

“She’s in a robe?”

“She’s in a robe. That’s the one movie part.”

“And it’s fast?”

“It’s incredibly fast,” Eli said. “That’s what nobody tells you. The whole thing that reorganizes your entire life takes about nineteen minutes and there’s a woman typing and somebody’s got a folder and then they say the next case number and you’re in the hallway.”

Deb dried her hands.

“Nineteen minutes.”

“Give or take.”

“For three and a half years.”

“Yeah,” Eli said. “Well. They’re not weighing the three and a half years. They’re just writing down where I live.”


::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Later, after, he sat at the table with the letter and read it a fourth time, and Deb, drying a pot, watched him do it out of the corner of her eye and did not offer anything.

“They have to ask me,” he said. “At the hearing. That’s what this line’s about, where it says the right to be heard.”

“Renee said.”

“She asks me in front of everybody.”

“That’s how it works.”

“Yeah.” He set the page down. Squared it to the table edge, thumb and finger. “It’s a good law.”

“It is a good law.”

“I’m not being sarcastic. It’s a good law. When I was twelve nobody asked me anything, and now a woman in a robe has to look at me and ask, and that’s better. That’s actually better. It’s just.” He stopped. Started again. “You know how you can want a thing to be true and also not want to be in the room where it’s true?”

Deb put the pot down.

“Yes,” she said.

He nodded, once, like that settled something.

Then he got up and put the letter in the drawer with the school forms and the insurance papers, where it went, and closed the drawer, and said, “There’s a pool catalogue on the counter with a slide on the cover and I want to talk about it,” and Deb followed him into it.


# Chapter 9

[R]{.dropcap}enee came Thursday and did the procedural part in the kitchen with both of them at the table, and it took ten minutes, and Eli listened to all of it with his hands flat and asked two questions, both of them logistical, one about the guardian ad litem and one about whether he needed to bring anything with him.

“Just yourself. And an ID if you’ve got one.”

“I’ve got one.”

“Then just yourself.”

He nodded. Renee closed the folder. Nobody said the other thing.

At the door she said, “Battery’s still holding,” and he said, “It won’t past March,” and she said, “You told me three weeks in January and it’s February,” and he said, “I said two or three weeks. It’s been five. That’s not a win for the battery, that’s a win for the weather,” and Renee said something under her breath about teenagers and got in the van and it started on the first try, which Eli called luck and she called vindication.

Then she was gone, and it was five-forty on a Thursday, and he had a shift at six.

“You want the plate now or after,” Deb said.

“After.”

“It’s the pork thing.”

“Then after, obviously. If I eat it now I’ll be asleep by seven.”

He got his keys. And at the door, with his hand already on it, he said, “I’m taking Reggie’s Saturday, by the way. He’s got a wedding.”

“That’s a double.”

“It’s eight to eight.”

“That’s a double, Eli.”

“It’s a Saturday,” he said. “Gary’s paying time and a half after six. It’s a hundred and forty bucks I don’t currently have.”

Which was true, and which was checkable, and which was the kind of reason that ended a conversation, and Deb heard herself say “Okay” and heard the door do its stick-and-give, and stood in the kitchen with the pork thing in the oven and a whole new arithmetic starting up somewhere behind her sternum.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

He took the Saturday. Then the Sunday after it, four to eight, because Trevor’s shift got moved. Then a Wednesday that wasn’t his.

By the third week of February, Eli Vance was working every hour AutoWorks would legally give a seventeen-year-old, and every single one of those hours had a reason attached to it, and every reason was good.

The money was real. That was the part Deb kept snagging on. If he’d been lying she’d have known what to do. She’d had a kid in 2021 who lied about where he was going with a fluency that had frightened her, and she’d handled it, and it had been almost restful compared to this, because a lie is a thing you can name.

This wasn’t a lie. He was at work. He was at work an enormous amount, and he came home smelling like cardboard and cold rubber, and he ate the plate she left in the fridge, and he did his homework at eleven at night at the kitchen table with a glass of milk, and his grades did not slip, not one point, because letting his grades slip would have given her something to say.

He was leaving in increments. He’d worked out the exact procedure for leaving a house four months before you leave it, which was to be in it less every week by an amount too small to argue with.

She counted it once, on a Sunday, sitting at the table with the calendar. Two weeks in January, they’d had eleven dinners together. The last two weeks of February: four.

She looked at that number for a while and then closed the calendar and did not do the count again, because she recognized what she was doing, which was building a case, and she had no court to bring it to and no charge to bring.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The store’s schedule went up on a Sunday for the following week and Eli photographed it every time and sent it to nobody.

He had begun, in the second week of February, to do a thing he was not proud of, which was to look at that schedule before he agreed to anything.

Reggie wanted a Saturday swapped: he checked the schedule, saw a Tuesday he could give up instead, and offered the Tuesday. Gary asked if anybody could do a Sunday: he took it, and it cost him nothing, because Sunday was already a day when the house was full of a woman doing things in a kitchen and he would have had to be in it.

He was aware of the pattern by about the twentieth of February and did not stop.

What he told himself, and what was true, was that every extra hour was twelve dollars and forty cents and that twelve dollars and forty cents was a real number in a budget with a slack line in it.

What was also true, and which arrived at about two in the morning on the twenty-second of February and would not go back in its box, was that a boy who had spent three and a half years learning exactly how to be in a house was now using the same equipment to work out how to be in it less, and that he had gotten so good at the first thing that the second one was almost effortless.

He lay in the dark and thought: *she’ll notice inside of three weeks.*

And then, worse: *and she won’t say anything, because she never says anything, and I know that, and I am using it.*

He took a Wednesday shift the next day that he did not need.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

There was a Thursday in there.

He’d been on the schedule and then he wasn’t, some mix-up with Reggie, and he came home at five-thirty on a night she had not expected him and found her at the table with the school’s food order spreadsheet open and a calculator.

“You’re home.”

“Reggie double-booked himself.”

“There’s nothing made. I was going to have toast.”

“You were going to have toast for dinner.”

“I was going to have toast for dinner,” Deb said, “and I want you to understand that I was looking forward to it.”

So they had toast for dinner. That was the entire meal: a loaf of bread, the toaster, butter, the jar of the good jam Marisol had brought in November, and two people at a table at six o’clock eating toast and doing paperwork, and it was, Deb would think in April, possibly the best evening of that entire month.

He did his calculus. She did the food order. Every so often one of them said something.

“What’s a hotel pan.”

“It’s a pan.”

“That’s not an answer.”

“It’s a metal pan that’s a specific size. Everything in a commercial kitchen is that size or half that size or a third of that size. That’s the whole system.”

“Why?”

“Because in about 1900 somebody decided, and now it’s fixed forever and nobody can change it.” Deb turned a page. “Your entire industry’s the same. Why is a socket set the sizes it is?”

“Because a guy in 1908 picked them.”

“There you go.”

“That’s actually not why, it’s more about…” He stopped. “Okay, it’s mostly because a guy in 1908 picked them.”

“Everything’s a guy in 1908,” Deb said. “Everything you’ll ever do at a job is a guy in 1908 and nine people since who didn’t want the argument.”

Eli laughed with his mouth full.

At eight-fifteen he said, “I’m going to do the rest of this upstairs,” and went up, and she heard the fourth stair, and she sat in the kitchen with a spreadsheet and thought: that was two and a half hours and neither of us did anything.

She wrote *toast* in the corner of the food order sheet, for no reason, and then scribbled it out, and the sheet went to the district office on Monday with a scribble in the corner.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The Saturday of the double, she brought him lunch.

She thought about it all morning and knew the entire time that it was a bad idea and did it anyway. Two sandwiches, a thermos of the good soup, the little bag of the cookies from the place on Fourth. She told herself it was normal. Parents brought their kids lunch. That was a thing that happened in the world.

She got there at twelve-fifteen. The store was busy, four people at the counter, a guy at the far register arguing about a core charge, and Eli was in the middle of it with a headset on and a computer screen full of part numbers, and he looked up and saw her and something crossed his face that she would take home with her and look at for three days.

It wasn’t anger. She could have worked with anger.

It was the look of a man at his job whose mother has come to his job.

He finished with the customer. Came around the end of the counter, wiping his hands on the rag from his pocket.

“Hey.”

“I brought lunch. You’re on a double, and I know what you eat on a double, which is a bag of chips and a Mountain Dew, and I’ve got soup.”

“Deb.”

“It’s soup.”

“I’ve got twenty minutes at two. There’s a break room but it’s got a microwave that doesn’t work and Gary’s dog in it.”

“Gary has a dog in the break room?”

“Gary has a dog in the break room,” Eli said, “and the dog is not friendly, and I’ve been meaning to bring that up with someone.”

She laughed. It came out of her sideways and too grateful, and she heard it happen, and so did he.

He took the bag. Held it a second.

“Thank you,” he said, and he meant it, and that was the worst part, because he meant it and it still cost him something to accept it in front of the counter with four people waiting, and she watched him decide not to say the second thing, whatever the second thing was, and go put the bag under the counter.

She left. She sat in the Escape in the AutoWorks lot for a minute before starting it, looking at the crack in the windshield.

Then she drove to the Kroger and bought things she didn’t need, because she was already out and going home to that house at twelve-forty on a Saturday with nine hours of it in front of her was not something she was prepared to do yet.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She counted the Saturdays instead of the dinners, for one week, as a change of method.

One Saturday left in February. Then four in March. Five Saturdays, and he was working four of them, which she knew because the schedule was on the fridge next to the calendar, posted there by him, unprompted, in the second week of January, which had been a kindness and was now a document she read the way other people read a barometer.

She caught herself standing in front of the fridge at ten past six on a Wednesday reading a work schedule with a pen in her hand and no intention of writing anything, and she made herself walk away from it, and she came back forty minutes later.

That was the week she started sleeping badly.

Not dramatically. She fell asleep fine and woke at three-forty, most nights, and lay there for between forty minutes and two hours with a mind that was not thinking about anything in particular, just running, the way a fridge runs.

At four-fifteen one morning she got up and went down and stood in the kitchen in the dark, and the light over the stove was off, and the plate was in the fridge with foil on it because he’d closed and eaten at the store, and she stood there and looked at the closed refrigerator door for a while, and then went back up.

She told nobody about the three-forties. Not Marisol, who would have had something to say, and not the doctor she saw in March for a completely unrelated thing and who asked, in the standard way, how she was sleeping, and to whom Deb Hale, fifty-four, said, “Fine.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

He came home at eight-forty. He’d eaten the soup. He said so first thing, without being asked, which she recognized as a kindness and which landed like one.

“It was good. Trevor tried to get some and I told him to get his own mother.”

The word went across the kitchen and both of them heard it and neither of them touched it.

“Anyway,” Eli said, after about a second and a half. “It was good.”

“There’s more.”

“I know. There’s always more.”

He went up. She stood there.

He had done that once before, about a year ago, in a sentence about a school form, the same slip, and both times he had recovered in under two seconds and neither time had she said anything, because what was she going to say, and because the one thing she was certain of in the entire situation was that if she ever once made a thing of it he would never do it again.

She got the calendar down off the fridge, looked at it, and put it back without counting anything.

Forty-one days.


# Chapter 10

[T]{.dropcap}he kid’s name was Dominic Reyes and he was in sixth grade and he was, by a considerable margin, the most annoying human being who came through Deb Hale’s lunch line.

This was not a private assessment. It was the settled opinion of the entire kitchen staff, arrived at independently and confirmed at a staff meeting in October. Dominic Reyes asked for extra rice every single day. He asked for it in a specific tone. He had, over the course of one school year, developed nine distinct strategies for obtaining extra rice, including a period in November when he had attempted to convince Deb that he had a note.

“You don’t have a note.”

“I have a note at home.”

“That’s not where notes go, Dominic.”

She loved him. That was the thing nobody in the kitchen said out loud. He was relentless and shameless and he was the only kid in four hundred and twelve who said thank you every day, every single day, on his way past with his stolen rice, and he had a laugh that could be heard from the far end of the gym.

On the last Tuesday in February he came through the line and asked for extra rice, and Deb said, “No.”

Which was normal. She said no most days. The no was part of it.

“Come on.”

“No.”

“Just a little.”

“Dominic.”

“You gave Trey extra.”

“Trey is a foot taller than you and plays a sport.”

“That’s discrimination based on sports.”

“Move along.”

“Ms. Hale.”

And she said, sharp, loud enough that the two kids behind him heard it and the aide by the door turned around, “I said *no*. It’s the same answer every day, it’s been the same answer since September, and I am not doing this with you today.”

The line went quiet in that specific way.

Dominic’s face did something fast. Twelve years old, and he got hold of it almost immediately, which was its own small awful thing to witness, and he said “okay” in a completely different voice and took his tray and went.

Deb served the next kid. And the next one. Her hands did it. She did about forty more trays with the ladle going up and down and something rising in her chest that had nothing to do with rice.

Marisol came down the line and took the ladle out of her hand without a word and said, at normal volume, like it was nothing, “Go check the walk-in, I think the cooler’s off again.”

The cooler was not off. Deb stood in it for four minutes with her hands on a shelf.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She went and stood in the walk-in for the four minutes and did not think about Eli at all.

What she thought about was a boy called Marcus Dwyer who had been in the sixth grade in 2009 and whose mother had died in March of that year, and who had come through her line every day for the twelve weeks afterward, and to whom she had never said a single word about it, because she had been thirty-seven and had had no idea what to say and had decided that saying nothing was safer.

She had given him extra everything for twelve weeks. That had been her entire contribution. Extra rice, extra roll, the good milk, and never a word, and he had never said a word either, and in June he had gone to the high school and she had not seen him again until 2019, when he had turned up at the pancake supper as a grown man of twenty-two with a girlfriend, and had come through her line, and had said, “You gave me extra for like three months.”

“I did.”

“My mom died.”

“I know,” Deb had said. “That’s why.”

And Marcus Dwyer had stood in a gymnasium holding a paper plate and said, “Yeah, I figured that out when I was about nineteen,” and had gone off to sit with his girlfriend, and that had been the whole conversation, and Deb had thought about it approximately once a month ever since.

She stood in a cooler in February and thought: *twelve weeks of rice, and he worked it out at nineteen, and it counted.*

Then she thought: *and I have just yelled at a twelve-year-old for asking for rice.*

She went out and found him.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She found him at the end of lunch, at the tray return, and she did it right there where people could see, because she had learned somewhere along the way that an apology delivered in private to a kid is half an apology.

“Dominic. Hey.”

He turned around, wary in the way of a boy who has been called back by an adult before.

“I was sharp with you,” Deb said. “That wasn’t about you and it wasn’t fair, and I’m sorry. You didn’t do anything you don’t do every day.”

He stared at her.

“You’re not in trouble,” she added. “This is me being in trouble, with myself.”

And Dominic Reyes, twelve years old, said, “Okay,” and then, after a beat in which she could actually see him decide the coast was clear, “So can I have extra rice tomorrow?”

Deb put her hand over her eyes.

“No.”

“WORTH A SHOT,” he yelled, already walking away, arms up like he’d won something, and the aide by the door laughed out loud, and Marisol, twenty feet back, said, “Unbelievable,” to no one in particular.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She had not slept properly since the twenty-second of February.

That was the context, and she offered it to nobody, including herself. She got up at five-forty and did four hundred and twelve trays and came home and ate something and got into bed at ten and lay there.

What she had was a house with a boy in it three nights a week and a court date on a calendar and a phone that did not ring, and a specific piece of arithmetic she had done on a Sunday and had promised herself she would not repeat and had repeated a dozen times.

She had a third data point by the last Tuesday of the month and she did not write it down, because writing it down would have made it a project, and instead she carried it around in her head where it was somehow worse.

Two.

Two dinners in the first week of March, which had not happened yet on the day she snapped at Dominic Reyes, but which she could already see coming off the schedule on the fridge, because he posted the schedule on the fridge, because he was scrupulous, because he had never once left her guessing about where he physically was.

He was not disappearing. He was documenting his own disappearance and putting it on the refrigerator with a magnet.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The kitchen ran seven people. Deb, Marisol, a woman named Bev who had worked there twenty-seven years and was retiring in June and who had been announcing her retirement since approximately 2019, two part-timers, a dishwasher called Andre who was nineteen and wanted to be a firefighter, and a floater from the district who came Tuesdays and Thursdays and whose name Deb had learned four times.

They were, on any given day, feeding four hundred and twelve children on a budget that had last been meaningfully adjusted in 2017.

That was the part nobody outside the building understood, and Deb had stopped trying to explain it around 2019. The job was not cooking. The job was arithmetic performed at speed under a federal reimbursement structure, with a produce order that had to be placed nine days out and a freezer that held what it held and a state requirement about the number of components on a tray, and inside all of that there was a woman deciding, on a Tuesday, whether she could make the numbers work for the good rolls instead of the other rolls.

She could, that Tuesday. She’d been moving money around the order for three weeks to get there.

Nobody noticed. That was fine. That was the entire nature of it: four hundred and twelve children ate a roll that was better than the roll they would otherwise have eaten and not one of them would ever know that a fifty-four-year-old woman had spent three weeks on it.

Bev noticed.

“You got the rolls.”

“I got the rolls.”

“How.”

“I moved the canned pears to the twenty-eighth and dropped a case of the corn.”

“You dropped a case of corn to get rolls.”

“They eat the rolls,” Deb said. “They don’t eat the corn. They’ve never eaten the corn. We’ve been putting corn on those trays since I started and I have personally scraped ninety percent of it into a bin every single day for twenty years.”

“The state says corn.”

“The state says vegetable. I’ll give them the vegetable.” Deb closed the binder. “But I’m not doing it with corn and rolls both, so this week it’s rolls.”

Bev, twenty-seven years in, retiring in June, said: “That’s why you run it and I don’t.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

They ate their own lunch at one-fifteen at the little table by the office, which was the ritual, cold and rushed and better than most meals Deb ate all week.

“You want to tell me,” Marisol said.

“Not really.”

“Okay.”

They ate. Deb got about ninety seconds in.

“Four dinners,” she said.

“What?”

“Four dinners in the last two weeks. It was eleven in January.” She turned her fork over. “And every hour of it’s at that store, and every hour’s got a reason, and the reasons are all good, Marisol, that’s what I can’t get past. He’s not out drinking in a field. He’s at a job. He’s saving money for a room he found himself on a website. If I called Renee right now and told her what was happening she’d say that’s a young man handling his business.”

“It is a young man handling his business.”

“I know it is.”

“And you’d like to strangle him.”

“I would like,” Deb said carefully, “to sit down for a meal.”

Marisol chewed for a while.

“You know he’s doing it on purpose.”

“I know he’s doing it on purpose.”

“No, but do you know why.”

“Because it’s easier to leave a house you’re barely in.”

“For who,” Marisol said.

Deb stopped.

“That’s not a trick question,” Marisol said. “I’m asking. He’s making the leaving easier. For who.”

Deb looked at the far wall of the cafeteria, the mural the eighth graders had painted in 2019 that had a dolphin in it for reasons nobody had ever explained.

“He thinks it’s for me,” she said.

“Mm.”

“He thinks if he’s already gone by the time he’s gone it’ll be a smaller thing. He’s rationing it out. He’s doing to me exactly what he’d want somebody to do to him, which is get it over with in pieces so nobody has to have a moment.” She put the fork down. “And the horrible part, Marisol, the actual horrible part, is that he’s not wrong. It is easier. Four dinners hurt less than eleven. He’s right. He’s doing something kind and it’s working and I hate every part of it.”

Marisol nodded slowly and did not disagree, which Deb noticed and was grateful for and slightly winded by.

“So say that to him.”

“Say what to him? ‘Please come home more so it hurts worse’?”

“Yes,” Marisol said.

“That’s insane.”

“It’s completely insane and it’s the true thing, and the true thing is the only thing that ever works with that kid, you’ve told me so yourself about four hundred times.” She stood, picking up both trays because she was already up. “He’s been handled his whole life, Deb. Everybody handles him. The caseworker handles him, the school handles him, every adult in that boy’s life has managed him beautifully. You want to know what would actually get his attention?”

“What.”

“Somebody being inconvenient about him,” Marisol said. “On purpose. Out loud.”

She took the trays. Deb sat there another minute with the dolphin.

Thirty-eight days.


# Chapter 11

[T]{.dropcap}he week before, she had done something she told nobody about, which was to look up the actual procedure.

Not ask Renee. Look it up, at eleven at night, on the county’s own website, and then on the state’s, and then on a legal aid page that turned out to be the clearest of the three.

She learned that an adoption petition for a child in permanent custody went through the probate court, not the juvenile court, which she had not known. She learned that there was a home study requirement that would in her case be abbreviated, given the licensing. She learned the filing fee, which was less than she expected. She learned that there was a period after the petition during which things happened that were described in language she read four times.

And she learned, on the third page of the legal aid document, in a box with a heading, the thing she already knew:

*A child over the age of twelve must consent to their own adoption. A court may proceed without that consent only if the court finds that doing so is in the child’s best interest.*

She read it three times, because there was a door in it and she wanted to be certain about the size of the door.

It was not a door. She worked that out in under a minute, sitting in her own kitchen, and she worked it out the way she worked out an oven that ran forty degrees hot: not from the rule but from the room. No magistrate in that building was going to look at a boy sixteen days short of eighteen, with a job and an address and a plan in a green folder, and decide it was in his best interest to be adopted against his own answer. The clause existed for a nine-year-old with nobody. It did not exist for Eli Vance.

So she had sat at her kitchen table at eleven-forty at night with a laptop and read a sentence with a door in it that went nowhere, and had then, because she was who she was, read the following four pages anyway.

Then she closed the laptop and washed a mug and went up.

She thought about that week afterward, quite a lot, and what she settled on was this: that she had spent an evening researching a legal process she had known from the first day was unavailable, and that she had done it not because she was hoping to find a way around it, but because it was the only form of doing something that existed.

There was no action available to her. Not one. In the entire situation there was not a single lever a fifty-four-year-old woman could pull, and so she had read a legal aid page for an hour and a half at eleven at night, the way people in her position have always done whatever the equivalent was in their own era.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

It happened on a Thursday, over nothing, which she should have expected, because in her experience the big things never arrive on a day you’ve set aside for them.

He was home. That was the first unusual thing. Six-fifteen on a Thursday and Eli was at the kitchen table with his laptop and a mug, and Deb came in from the school with her bag on her shoulder and stopped for half a second in the doorway before she got hold of herself.

“You’re here.”

“Gary sent me home. The system went down, the whole register network, corporate’s problem. He said there’s no point paying two of us to stand in the dark.”

“Is there anything for dinner in this house?”

“There’s the ziti.”

“The ziti’s from Sunday.”

“The ziti’s fine. The ziti’s better.”

The ziti had been made on the Sunday and was on its third appearance, and there is a specific quality to a Thursday dinner that is the last of a Sunday dish: nobody chose it, it is not an occasion, it is simply the food that exists.

Deb had made it in the volume she made everything, which was for four.

She thought about that afterward a great deal. That the largest conversation of the entire three and a half years happened over reheated pasta on a weeknight, because a register network went down in a store on Route 4 and sent a boy home at five.

There had been no version of that conversation that she could have arranged. She had tried. She had constructed, over seven weeks, four separate occasions on which it might sensibly occur, and had abandoned all four, and in the end a corporate IT failure did what she could not.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

So they had the ziti.

And halfway through it, with the oven still ticking as it cooled and the window over the sink gone black and Deb halfway through a story about the cooler at school throwing another fit, Eli put his fork down and said:

“I’m going to say no.”

Deb kept hold of her fork. Later she’d be glad about that, the fork, that her hand had simply continued to exist.

“At the hearing,” she said.

“At the hearing. When she asks. I’m going to say no, and I didn’t want the first time you heard it to be in a room with a magistrate in it.”

The refrigerator hummed. Somewhere out on the street a car went past too fast, the way they did on that stretch.

“Okay,” Deb said.

“I want to tell you why. Not the version I’ll say in there. That one’s going to be short because it has to be.” He was looking at the table, not at her, and his hands had gone flat the way they did for Renee. “Can I do it in one go? If I stop I won’t get back in.”

“Go ahead.”

He took a breath.

“When I was fourteen and I came here, I had a thing I did,” he said. “I did it at every place. I’d figure out in about the first week what the people wanted from me, and then I’d be that. Not lying. Just picking out of the available options. At the Bakers it was quiet, so I was quiet. At the place before that the woman liked when you asked her about her day, so I asked her about her day, every day, for the better part of a year. It’s not hard. You just watch, and then you do it.”

“I know,” Deb said.

He looked up.

“You know?”

“Eli, you asked me about my day every day for two months and then you stopped, and I have never in three and a half years been so relieved about anything in my life.”

He sat with that.

“Right,” he said. “Well. So. What I was going to say is that it took me about a year here to stop doing it, and it wasn’t because you did anything. It’s because you didn’t. You never told me what you wanted. You just kept feeding me and telling me where your hands were going and letting me be in the house.” He turned his mug a quarter turn on the table, a gesture she recognized as her own and had never once seen him make before. “So I got to find out what I’m like. That’s what this house was. It’s the only place I’ve ever been where I found out what I’m actually like when nobody needs me to be something.”

“Eli.”

“I’m not finished, I’m sorry, I have to finish.”

“Okay.”

“So here’s the thing about the adoption.” His jaw worked once. “If I say yes, then on April third a woman in a robe writes down that this worked. That the placement was a success. That the story goes: a boy came out of the system and a good woman took him in and at the end he got a family, the end, and everybody in that room gets to feel like the thing they do all day works.” He shook his head, small. “And that story is not wrong exactly. But it makes me the outcome. It makes me the thing that happened to you. And I have been an outcome since I was twelve years old, Deb. I’ve been a case number and a placement and a data point and a kid who beat the odds, and the one thing I have never been in any of it is a guy who decided something.”

He stopped. Started again, lower.

“I want to walk out of that building on my own paperwork. Not because you didn’t offer. Because you did, and I get to turn it down, and that’s the first time in my whole life that both of those have been available at once.”

Deb sat very still.

“And if I say yes,” he said, “I’d always know I took the thing that was offered. I’ve taken every single thing that was ever offered. It’s how you survive it. You take the placement, you take the bed, you take the coat, you say thank you, and every one of those is somebody being kind and every one of them is you having no other option. This is the first offer in my life I can refuse without losing anything.” His voice went, briefly, and came back. “And I need to. I need to have refused one. Just one.”

He stopped and looked at the table for a second.

“There’s a thing I worked out in about November,” he said, “and I’ve never said it out loud, and I’m going to say it now because I’m not going to get another run at this.”

“Go ahead.”

“When I got here I was fourteen and I’d been in three houses and I had a thing I believed, which was that everybody’s kind to you for a reason, and the job is to find the reason fast so you can be useful about it.” He turned the mug again. “And I found reasons in all three of those houses and I was right every time. That’s the part people don’t get. I wasn’t being paranoid. I was correct. In every single case there was a reason and I found it and I fed it and it worked, and I got fed and I got a bed and nobody hit me and I would call all three of those houses a success.”

“Eli.”

“And then I got here and I looked for the reason and I could not find it.” He looked up. “For about a year. I looked really hard, Deb. I went through everything. I thought it was money at first, then I thought it was church, then I thought it was some kind of guilt thing, then for about four months in 2022 I was pretty sure you were lonely and I was furniture.”

“That’s…”

“That one was closest,” he said. “But it wasn’t right either, and I know it wasn’t right because a lonely person needs you in the room, and you never once needed me in the room. You’d feed me and then you’d go do the crossword. That’s not what lonely does. I know what lonely does, I lived with it when I was twelve.”

“So what did you decide?”

“I decided there wasn’t one.” He said it flatly. “That’s it. It took me about fourteen months, and it’s the strangest thing that’s ever happened to me. There’s a woman in Ohio who ran a lunch line and let a kid live in her house for three and a half years and there’s no reason underneath it. There’s just a person who does that.”

He shook his head slightly.

“And I want you to know I’m aware that’s probably not completely true,” he said. “Everybody’s got reasons. You’ve got reasons. I don’t need to know them and I’ve never asked and I’m not asking now. But whatever they are, they never once got in the room with me, and that’s the difference, and that is the entire reason I’m sitting at this table able to say no to something.”


The oven ticked.

“That’s not about you,” he said. “I’ve been trying for four months to find a way to say that so it doesn’t sound like the thing people say. It’s not about you. If it was anybody else offering, I’d say yes just to be polite, and that’s how I know.”

Deb opened her mouth.

And the doorbell rang.

Both of them jumped. It was a doorbell that got used maybe five times a year and it was, they had both long agreed, far too loud, some previous owner’s decision from the eighties.

She had, she realized afterward, been about four seconds from saying something she had prepared.

She had prepared several things. That was the truth of it, and she made herself look at it later: that she had rehearsed lines for this conversation over three weeks, in the car, in the shower, at the sink, and that at least two of them had been designed to work.

Not lies. Nothing dishonest. Just sentences shaped to land in a particular place.

*I’d have adopted you at fourteen if the law had let me.* True. Also a crowbar.

*You know you’d still be free to go, right? Adoption isn’t a leash.* True. Also an argument dressed as a clarification.

*Can I tell you what it’s been like for me?* True, and the worst of the three, because there is no answer a decent seventeen-year-old can give to that question that leaves him anything.

She had all three of them loaded and she had been about to reach for the second one when the doorbell went, and afterward she would not be able to decide whether she’d have used it, and the not knowing sat in her for a long time.


Deb went. Because that was what you did, you went to the door, even at six-forty on the worst Thursday of the year.

It was Mrs. Petrosyan, in the man’s parka, holding a foil-covered plate with both hands.

“I made too much,” she announced.

“Mrs. Petrosyan.”

“I make it for four. I have made it for four since 1994 and I do not know how to stop, and my son does not come until Easter, so.” She pushed the plate forward. “It’s the lamb thing. There’s rice.”

Deb stood in her doorway with her whole chest full of something and took the plate.

“Thank you.”

“You look terrible.”

“Thank you.”

“Not terrible. Tired. Is he in there? The boy?”

“He’s in there.”

“He did my walk in January and he would not take money.” She said this as though reporting a crime. “Tell him there is rice. He likes rice. I have watched him eat it.”

And she went back down the steps, one hand on the rail, in a housecoat and a parka in February, eighty-one years old, having cooked for four.

Deb closed the door.

She stood in the hallway for a second with the plate warm through the foil and her forehead very briefly against the door frame.

Then she went back in the kitchen and put the plate on the counter and said, “Mrs. Petrosyan made too much.”

“She always makes too much.”

“She makes it for four.”

“Yeah,” Eli said. “I know.”

And that was when it landed, for both of them at once, the pan of ziti that fed four and the woman two doors down cooking for a table that had emptied out in 1994, and for Deb alone the eight extra plates in the basement under a towel, and neither of them said one word about any of it.

Eli looked at the foil plate for a while.

“Is it the lamb thing?”

“It’s the lamb thing.”

“That’s actually incredible,” he said. “That’s genuinely the best food that comes into this house.”

“I’m aware. I’ve made my peace with it.”

He almost smiled. Then he said, still looking at the plate, “So that’s it. That’s the whole reason. I didn’t want you sitting in that room thinking it was a referendum on you, because it isn’t, it’s the opposite, it’s only possible because of you.”

Deb sat back down.

She had practiced in the car for three weeks. She had practiced in front of the rearview mirror like a lunatic, exactly as instructed, until she could say the words like a woman saying a normal sentence in a kitchen.

She looked at him across the ziti and Mrs. Petrosyan’s lamb and said, “That’s all right.”

And her voice did nothing at all, and it came out sounding like nothing, like a normal sentence in a kitchen, and she watched him take it, and watched his shoulders come down about a quarter of an inch, and thought: there. That one’s his. He gets to keep that one.

The rest was hers, and she’d carry it upstairs later, and it would be waiting for her at two in the morning, and that was fine. She’d known the terms going in.

“Eat the lamb,” she said. “It’s better hot.”


# Chapter 12

[S]{.dropcap}he lay awake from two until about four-thirty, and what she did in those two and a half hours was not what she expected.

She did not go over the conversation. That surprised her. She had assumed she would run it back the way you run back an argument, hunting for the place where a different sentence would have produced a different room, and she found when she tried that there wasn’t one. He had given her the whole reason, in one go, exactly as he’d said he would, and there was no seam in it anywhere.

What she did instead was inventory.

She lay in the dark and went through the house, room by room, cataloguing what would still be here on the fourth of April.

The dresser in the back bedroom: hers, staying. The mattress: hers, staying, and she’d have to decide about that. The desk chair, his. The lamp in the corner of the front room she’d bought at the same Goodwill in 2022, hers.

The good frying pan, which he had never let her put in the dishwasher after learning why, and about which he had subsequently become significantly more evangelical than she was.

The mug. There was a mug he used, a brown one, no history to it, from a set of four she’d had since Toledo, and he had used that mug every single day for three and a half years and neither of them had ever remarked on it, and it would be in the cupboard on the fourth of April with the other three.

She got as far as the mug and stopped.

At about four she thought, clearly: *the trick is going to be the objects.* Not the room. Not the empty chair at the table, which everybody warns you about and which is therefore survivable, because you see it coming from three hundred yards.

The mug is going to do it. Or a receipt in a coat pocket, or a tire gauge in a drawer, or the specific pretzels on a shelf at the Kroger in aisle seven, encountered at speed, on a Tuesday, a year from now, by a woman who thought she was fine.

She got up at five-thirty and made a coffee cake.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She did not sleep, and she did not fall apart, and in the morning she made a coffee cake.

It was six-ten. The cake was in the oven by six-forty and she was at the school by seven, and she’d left a note on the counter that said there was cake and that he should take some for Trevor, and by the time she got home that afternoon two thirds of it was gone and the pan was in the sink with water in it.

That was Friday.

On Saturday she made the chili, the big one, the one that took four hours and fed eight, and she froze four portions in the containers and put them in the deep freeze in the basement, and on the way back up the stairs she did not look at the shelf with the towel on it.

Sunday she made the soup with the little pasta, and a tray of the enchiladas, and a loaf of bread that came out dense because she’d rushed the second rise.

On Monday, Marisol came over.

She came over unannounced at four in the afternoon, which was not a thing Marisol did, and she came in without waiting to be asked in, which was, and she stood in Deb’s kitchen and looked at the counter and the stovetop and the two cooling racks and the flour on the floor by the cabinet.

“What’s happening in here.”

“I’m cooking.”

“I can see that. I said what’s happening.”

Deb was cutting an onion. She kept cutting the onion.

“There’s a chicken thing in the oven,” she said. “There’s chili in the freezer, four portions. There’s soup. There’s the enchiladas but the enchiladas didn’t work, the tortillas went to mush, I don’t know what I did wrong. I’m doing the bread again because Sunday’s was dense.”

“Deb.”

“I’ve got a lasagna I want to do tomorrow. The real one, not the fast one.”

“Deb.”

“What.”

“How much of this has he eaten?”

Deb’s knife stopped.

“He’s had the cake,” she said. “He took cake to Trevor’s.”

“And the chili?”

“The chili’s in the freezer.”

“And the soup.”

“He’s had some of the soup.”

“And the enchiladas, which didn’t work, and the bread, which was dense.” Marisol pulled out a chair and sat down in it without taking her coat off. “Sit down.”

“I’m doing the onion.”

“The onion’s done. That onion’s been done for a minute and a half and you’re just moving it around now.”

Deb put the knife down.

She stood at the counter with her back to the room and her hands flat on the edge of it.

“He told me Thursday,” she said.

“I figured.”

“He gave me the whole reason. He’d worked it out, he’d been working it out for months, he said it in one go because he was afraid he’d stop.” She heard her own voice doing something and stopped and started again. “And it was a good reason, Marisol. That’s the thing. It was the best reason I’ve ever heard anybody give for anything. He said he’s taken every single thing that was ever offered to him because that’s how you survive it, and this is the first offer he’s ever been able to refuse without losing something, and he needs to have refused one. Just one.”

Marisol didn’t say anything.

“So I said ‘that’s all right,’” Deb said. “The way we practiced. And I got it right, and you’re the only person alive who’ll know what it cost. I got it right and he took it and his shoulders came down and he ate Mrs. Petrosyan’s lamb and we talked about the pool catalogue.”

“Deb.”

“And then I came in here on Friday morning at six and I made a coffee cake,” she said, “and I have not stopped since, and I am aware of what that is, so if that’s what you came over to tell me, you can save it.”

Marisol sat with her coat on and her hands in her lap.

“That’s not what I came over to tell you,” she said.

“No?”

“No. I came over because you’ve cooked for a family of six every day for four days and I wanted to see if you’d share.”

Deb turned around.

“What?”

“I’m hungry, Deb. I’ve been thinking about that chili since you texted me a picture of it at nine o’clock at night like a lunatic.” Marisol shrugged out of her coat finally, hung it on the back of the chair. “Get two bowls. Real ones, not the paper ones you use when you think nobody’s coming.”

Deb stood there.

“I’ve got four portions frozen.”

“Then you’ll have three.”

“Sit,” Marisol said again. “I’m not doing this while you’re vertical.”

Deb sat.

“Now. Here’s what happens next, and I’ve thought about it in the car so it’s not off the top of my head.” Marisol counted on her fingers. “One, you’re going to keep cooking, because that’s not a symptom, that’s what you do, and I’m not going to be the woman who tells a person her one good thing is a problem. Two, you’re going to freeze it instead of throwing it out, which you have not been doing, and I know because I looked in your bin on the way in and there’s a whole lasagna in there.”

“There’s not a whole lasagna in the bin.”

“There’s most of one, Deb. There’s a majority of a lasagna in your bin under a cereal box.”

Deb put her hand over her eyes.

“It didn’t work.”

“It didn’t work because you were angry at it. That’s why the tortillas went to mush too. You’ve been cooking angry for four days and you’ve thrown out about forty dollars of food.” Marisol put her hands flat on the table. “So. Freezer. Containers. I’ll bring you the containers, I’ve got a dozen of the good ones and I’ve never used more than four.”

“Fine.”

“Three,” Marisol said. “You’re going to tell him you’re all right.”

Deb looked up.

“He hasn’t asked.”

“He’s not going to ask. He’s seventeen and he’s got a whole apparatus. But he watched you make a coffee cake at six in the morning on the day after he told you no, and then he watched four more days of it, and he is currently upstairs doing calculus doing his own arithmetic about what he’s done to you.” Marisol’s voice was flat and kind. “That’s the part you haven’t thought about, because you’ve been busy having a feeling, and I say that with love.”

Deb sat with that.

“He’ll be doing the math,” she said.

“He’ll be doing the math. That kid does nothing but math.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

They ate at the table. The chili was very good, and Marisol said so twice, and complained about the level of cumin in a way that constituted the highest compliment in her available register, and told a story about her nephew’s driving test that had a shocking ending.

Somewhere in the middle of the second bowl Deb said, “I’m not going to fight him on it.”

“I know.”

“I thought about it. I want you to know I thought about it. I sat up Thursday night and went through every version. Ask him to wait. Ask him to think about it till the hearing. Tell him what it’d mean to me, actually say the sentence, because he’s never once heard me say a sentence like that and it might land.” She turned her spoon over. “I could probably get him. That’s what I figured out at about two in the morning. If I really went at it, if I said the true thing in the right voice, I think I could probably get him to say yes.”

Marisol put her spoon down.

“Yeah,” she said. “I think you probably could.”

“And then he’d have taken one more thing that was offered.”

Neither of them said anything for a bit.

“That’s the answer, isn’t it,” Deb said. “There’s no version where I win it. If I get it, it’s not the thing. The only way it’s worth anything is if he picks it, and he’s not going to pick it, and the reason he’s not going to pick it is the exact same reason I’d want him to.”

“That’s about the size of it.”

“So I cook.”

“So you cook,” Marisol agreed. “And I come over and eat it, and that’s the arrangement, and I’d like to formalize it because frankly I’ve been eating like a raccoon since Christmas.”

“You always eat like a raccoon.”

“I eat like a raccoon with a job,” Marisol said. “There’s a difference and it’s mostly hours.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

“Can I ask you a thing,” Deb said, into her second bowl.

“You’re going to.”

“Why do you come?”

Marisol looked up.

“To dinner?”
