“Eleven years of Fridays. You’ve got a house, you’ve got a son in Toledo, you’ve got that entire book club you complain about constantly.” Deb turned her spoon. “You could eat at home.”

“I could eat at home.”

“So why?”

Marisol put her spoon down and sat back, and did the thing she did where she took slightly too long, which meant she was going to say a true thing and was checking it first.

“Ray died in 2013,” she said.

“I know when Ray died.”

“You know when Ray died because I told you at a staff meeting in a hallway, which is the single worst way I have ever done anything.” Marisol turned her glass. “And for about a year after that I ate standing up. Every night. Over the sink, out of the pan, whatever was there, and I did not notice I was doing it, and one night my son came home unannounced from school and found me eating rice out of a pot at nine-forty at night with the lights off.”

Deb didn’t say anything.

“He didn’t make a thing of it,” Marisol said. “He was twenty-two. He just got a bowl down and put half of it in the bowl and sat at the table and waited until I sat down too. And then he went back to school on Sunday, and I ate standing up again on Monday.”

“Marisol.”

“Then in 2014 you had that thing with your hip, and you asked me to bring you a casserole, and I brought you a casserole and you made me sit down and eat half of it.” She shrugged. “And I’ve been sitting down at your table every Friday ever since, and you’ve never asked me why, and I’ve been extremely glad about that for eleven years.”

The kitchen was quiet.

“You’d have said if I’d asked,” Deb said.

“I’d have said something. It wouldn’t have been that.” Marisol picked her spoon back up. “You have to be eleven years in and two bowls down before you get that one. That’s just the price.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Later, at the door, coat back on, she said, “Can I say one thing you’re not going to like.”

“You’ve said nine.”

“One more.” Marisol got her keys out and didn’t look up. “He’s going to leave in April and you’re going to be all right, eventually, in the way people are. But you’ve got about six weeks left where he’s in that house, and you are currently spending them making lasagna.”

Deb leaned against the door frame.

“He’s never home.”

“He’s home more than you think. He’s home at eleven at night doing homework at that table, you’ve told me, and you go up at ten.” Marisol found the right key. “You’ve got a boy in your house for six more weeks who found out what he’s like in it. Be in there with him. That’s it, and I’ll shut up about it now.”

She went out to the car.

Deb closed the door and stood in the hallway and looked into the kitchen, where every surface was covered in food that nobody in that house was going to eat.

Then she got a container down and started putting the chili away, and at eleven that night, when the fourth stair creaked and the kitchen light went on downstairs, she was still awake.

She got up. Put on the robe.

Went down.


# Chapter 13

[T]{.dropcap}he eleven o’clock arrangement began by accident on a Monday in March and neither of them ever acknowledged that it had begun.

That was its entire operating principle. If it had been proposed it would have been declined; if it had been named it would have collapsed. What happened instead was that a woman came downstairs one night for a glass of water at an hour she did not normally come downstairs, and found a boy at a table, and stayed for twenty minutes, and then did it again two nights later.

By the second week it was four nights out of seven. By the third it was most nights he was in the house, and she had begun to go to bed at ten with the specific intention of getting up at eleven, which is a thing she would have found ridiculous if anybody had described it to her.

They talked about nothing. That was the other operating principle. In something like twenty of those sessions across four weeks, the following subjects were never raised: the hearing, the adoption, Bridges, April, the boxes at Trevor’s, the empty drawer, Danny.

What they talked about was the slack line, and torque specs, and a documentary about a bridge, and whether Bev would actually retire, and a customer who tried to return an alternator, and the correct way to store a cast iron pan, and the auditor’s website, and whether a person needed a car in a town like this or merely wanted one.

It was the most sustained conversation of both their lives and it was entirely about nothing, and neither of them ever once said so.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

He was at the table with the laptop and a bowl of the chili, and he looked up when she came in like a man caught.

“I didn’t wake you.”

“You didn’t wake me. I was up.” She got a glass down, filled it, sat. “Is that the chili?”

“It’s the chili.”

“How is it.”

“It’s got too much cumin,” Eli said.

“Marisol said that exact sentence in that exact chair four hours ago.”

“Well. She’s right.”

So Deb sat with him at eleven at night while he did the last of a government assignment, and she didn’t talk much, and he didn’t either, and that turned out to be the arrangement. She came down at eleven. Not every night. Most nights. She brought whatever she had going and he ate it and told her what was wrong with it, and around midnight one or the other of them would say something about going up, and neither of them ever named the thing as a thing.

It was the best four weeks she’d had with him, which was a fact that would ambush her, at intervals, for years.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She found the drawer on a Tuesday in the first week of March.

She was looking for the extra phone charger, which lived in the top drawer of the dresser in his room, and she’d knocked, and he wasn’t home, and going in was normal, they’d had three and a half years of her going in for a charger or laundry and it had never been a thing.

The drawer was empty.

Not tidied. Empty. The top drawer, which had held socks and a charger and a small pile of things that accumulate in a top drawer, an old watch, a broken pair of sunglasses, a rubber band ball he’d built over about a year at the parts counter.

She stood there with her hand on the drawer front.

Then she did a thing she was not proud of, which was that she opened the second drawer.

Two thirds gone. Third drawer, mostly there. Closet: his coats were there, the good boots were there, but the duffel bag was not, the eleven-dollar Goodwill duffel bag that had lived on the closet floor for three and a half years was not on the closet floor.

She closed everything. Went downstairs. Sat at the table.

He’d been moving out for weeks. In pieces small enough that nothing announced itself, exactly like the dinners, exactly like the shifts, the same method applied to a dresser. She thought about the boxes and where they’d be going and arrived at Trevor Pham’s garage in about four seconds, because Trevor’s family had a two-car garage with one car in it and Trevor had never asked Eli a question in his life.

She sat there for a long time.

Then she got up and put the pan on for the fish, because it was Tuesday, and she’d bought fish.

She did not say anything about the drawer.

Not that night, and not that week, and eventually not at all. She wasn’t sure, at the time, whether she was being generous or a coward. She decided later it had been both, in a proportion she never settled.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She started leaving the light on over the stove.

That was the first adjustment, and she made it deliberately on a Wednesday and never mentioned it. He came in at eleven, most nights now, and for three and a half years he had come into a dark kitchen and put a light on himself, and starting in the second week of March there was a light on when he got there.

He noticed the first night. She watched him notice, from the doorway, in her robe.

“You left the light on.”

“The switch is broken.”

“The switch is not broken.”

“The switch is intermittently broken,” Deb said, “and I’d rather not discuss it,” and he let it go, and it stayed on every night after that, and neither of them touched it again.

The second adjustment was the leftovers.

She’d been putting a plate in the fridge with foil on it since 2022, and in the second week of March she started putting two things in there instead of one, the dinner and something else, whatever was around, a piece of the coffee cake or the last of the soup in a mug. Not a lot. An extra thing. And she began, without ever once framing it to herself in words, to buy the food he liked in quantities that would outlast him: the specific pretzels, a second jar of the jam, the good cereal that cost two dollars more.

She had a cupboard, by the third week of March, that was stocked for a household of two well into May.

She did not think about it. She was extremely careful not to think about it, in the way a person can be, and then on a Sunday she opened that cupboard to get the coffee and stood there looking at four boxes of cereal, and closed it again, and went and sat down in the front room for a minute.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She told Renee about the drawer. That was the one deviation, and she made it on a Tuesday in the first week of March, on the phone, in the school parking lot, and it took forty seconds.

“He’s started moving out.”

“I know.”

Deb sat with the phone against her ear.

“You know.”

“He told me on the twelfth of February that he had boxes at a friend’s. He’s told me every step of it, actually, in advance, every visit since Christmas.” Renee’s voice was matter of fact. “That’s not me keeping something from you. He’s seventeen, the conversations are his, and he’s under no obligation to route them through your kitchen. He’s also told me, every single time, that he hadn’t told you yet and that he was going to.”

“He hasn’t.”

“No. He won’t.” A pause on the line. “Deb, can I say the thing I actually think?”

“Yes.”

“He’s not hiding it. He’s leaving it where you’ll find it.” Renee said it slowly. “The drawer. The boxes at Trevor’s that you worked out in four seconds. The letter he left face up on the counter. That’s a young man who has spent his whole life unable to say a thing out loud and has become extremely good at putting it where you’ll trip over it.”

Deb looked at the windshield.

“That’s what I thought about the letter.”

“You were right about the letter.”

“So what do I do?”

“Keep tripping over it,” Renee said. “That’s the entire job. He puts it somewhere and you find it and you don’t make him say it, and that’s the arrangement you two have had since about 2022 and it’s working, and I’d change nothing about it.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The June schedule she found out about at the Kroger.

That was the part that got her. Not the drawer, which she’d half expected, which she’d have put money on. The schedule.

She was in the cereal aisle on a Saturday and a woman said, “Deb Hale?” and it was Sheila Kowalczyk, who had done the school board thing for years and whose husband Ron had been buying his parts at AutoWorks since the Reagan administration.

“Sheila.”

“I hear congratulations are in order. Ron says your boy’s going on full time in June.”

Deb’s hand was on a box of cereal.

“He is,” she said.

“Thirty-eight hours, Ron says, and Gary’s fighting corporate to get him benefits in September, which frankly I did not know Gary was capable of that level of exertion.” Sheila laughed. “Ron says he’s the only person in that store who knows what he’s talking about. Says he’d rather deal with him than the manager, which he has told Gary. To his face.”

“That sounds like Ron.”

“He’s got no filter, none, it’s been forty-one years.” Sheila patted her arm. “You must be so proud of him.”

“I am,” Deb said. “I’m very proud of him.”

She stood in the cereal aisle for a minute after Sheila went.

Thirty-eight hours. Benefits in September. Gary fighting corporate.

She did the arithmetic on when, and it came out at some point before the middle of February, because Sheila had said Ron had known “for a while,” and Ron did not sit on information for a single hour of his life.

Six weeks. He’d had it six weeks and hadn’t said.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She thought about it all the way home, and here was the thing she landed on, somewhere around the light at Fourth and Chestnut:

He hadn’t hidden it. If she’d asked him a direct question, he’d have told her the truth, immediately, in full, with the wage per hour. He didn’t lie. He had never once lied to her, about anything, which was more than she could say for her own son.

He’d just declined to hand it over.

And she understood why, sitting at that light, with the turn signal going. Because if he brought it home she’d have been glad, so glad, and it would have become theirs. A thing they had together. And he’d built it by himself at a counter on Route 4, and he needed one part of the structure to have only his name on it, and if she thought about it honestly for four consecutive seconds she’d have to admit she’d been doing the same thing in reverse since January with a box of dishes under a towel in the basement.

Both of them, all winter, quietly building the other one a life they weren’t going to mention.

She got home and put the groceries away.

She thought about the drawer for twelve days before she understood what was actually bothering her about it.

It was not that he was packing. She had known he was packing, and packing was correct, and a boy who was moving in six weeks and had not begun to pack would have been a boy she was worried about.

It was the order.

He had emptied the top drawer first and then two thirds of the second. Which meant he had taken the things he did not need for six weeks and left the things he did, which was exactly right, and exactly what a competent adult would do.

Which meant he had stood in that room with the drawers open and sorted his own belongings into *before* and *after.*

He had made a small pile of the parts of his life that did not require this house anymore, and he had done it alone, on a weeknight, without mentioning it, and then he had come downstairs and eaten a plate of chili and told her about a man returning an alternator.

She could not decide whether that was heartbreaking or magnificent and eventually concluded, a day or two after that, that it was simply what it was, and that her requirement that it be one of the two was hers and not his.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

That night at eleven she went down, and he was at the table, and she put the kettle on and said, “Ran into Sheila Kowalczyk at the Kroger.”

Something in his shoulders went very slightly still.

“Yeah?”

“Ron’s been telling the whole town about the June schedule.”

Eli looked at his laptop for a second.

“I was going to tell you,” he said.

“I know you were.”

“I was. That’s not a thing I’m saying. I had it worked out that I’d tell you when the schedule actually posted, because until it posts it’s a conversation and after it posts it’s a fact, and I wanted to hand you a fact.” He rubbed the back of his neck. “That’s the reason I gave myself. I don’t know if it’s the actual reason.”

“It’s part of the actual reason.”

“Yeah.”

Deb got two mugs down instead of one.

“Thirty-eight hours,” she said. “And Gary’s going after benefits in September.”

“He says he’ll lose. He’s going to do it anyway.”

“He won’t lose. That man’s been running that store for twenty years, he knows exactly which forms to fill out wrong.” She poured. “Fifty cents over what you had in the folder, wasn’t it.”

Eli’s head came up.

“How do you know what was in the folder?”

“You left it on the table in January,” Deb said. “I read every page of it and I have felt bad about that exactly zero percent of the time.”

He stared at her.

Then he laughed. Actually laughed, out loud, once, the sound of it strange and good in the kitchen at eleven at night.

“You read my budget.”

“I read your budget. Your slack line’s too thin.”

“My slack line’s fine.”

“Your slack line’s a hundred and ten dollars and you’ve got no line at all for a car repair, and you drive a Civic with a hundred and sixty thousand miles on it.”

“I can do my own repairs.”

“With what parts, Eli?”

He opened his mouth. Closed it.

“That’s fair,” he said.

“Yes it is.”

“I’ll adjust the slack line.”

“You’ll adjust the slack line,” Deb agreed, and put his mug down in front of him, and sat down across the table, and they argued for forty minutes about how much a person should keep in reserve.


# Chapter 14

[T]{.dropcap}he week between the slack line and the supper was the flattest stretch of the whole winter, and Deb was grateful for it in a way she would not have predicted.

Nothing happened. He worked and she worked and he came in late and she came down at eleven, and neither of them made anything of it. He was at that table most nights and not at it the rest of the time and there was no arithmetic being conducted by anybody.

That was the whole gift of it: for six days, a boy and a woman had an arrangement, and the arrangement held, and nobody had to be brave about anything.

She got the gutters quoted, which came to four hundred and eighty and which she declined. She got a filling done. She read most of a book.

And on the Thursday of it, at eleven at night, Eli said, out of nowhere, “You know what I’m going to miss,” and Deb said “what,” and he thought about it for a second and said, “I don’t know. That’s the problem. I’ve been trying to finish that sentence for about a week.”

“Take your time.”

“That’s not…” He frowned at the laptop. “I mean I can’t get it to be a thing. It’s not the food. It’s not the house. I’ve been going through it like a list and there’s no item.”

“Maybe there isn’t one.”

“There has to be one.”

“Why?”

He looked up.

“Because if there’s no item,” Eli said slowly, “then it’s the whole thing, and I can’t carry the whole thing to Pryor Street.”

And then he went back to his laptop and neither of them said anything else about it, and Deb went up at twenty past eleven and sat on the edge of her bed for a while.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The Deerbrook Falls Middle School Spring Auction and Pancake Supper was held on the second Friday in March in the gymnasium, and it had been held on the second Friday in March in that gymnasium every year since 1991, and it was, by common agreement among the staff, the single worst evening of the school year.

Deb loved it.

She loved it the way you love a thing that is entirely yours to fix. Four hundred pancakes, two griddles, a warming oven that worked if you knew it, three parent volunteers who did not know it, and a hard stop at seven-thirty when the auction started. She had run this supper for fourteen years. She had a laminated sheet. Nobody had ever asked her for the laminated sheet.

“The batter’s wrong,” said one of the volunteers, a man named Craig in a quarter-zip.

“The batter’s fine.”

“It seems thin.”

“It’s supposed to be thin, it’s a griddle, if it’s thick you get a hockey puck with a wet middle and then a sixth grader tells his mother about it and she puts it on Facebook.” Deb took the ladle off him with no ceremony whatsoever. “Go do the syrup station. The syrup station is a real job and I need somebody good on it.”

Craig went to do the syrup station, visibly restored.

Marisol, at the second griddle, said, “You just handled that man like a horse.”

“He’s a nice man.”

“He is a nice man and you put a bag over his head and led him to the syrup.” She flipped four at once. “How’s your night going, other than professionally.”

“Fine.”

“Uh huh.”

The gym filled up. It always did. Four hundred people on folding chairs eating pancakes off paper plates under a basketball hoop, and the noise in there was tremendous, kids on the stage risers, somebody’s toddler screaming with joy at nothing, the PTO president trying to get the microphone to work and being defeated by it in front of everyone.

Around six-forty a kid came through the line and said, “Ms. Hale.”

Dominic Reyes, in a shirt with a collar, hair combed by an adult.

“Look at you.”

“My mom made me.” He looked appalled about it. “Can I have three pancakes.”

“Everybody gets two.”

“That’s the thing about a pancake supper, though,” Dominic said. “The word supper implies a certain…”

“Two, Dominic.”

“…implies a certain quantity…”

“Two.”

He took his two and moved down the line, and then turned around and came back and said, at a slightly lower volume, “Are you okay, though? Because you were weird that one day.”

Deb stopped with the ladle in her hand.

“I’m okay,” she said. “That was a hard week and it wasn’t about you and I’m still sorry about it.”

“It’s fine,” Dominic said, with enormous magnanimity. “Everybody has a week.” And then, immediately: “So three pancakes?”

“Get out of my line.”

“WORTH A SHOT,” he bellowed, and went, and Marisol at the second griddle said, without looking up, “That child’s going to run for office.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Between six and seven the gym did the thing it did every year, which was fill up past the point of comfort with people who all knew each other.

Deb worked the line and watched it happen. She had been watching it happen for years and she had a private taxonomy for it.

There were the young families, who came at five-thirty because of bedtimes and were gone by six-forty-five, harried and grateful, one parent holding a plate above a toddler’s reach.

There were the grandparents, who came at six because that was when the middle child was performing something on the risers, and who arrived in numbers wildly disproportionate to the child in question, six adults for one ten-year-old with a recorder.

There were the teachers, who came because they had to, and who sat together at the back in a cluster of exhaustion and were served first by unspoken arrangement.

There were the people who had no child in that building at all, who came because it was the second Friday in March and they had come to this supper since 1998, and who Deb knew by name and by pancake preference, and who would keep coming after their own grandchildren had graduated, and some of whom would stop coming, each year, quietly, in a way nobody announced.

Mr. Halvorsen was not there that year. He had come every year since 2011, always at six-fifteen, always three pancakes and coffee, always a five-dollar bill in the donation bucket and never more, and he had died in November, and Deb had known that and had still, at six-fifteen, looked toward the far doors.

She noticed herself doing it. She put four more pancakes on the griddle.

Marisol, without turning her head, said, “Halvorsen.”

“Yes.”

“I did it at ten past. I looked at the doors.”

“There’s about nine of them now,” Deb said. “Nine people I look for at this thing who aren’t coming.”

“That’s what twenty years is.”

“That’s what twenty years is,” Deb agreed, and flipped, and the line kept moving.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Deb saw Eli at seven-fifteen.

She hadn’t expected him. He’d said he had a shift, and then apparently he hadn’t, and he came in through the far doors in his coat with Trevor Pham beside him, and the two of them worked the room like men on a mission, which they were, because the mission was free pancakes.

He came through the line last, when it was thinning.

“You said you had a shift.”

“Gary let me off at six. Trevor drove.”

“You want two?”

“I want six.”

“Everybody gets two.”

“Deb.”

“That’s the rule. Ask Dominic Reyes, he’s been litigating it since September.”

She gave him four, because she was the one holding the ladle and there was no higher authority in that gym, and Eli took the plate and looked at it and said, “This is a corrupt institution,” and went to eat them standing up by the wall with Trevor, and Deb watched him do it, over the heads of four hundred people, for about three seconds, and then went back to the griddle.

That was the whole thing. He came, he ate four pancakes, he left at seven-forty because Trevor had a curfew.

Nothing happened in those three seconds. He was in a gymnasium eating pancakes off a paper plate at seventeen years old and there was no part of it that was hard, and there had been a March three years ago when he’d have stood outside the doors for ten minutes deciding whether to come in.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The auction itself was the part Deb had no patience for, which she had long ago stopped apologizing about.

Sixty-one items, donated by local businesses and parents, sold by a man named Lyle who did it every year for free and who was, in fairness, extremely good at it. A weekend at somebody’s lake place. A quilt. Four rounds of golf at the club out on 725. A hand-carved bench that went for twelve hundred and fifty and had gotten a thousand dollars in some form every year since 2014 because the same three families bid against each other for it out of a rivalry nobody could date anymore.

It raised about nine thousand and it went to the classroom fund and it mattered.

Deb watched from the kitchen doorway with a coffee, over the heads of four hundred people, and thought about the fact that the pancakes had cost the school four hundred and ten dollars in ingredients and had taken her a dozen hours of unpaid preparation across two weeks, and that the auction would raise nine thousand in fifty minutes, and that nobody, ever, in fourteen years, had asked her to stop doing the pancakes.

That was the thing she’d say if anybody ever asked her, which nobody had. You could raise nine thousand dollars in a gymnasium in fifty minutes and it would be a fundraiser. You could not do it in a gymnasium where four hundred people had already eaten together off paper plates, unless somebody spent those unpaid hours making that happen first.

Lyle got to the bench. The three families started. Marisol appeared at her elbow with two coffees and said, “Here we go,” and both of them settled in to watch, and the Kepners took it at twelve hundred and fifty, and the gym made the noise it made.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The auction ran till nine. The cleanup ran till ten. At ten-fifteen Deb and Marisol sat on the edge of the stage risers with two cups of terrible coffee and their shoes off.

“So,” Marisol said. “Craig.”

“Don’t.”

“He asked me about you. At the syrup station. He asked me, and I quote, whether you were seeing anybody, and I told him I’d look into it, which is why I’m looking into it.”

“He’s married.”

“He’s divorced. Two years. Ann Marie left him and moved to Tempe and everyone in this school district knows about it except you, because you don’t listen to anything anybody says in that kitchen.”

“I listen to everything anybody says in that kitchen. That’s how I know he’s a nice man who can’t read a batter.”

Marisol let it go for about forty seconds.

“You could have dinner with somebody,” she said.

“I have dinner with somebody. I have dinner with you, at my table, every Friday, and you never bring anything.”

“I bring myself, which is a great deal.” She stretched her feet out. “I’m going to say the thing now.”

“I know you are.”

“He’s out of that house in three weeks.”

Deb looked at her coffee.

“And I’ve been at your table eleven years,” Marisol said, “and I’ve watched you fill that house four times with four different kids, and every time one goes, you’re on the phone to Renee inside a month. Every time. Malik went to that aunt in Michigan in the January and you had the little girl with the braids in the house inside a month. And she went back to her mother in the fall and you had Jaylen by Thanksgiving.”

“That’s the job.”

“That’s not the job. The job is fostering. Getting somebody in the house before the sheets are cold is a different activity and it doesn’t have a name.”

Deb didn’t say anything.

“Are you calling Renee in May,” Marisol said.

“I don’t know.”

“Deb.”

“I don’t know, Marisol.” It came out harder than she meant, and it echoed slightly in the empty gym. “I really don’t know. I’ve thought about it maybe nine hundred times since Christmas and every time I get to the same place, which is that I don’t know what I do in that house if I’m not doing that.”

“You live in it.”

“That’s not an answer, that’s a sentence.”

“It’s an answer.”

“It’s what people say.”

Marisol put her cup down on the riser.

“What are you afraid of,” she said. “Say the actual thing. Not the version. I’ve had four hours of pancakes and I’ve earned the actual thing.”

Deb sat there with her shoes off in an empty gymnasium at ten-twenty at night.

“That it’s the house,” she said.

“What?”

“That there’s something about the house.” She turned the cup around in her hands. “Frank left. Danny left, and Danny moved out at nineteen and he hasn’t been back through that door since 2019. Three kids in that time, all of them gone, and I know, I know, that’s how fostering works, that’s the whole design of it, they’re supposed to go. But it’s three for three, Marisol. And now Eli, who I’d have said, if you’d asked me two years ago, who I’d have bet on.” She stopped. Made herself finish. “So at two in the morning it stops being about the system and starts being about whether there’s something in that house that people get to the end of.”

Marisol didn’t jump in. She let it sit for a good ten seconds, which felt enormous.

“Okay,” she said. “That’s the actual thing. Thank you.” She picked her cup back up. “Now I’m going to tell you the two parts of it that are true and the one part that isn’t, and then we’re going home because my feet have died.”

“Marisol.”

“True part one: they all left, that’s a fact, you’re not making it up. True part two: you don’t know how to be in that house without somebody to feed, which is a real problem and it’s yours and it’s going to be sitting there on April fourth whether you like it or not.” She stood, wincing, and found her shoes. “The part that isn’t true is the house.”

“You don’t know that.”

“I know Frank,” Marisol said. “Frank would have left a palace. Frank left because Frank leaves, and you knew that for years before he went, and you’ve been quietly carrying it as evidence against yourself ever since anyway, which is the single most Deb Hale thing about you.” She put a hand on Deb’s shoulder on the way past, brief, not making a thing of it. “Come on. Get your shoes. I’ll help you do the griddles.”

“The griddles are done.”

“Then get your shoes anyway.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Deb got home at eleven. The kitchen light was on.

Eli was at the table with his laptop, and there was a plate next to him with four pancakes on it, cold, uneaten, on one of the good plates.

“What’s this.”

“I took extra.”

“You took extra.”

“I took a lot of extra. Trevor created a distraction.” He didn’t look up from the laptop. “They’re cold. You’ll have to microwave them and it’ll ruin them, which is your problem, not mine.”

Deb stood there in her coat.

“I was going to eat cereal,” she said.

“I know. That’s why.”

She microwaved four ruined pancakes and ate them at eleven o’clock at night at her own kitchen table, and they were terrible, and she ate all of them.


# Chapter 15

[T]{.dropcap}he argument they had was about a Tuesday, and it was not about a Tuesday.

She’d told him about the lasagna on Sunday. Not asked. Told, on purpose, standing at the sink with her back to him, in the flattest voice she owned: “I’m doing the real lasagna Tuesday. The four-hour one. So be here at six.”

“I’ve got a shift Tuesday.”

“Then swap it.”

There was a pause behind her.

“Deb.”

“Reggie swapped with you four times since January. You’ve got credit. Use it.” She kept washing the pan she was washing. “I’m doing a four-hour lasagna and I would like the person who lives in this house to eat some of it while it’s hot. That’s the entire request.”

She did not turn around, because she was not sure what her face was doing, and because Marisol had told her to be inconvenient on purpose and she had discovered in about nine seconds that being inconvenient on purpose felt exactly like being a bad person.

“Okay,” Eli said, after a second. “I’ll swap it.”

“Thank you.”

“Yeah.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She had taken a half day, which she never did, and she got the lasagna in at two-fifteen and it needed until five and she had two and three quarter hours to fill in a house by herself.

She cleaned the front room, which did not need it. She sorted the drawer in the hall that had been a disgrace since 2019 and found: a dozen pens of which four worked, a phone charger for a phone nobody in that house had owned since 2017, a set of keys she could not identify and did not throw away, three batteries of unknown charge, a church bulletin from 2021, and a photograph of Danny at about ten in a soccer uniform that she had no memory of putting in a drawer.

She put the photograph on the counter, propped against the toaster, and then moved it, and then put it back.

At four she sat down at the kitchen table with a cup of tea and the lasagna doing its thing and thought about the sentence she was going to have to say at six o’clock, which she had been assembling since Monday and which she had rehearsed in the car twice.

*I would like you to stop.*

She turned it over. It was, she recognized, an outrageous thing to say to him. It was asking a seventeen-year-old to make the last three weeks of his childhood harder, on purpose, for the benefit of a woman who was not legally anything to him.

She got up and checked a lasagna that did not need checking.

At four-forty she sat back down and thought: *Marisol’s right that he’s rationing it. Marisol’s right that it’s a kindness. Marisol’s right that somebody should be inconvenient about him on purpose, once, before he goes.*

And then, at four-fifty, the thing underneath, which she had not let up until then:

*And I want it. Not for him. I want three more dinners a week and I have wanted them since February and I have been sitting in this house being extremely gracious about not having them.*

She looked at that for a while.

Then she got up and set the table for two at ten to five, an hour and ten minutes early, which was the most transparent thing she did that entire year.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

He wasn’t there at six.

He wasn’t there at six-fifteen. At six-thirty she took it out because it was going to catch on top, and she put the foil back over it, and she sat down at the table with her hands in her lap and looked at the clock on the stove.

At six-fifty the front door did its stick-and-give.

He came in with the cold on his coat and said, “Sorry. Sorry. Trevor’s belt went in the lot at the school and took the charging with it and I had to get him started, he was going to leave the car overnight and it’s supposed to freeze and if it freezes with the belt like that it’s…”

“It’s ten to seven.”

“I know. I’m sorry.”

“I asked you for one thing.”

“I know.” He was already taking his coat off. “It’s four minutes of work if you’ve got a wrench and Trevor doesn’t have a wrench and doesn’t have anybody else to call, and I couldn’t just…”

“You couldn’t just what?”

He stopped, coat half off.

Deb heard her own voice come out with an edge on it that she hadn’t authorized.

“You couldn’t just leave him,” she said. “Right? Because that’s not the kind of thing you do. Because he’s your friend and he’s stuck in a parking lot and you can fix it in four minutes and you’d never in your life leave somebody sitting there.”

“Deb, what is…”

“So you’ll do it for Trevor Pham,” she said. “In a parking lot. In the cold. For four minutes.”

The kitchen was very quiet.

Eli stood there with his coat in his hand.

“That’s not fair,” he said.

“No, it isn’t.”

“That’s actually not fair. You want to have a different fight, have the different fight, but don’t do it about Trevor’s car.”

“Fine.”

“No, say it.”

“Fine, Eli.” She stood up, because sitting had become impossible. “You’ve been out of this house since February. You’ve been out of it in an extremely intelligent way, in increments, with a reason for every single one, and I have not said one word about it, not one, for six weeks, because every reason was good and because I have some idea what it costs you to be doing it. But I asked for a Tuesday. I asked for one Tuesday with a lasagna in it, and I got ten to seven and Trevor Pham’s serpentine belt.”

“I was fixing a car.”

“I know what you were doing.”

“Then what are we…”

“You’ve had six weeks,” Deb said, “and you’ve got seventeen days, and you’re spending them somewhere else on purpose, and I know why, and I understand it, and I want you to stop.”

It came out of her sideways and much louder than she’d planned, and it hung there in the kitchen over a covered lasagna.

Eli’s jaw did the thing.

“You want me to stop,” he said.

“Yes.”

“You know why I’m doing it.”

“I know exactly why you’re doing it. You’re rationing it. You’ve worked out that four dinners hurt less than eleven and you’re doing me a kindness, and it’s working, and I’d like you to stop doing it.”

“Because you’d rather it hurt more.”

“Yes,” Deb said.

The word landed in the kitchen and sat there, and she heard how it sounded, and did not take it back.

Eli put his coat on the chair. Very carefully, the way he did everything with his hands.

“That’s insane,” he said.

“I’m aware.”

“That is genuinely insane, Deb.”

“I have been told that by a professional. Marisol Ferreira. She said the same thing and then she told me to say it anyway.”

“So this is Marisol.”

“This is me,” Deb said. “Marisol just got tired of watching.”

He stood there. Something was happening behind his face, and for once she couldn’t read it, and she made herself stand in the middle of her kitchen and let it happen.

“You want to know why I took the Saturdays,” he said.

“I know why you took the Saturdays.”

“No, you know one reason. You’ve got the kind one. That’s the one I’d have told you.” His voice had gone quieter and considerably harder. “You want the other one?”

Deb put her hand on the back of the chair.

“Yes.”

“Because I can’t sit in here,” Eli said. “That’s the other reason. I can’t sit at that table across from you at eleven at night eating your chili and talking about my slack line, knowing what the date is, and be fine. I can’t do it. I did it in January and I did it most of February and it got harder every single week, and the store is eight hours where nobody in the building knows what April third is.” He was breathing wrong. “And the whole time, the entire time, I’ve been telling myself I was doing it for you. That’s the part I want you to have, since we’re doing this. I have been walking around for six weeks feeling like a decent guy for sparing you, and about ten days ago I worked out that I’ve been sparing me, and I have been trying to figure out ever since how to come back in here without you seeing that on my face.”

Deb didn’t move.

“So no, I don’t want it to hurt more,” he said. “I want it to hurt less. I’ve spent my whole life engineering things to hurt less, I’m extremely good at it, it’s the only actual skill I’ve got. And I can’t make this one work, and I’m getting really tired, and you asking me to come home and sit in it on purpose is asking me to do the one thing I don’t know how to do.”

The oven ticked. It had been ticking for an hour.

“I know,” Deb said.

“So don’t ask me that.”

“I’m asking you that.”

He laughed. It was not a good sound, but it was not a bad one either.

“Yeah,” he said. “I know you are.”

They stood in the kitchen.

Then Deb said, “It’s going to be cold.”

“What?”

“The lasagna. It’s been out since six-thirty. It’ll be warm in the middle and cold at the edges and that’s the worst version of it, so I’m putting it back in for fifteen minutes at three-fifty, and it’ll dry out on top, and it’ll be fine. It’ll be fine. It won’t be what it was at six.”

She turned the oven back on.

Eli sat down at the table.

“I should have swapped the shift on Sunday instead of Monday,” he said. “Reggie already had the Tuesday off. That’s why he could take it. I could have asked him Sunday and I sat on it a whole day.”

“Yes.”

“I asked him Monday night.”

“I know,” Deb said. “Sheila Kowalczyk’s husband buys his parts in that store and I know everything that happens in that building four hours after it happens, which I’d have thought a smart kid would have factored in by now.”

“That’s a genuine violation of something.”

“It’s Ohio,” Deb said. “There’s no privacy. There’s just people who haven’t run into your neighbor yet.”

Neither of them moved for a second.

“I’m not doing three nights as a concession,” Eli said. “I want that clear, because if you think it’s a concession you’ll be grateful for it, and then I’ll notice you being grateful and I’ll feel like I did you a favor, and then the three nights turn into a thing I’m giving you.” His coat was still over the back of the chair. “And then in about two weeks I’ll start resenting them, because that’s how that goes, and I’d rather tell you now than find out in April.”

“So what are they?”

“They’re three nights I want,” he said. “That’s all. I looked at the schedule Sunday morning before you said anything about the lasagna and I worked out I could do Tuesday, Thursday and Sunday if Reggie takes the Wednesday, and I hadn’t said it yet because I was working out how to say it without it sounding like exactly what it is.”

Deb held onto the back of the chair.

“Which is what?”

“Which is that I’ve got seventeen days and I want three dinners a week out of them,” Eli said, “which is an enormous amount to want from somebody and I’m aware of it, and I’d like to be allowed to want it without either of us doing a voice about it.”

The kitchen was very quiet.

“You worked that out Sunday morning.”

“I worked it out at about six-fifteen Sunday morning standing in the shower, and then I came downstairs and you told me about the lasagna in the flattest voice I’ve ever heard a human being use, and I thought, well, there’s no way to say it now, she’ll think I’m managing her.” He rubbed his eye with the back of his wrist. “So I said okay and went to work and then I was fifty minutes late because of a car, and now we’ve had a fight.”

Deb sat down slowly at her own table.

“We’ve both been standing in different rooms of this house,” she said, “planning how to ask each other for the same thing.”

“Yeah.”

“For how long?”

“Weeks,” Eli said. “I’d say weeks.”



They ate the lasagna at seven-forty. It was dry on top. It was still the best thing she’d made all year, and he had two pieces and then a third one standing up at the counter, and neither of them apologized to the other for anything, and neither of them took anything back.

At nine he said, “I’m not going to be able to do it every night.”

“I know.”

“Three. I can do three a week. That’s real, that’s not a number I’m saying to end a conversation, I’ve looked at the schedule.”

“Three’s good.”

“Three’s not eleven.”

“Nothing’s eleven,” Deb said. “Eleven was January.”

He nodded slowly and took his plate to the sink, and started the water, and she got up to dry.

Seventeen days.


# Chapter 16

[T]{.dropcap}here was a version of him that would have asked, and he’d been that version until he was about sixteen, and it had taken a specific event to stop.

November of his junior year. Deb had come home from work with something on her and he had known within four minutes and had done the thing: the good opening, delivered at the right moment, from the side. *You’ve been quiet. Everything all right at work?*

And she had told him. She had sat down at the table and told him about a woman named Charlotte at the district office and a proposal about the lunch program and twenty years of a thing she cared about being described in a meeting as an inefficiency, and she had talked for about twenty minutes, and she had cried once, briefly, and got hold of it.

And it had been fine. It had been good, even. He’d said some things and she’d said some things and she’d made pasta at nine at night and they’d both felt better.

And then at about eleven, lying in bed, he had felt the other thing arrive: the small clean satisfaction of a technique that had worked.

That was what stopped him. Not guilt about her. The satisfaction. He had lain in the dark at sixteen years old and recognized, with total clarity, that some part of him had been keeping score, and that the score had gone up, and that a woman had cried at her kitchen table and a boy upstairs had felt competent about it.

He had not used the technique on her since. Not once, in sixteen months, which was not the same as not using it at all, and he knew exactly which Thursday in January it was and exactly what it had cost a man in a store on Route 4, and he had thought about that Thursday a good deal more than he had told anybody.

Not using it on her was, as far as he could tell, the single largest thing he had ever done, and it was completely invisible, and nobody would ever know about it including her.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Something had changed in the house that week, and Eli decided, deliberately, on the Monday, not to ask about it.

He noticed it the way he noticed things, which was by inventory. Deb had stopped doing the thing where she narrated her day at him. She’d started leaving the radio on in the kitchen in the mornings, which she had never done, and twice he’d come down to find her already dressed and sitting at the table at six with coffee, which meant she’d been up since five, which meant she wasn’t sleeping.

He knew exactly what it was about. It was on the fridge in red pen with a box around it, and it was eighteen days out, and neither of them had said the date out loud since February.

He also knew there was a version of the next few days where he asked her about it, gently, at the right moment, and she told him some of it, and he said something useful, and both of them would feel closer afterward.

He’d been able to do that since he was about ten. It was the most effective thing in his toolkit, and it worked on nearly everybody, and he had used it in four houses to make himself easy to keep.

He decided not to.

It was not a decision that had a good reason attached to it, and he turned it over for two days trying to find one. The nearest he got was this: for three and a half years she had never once come at him sideways. She had never used the good moment. When she wanted something she said it out loud in a kitchen with her back to the sink and let him refuse it, and it had taken him about eighteen months to understand that this was not a technique, that she did not know the other way, and about a year after that to understand what it had done to him to live in a house like that.

So he didn’t ask.

What he did instead, on the Wednesday, the day after the lasagna, was cook.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

He had thought about the not-asking for two days and had ended up somewhere he hadn’t expected, which was a Tuesday afternoon in October of his sophomore year.

He’d come home from school with a thing that had happened, some small unfair thing involving a teacher and a grade and a boy called Ryan Tulley who had gotten away with it, and it had been sitting in his chest for six hours, and he had walked into that kitchen fully intending to say nothing about it, because saying things about it was not a service he had ever found available.

And Deb had been at the counter doing something with a chicken, and had said, without turning around, “You want to talk about whatever that is or you want me to leave it?”

And he had said, “Leave it.”

And she had said, “Okay,” and left it.

That was the entire event: a woman asking a question with two real answers in it and then honoring the one she got. He had gone upstairs and done his homework and come down at seven and eaten the chicken and told her about it a week later in a car, and she had been appropriately outraged about Ryan Tulley, and it had been fine.

But he had thought about that Tuesday more than any other single day in the house, because it was the first time in his life an adult had offered him a genuine no.

Every adult offers you a yes. *You can tell me anything.* That is what they say, and they mean it, and what it means in practice is that not telling them becomes a thing you are doing to them.

She’d offered him the other one. And she’d kept offering it, and never once made the no cost anything.

So on the Wednesday, when he could see plainly that she was still not sleeping, fight or no fight, and he knew exactly why, and when he had four different good openings available to him and could have used any of them, he made a decision he was not able to fully articulate to himself until much later, which was that he was not going to be the first person in that house to take the no off the table.

He cooked instead.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

It was a disaster of a particular kind, which is the kind where nothing actually goes wrong.

He made the chicken thing with the lemon, because it was the one he’d watched her make maybe two hundred times, and he had the sequence right, he had the pan hot and the chicken dry and the lemon in at the end and not the beginning, which he knew because she’d told him in about 2022 that putting the lemon in early was how you got a sour, gray, apologetic dinner.

He did everything right and it came out wrong.

It wasn’t bad. That was the maddening part. It was fine. It was the correct food, and it tasted like a thing a competent person had made, and it did not taste even slightly like hers.

He was standing over the pan at six-fifteen with a fork, tasting it for the fourth time and getting quietly furious, when the door did its stick-and-give.

Deb came in and stopped in the doorway.

“What’s happening.”

“I made the chicken.”

“You made the chicken.”

“It’s wrong,” Eli said, with real heat. “I did it exactly right. I had the pan at the right heat, I dried it, the lemon went in at the end, I did the whole thing, and it’s wrong, and I’ve been standing here for ten minutes trying to work out what’s missing and I’ve got nothing.”

Deb put her bag down slowly, the way you’d approach a horse.

“Can I taste it?”

“That’s why it’s still in the pan.”

She got a fork. Tasted it.

She chewed for what felt to Eli like an excessive amount of time.

“It’s good,” she said.

“It’s not the same.”

“No, it’s not the same.”

“So what did I do?”

“Nothing.” She put the fork in the sink. “There’s nothing wrong with it, Eli. It’s a good pan of chicken.”

“Deb.”

“You want the real answer?”

“Yes.”

“You didn’t put in enough salt, and you didn’t scrape the pan when you put the lemon in, so all the brown bits are still stuck on the bottom instead of in the sauce, which is where about forty percent of the flavor of that dish lives.” She turned the tap on and started washing her hands. “That’s it. It’s two mistakes and they’re both mistakes I made until I was about twenty-three.”

Eli stared at the pan.

“The brown bits.”

“The brown bits.”

“There’s brown bits stuck to the bottom of the pan and you’re supposed to get them off.”

“With the lemon juice. It’s the whole reason the lemon goes in at that moment, that’s the entire mechanism, I’ve never once said that out loud because I’ve been doing it since 1994 and it stopped being a step.” She dried her hands. “Which is my fault. That’s a thing I should have told you.”

“How would you know to tell me?”

“Because you asked me, in about November, how I make it,” Deb said, “and I said ‘you just make it,’ which is the least useful sentence in the English language and I heard it come out of my own mouth.”

They ate it. It was good, and it wasn’t hers, and both of them ate two helpings of it anyway.

Halfway through, Eli said, “So write it down.”

“Write what down?”

“The chicken. Actually write it. Not ‘you just make it.’ The brown bits, the salt, all of it.” He was looking at his plate. “And the chili, and the soup with the pasta, and the ziti.”

Deb put her fork down.

“Eli.”

“I’m not doing a thing,” he said. “I’m going to have a kitchen soon and I’m going to be extremely bad at it and I’d rather be bad at it with instructions.”

The kitchen was very quiet for a second.

“Okay,” Deb said.

“Okay?”

“I’ll write them down.”

“Not, like. A big project.” He was rearranging the food on his plate now. “Just the ones. Like six of them. Whatever.”

“Six of them,” Deb agreed.

“And I don’t want you making a whole…” He stopped. Started again. “If you make it into a whole thing I’m going to feel weird about it.”

“I’m not going to make it into a whole thing,” Deb said. “I’m going to write six recipes on index cards and put a rubber band around them and put them on the counter, and if you take them, you take them, and if you leave them I’ll use them myself because I don’t currently own a written recipe for anything I cook.”

Eli nodded at his plate.

“Okay,” he said. “That’s fine. That’s a normal amount.”

“That’s a normal amount,” Deb agreed.

“How many is six,” Eli said, at the sink, drying.

“What?”

“You said six. Which six?”

“I don’t know yet.”

“You do know. You’ve known since I said it, you’ve been standing there doing them in your head for four minutes.”

Deb handed him the pan.

“The chicken,” she said. “The chili. The soup with the pasta. The ziti.”

“That’s four.”

“The enchiladas, which don’t work, with a note about why.”

“Why would you write down the one that doesn’t work?”

“Because you’ll try it,” Deb said, “and it’ll go wrong the same way mine does, and I’d rather you knew in advance that it’s the recipe and not you.”

Eli dried the pan.

“That’s the best one,” he said. “Of the six. That’s the one that’s actually worth something.”

“It’s a bad recipe with a warning on it.”

“Yeah,” he said. “I know.”

He put the pan in the cupboard, in the right place, without looking, which he had been able to do for about three years.

“What’s the sixth?”

“The coffee cake.”

“Why the coffee cake?”

Deb wiped down the counter.

“Because you’ll want to bring something to somebody at some point,” she said, “and you’ll be twenty-two and you’ll have no idea what people bring, and the answer is a coffee cake, and it takes forty minutes and costs about four dollars and nobody has ever once been unhappy to receive one.”

Eli stood there with the towel.

“That’s a genuinely useful piece of information,” he said.

“I’m full of them. Nobody asks.”

“Yeah,” he said. “Well. I’m asking now, apparently.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She wrote them that night, at the table, after he’d gone up. It took her an hour and ten minutes because it turned out she did not know how much salt she used, in any dish, at all, and had to reconstruct it by standing at the counter with the box and doing the motion with her hand into her palm and then measuring what was in her palm.

Two teaspoons. Her whole life, in everything, two teaspoons, and she’d have sworn under oath it was one.

She did the chicken, the chili, the soup with the little pasta, the ziti, the enchiladas that didn’t work with a note explaining why they didn’t work, and the coffee cake.

Six.

She put a rubber band around them and set them on the counter by the microwave, next to the pool catalogue and a five-dollar bill.

Then she stood there and looked at them for a minute, and thought about the box of dishes under a towel in the basement, and about a woman two doors down who had cooked for four every night since 1994, and went up to bed.


# Chapter 17

[T]{.dropcap}he voicemail came in on a Thursday afternoon while Deb was in the walk-in checking the dairy, and she didn’t see it until four-ten, standing in the parking lot with her keys in her hand.

*Danny Hale (1)*

She looked at it for a while.

The name had been sitting in her phone for six years, under D, with a photograph attached to it from 2014 in which he was holding a fish. She had never considered deleting it and had never looked at it on purpose.

She got in the car. Sat. Put the key in and didn’t turn it.

Then she played it.

*Hey. Uh. It’s Danny.* A pause with traffic in it. *Obviously. Sorry, that’s dumb, you’ve got my number.* Another pause. *So, look, I’m not calling about anything. Nothing’s wrong. Nobody’s sick, I want to say that first because I know that’s where you’ll go.* Somebody honked, far off. *I’ve just been thinking about calling for a while and I got tired of thinking about it. That’s the whole reason. So. Yeah. Call me back or don’t, that’s fine either way, I mean it, I’m not doing a thing here.* A breath. *Okay. Bye.*

Fifty-one seconds.

Deb sat in the parking lot of Deerbrook Falls Middle School with her hands on the wheel at ten past four in the afternoon and looked at the crack in the windshield, which ran up from the lower left and had gotten longer again.

She played it a second time.

The third time she was listening for something specific and she found it, right at the end, in the *okay, bye*, which was a thing he’d done since he was nine years old: the slight upward turn on the *okay*, the way you’d sign off with somebody you were nervous about.

She put the phone face down on the passenger seat and drove home.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The photograph in her phone was from 2014. She looked at that too, in the car, in the school parking lot, which was a thing she had not done in six years.

Danny at eighteen, holding a bass by the mouth on a dock at Indian Lake, squinting the way he squinted, in a shirt she remembered buying. Frank had taken it. That was the thing about the photograph she had never resolved and which was the reason it had stayed as his contact picture through an entire estrangement: it was a good photograph of her son taken by a man she had thrown out of the house four years later, and there was no way to keep the first fact without the second one coming along.

She had looked for a replacement in about 2016 and hadn’t found one, because there weren’t many photographs of Danny after nineteen, because he had become a person who turned away from cameras, which is a thing that happens to boys around then and which nobody thinks to record.

She put the phone in the cupholder.

Then she picked it up again and looked at the picture for another minute, and then put it down for good and drove home.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She did not call him back that day.

She made the fish thing. Eli was on for Thursday, one of the three, and he came in at six-thirty and ate and told her about a customer who had tried to return a battery he had clearly bought at a different store, in a different state, in 2019, and Deb laughed in the right places and asked the right follow-up questions and did not hear a single word of it.

At eight-forty she was drying a pan that was already dry.

“Okay,” Eli said, from the table.

“What.”

“You’ve done that pan since I sat down.”

“It’s a big pan.”

“It’s a saucepan.” He closed the laptop halfway, which she recognized, distantly, as something Mrs. Kadeem apparently did. “You’ve checked your phone six times.”

“You counted?”

“I count things,” Eli said, which was the first time in three and a half years he had ever said that out loud, and he said it in exactly the tone you’d use to say you took the bus.

Deb put the pan down.

“My son called,” she said.

Eli didn’t say anything for a second.

“Danny,” he said.

“You know his name.”

“Deb, there’s a picture of him on the stairs and you’ve never said one word about him in three years. Obviously I know his name.”

She hadn’t known that. She’d assumed the photograph had gone the way things go, into the wallpaper of a house, unseen after the first month.

“He left a voicemail,” she said. “Fifty-one seconds. He said nothing’s wrong and nobody’s sick and he’s not doing a thing, and then he said call me back or don’t.”

“Are you going to call him back?”

“I don’t know.”

“Okay,” Eli said, and went back to his laptop, and that was the entire conversation.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

That was Thursday.

Thursday night she got as far as the contact screen once, at eleven-fifteen, lying in bed, and looked at a photograph of a boy holding a fish, and put the phone face down on the nightstand hard enough to make a noise.

And then lay there and had the argument.

The argument had two sides and she had been having it, in fragments, since four-ten that afternoon, and it went like this.

One side said: he called you. That’s the entire event. A man who has not spoken to you in six years picked up a telephone, and the ball is not in your court, the ball is on the ground, and all you have to do is bend down.

The other side said: and then what.

That was the whole of the second side and it was undefeated. *And then what.* Because there was no version of the next ten minutes that Deb could construct in which she did not, at some point, have to be a person who had said what she said, out loud, to the person she said it to. And she had been rehearsing not being that person for six years with such success that she had almost managed to believe the whole thing had been a misunderstanding about money.

At one-forty in the morning she thought, quite clearly: *if I call him back, I have to be the one who did it.*

At one-forty-one she thought: *and if I don’t, I’m the one who didn’t, and that’s worse, and it’s been worse for six years.*

She fell asleep at some point after two and woke at five-forty and lay there and did not get up, and that was the Friday.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Friday she did not call him back.

She thought about it approximately every few minutes for fourteen hours. She composed four different opening sentences while doing four hundred and eight trays. She rejected all four. She got as far as the contact screen twice, once at eleven in the morning and once at nine at night, and both times put the phone in a drawer, physically, in a drawer, like a woman confiscating something from herself.

At ten-forty on Friday night Eli came in from a shift and found her sitting at the kitchen table with the phone in front of her, face up, not touching it.

He got a glass of water. Drank it standing at the sink.

Then he said, without turning around, “You want the store version?”

“The what?”

“There’s a thing Gary does. When somebody calls the store and it’s going to be a bad call, a warranty thing, somebody’s engine, whatever.” He put the glass in the rack. “He does it standing up. He won’t take a hard call sitting down, he stands up first, every time. I asked him about it once and he said sitting down is for calls where you already know how it goes.”

Deb looked at the phone.

“That’s not a real technique,” she said. “That’s a superstition.”

“Yeah, probably.” He picked up his bag. “I’m going up. There’s four pancakes’ worth of leftover ziti in there if you want to eat like a person.”

He went up.

Deb sat there another six minutes.

Then she stood up.

She stood up in her own kitchen at ten past eleven on a Friday night, and she did not call him, not that night either, but she noted, with some distant part of herself, that standing up was in fact different from sitting down, and that a seventeen-year-old had just handed her the only piece of usable advice she’d received in six years, disguised as a story about a man in an auto parts store.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Saturday morning she was up at five-forty out of habit and the house was hers for three hours before anything could be expected of her.

She did the things a person does. She made the coffee. She sat with it. At six-forty she got the photograph off the stairs, which she had walked past approximately forty thousand times and had not held in her hands since she hung it there.

It was Danny at nineteen, on the porch of that house, in a Bengals shirt, squinting because the sun had been behind her when she took it. He had a bad haircut he had been extremely proud of. He was holding a garden hose for reasons entirely lost to history.

She sat on the third stair with it.

The thing about a photograph on a wall is that it becomes wall. She’d known this in the abstract and she experienced it now specifically: that she had put this frame up in 2016 and had not seen it since about 2017, that it had become an architectural feature, and that Eli Vance had walked past it every day for three and a half years and had looked at it more recently than she had.

*There’s a picture of him on the stairs and you’ve never said one word about him in three years.*

She turned it over. There was nothing on the back. She had never been a woman who wrote on the backs of photographs and had always intended to become one.

At seven she put it back on its nail and straightened it, and then went and cleaned the bathroom, which did not need it, for forty minutes.

At eight she ate a piece of toast standing up.

At nine she got out her phone and did nothing with it for fifteen minutes.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She called him Saturday morning at nine-fifteen, standing in the kitchen, with her free hand flat on the counter.

He picked up on the fourth ring.

“Mom?”

And there it was. Six years, and the whole thing came apart in one syllable, the way it does, and Deb Hale put her hand over her mouth for about two seconds and then took it away and said, in a perfectly ordinary voice:

“Hi, Danny.”


# Chapter 18

[T]{.dropcap}he call lasted nine minutes and was, by any reasonable measure, terrible.

They talked about the weather in Columbus, which was the same weather as in Deerbrook Falls, a fact both of them acknowledged and then discussed anyway for close to ninety seconds. They talked about his job. He was still at the HVAC company, he’d been there four years now, he was doing installs and some service, and they’d made him a lead in the fall, which he mentioned and then immediately downplayed in a way that Deb recognized so completely it hurt her teeth.

“That’s a big deal, Danny.”

“It’s not really. It’s a title. It means I do the schedule.”

“That’s what a lead is. That’s the job.”

“Yeah, well.”

Twice there were pauses long enough that she thought the call had dropped.

At about the seven-minute mark he said, “So how’s, uh. How’s things there.”

“Fine. Good. Busy.”

“You still at the school?”

“Twenty years in August.”

“Right.” A pause. “Right, yeah.”

And that was where it should have ended, and both of them clearly felt it, and neither of them hung up.

Then Danny said, fast, like a man going through a door before it shuts:

“Do you want to get lunch or something. Not, like. I’m not saying, I don’t have some agenda. Just lunch. I’m in Columbus, you’re an hour and forty, we could do halfway or I could come up or you could come down, whatever’s easiest, or not at all, honestly, no pressure, I just figured I’d ask while I had you.”

Deb stood in the kitchen with her hand flat on the counter.

Every reason not to arrived at once and stood in a line. She had thirteen days with Eli. She had a hearing on April third. She had a house that needed the gutters done and a windshield with a crack in it and she was fifty-four years old and she had not been able to make herself dial a phone for two full days.

“Yes,” she said.

“Yeah?”

“Yes. Lunch.”

“Okay.” He sounded winded. “Okay, good, that’s. Okay. When’s good?”

“When’s good for you?”

“Sunday?”

“This Sunday?”

“Or not this Sunday.”

“This Sunday’s fine,” Deb said, and heard herself say it, and thought, *the twenty-second, that’s the twenty-second, that’s twelve days.*

“I’ll come up,” Danny said. “There’s a place on Route 4, the diner, the one with the… it’s still there, right? By the tire place?”

“Bergstrom’s.”

“Bergstrom’s. Is it still any good?”

“It was never any good.”

“Right,” Danny said, and laughed, one short breath, and something in Deb’s chest turned over, because that was the family joke, that was a joke from 2004, and he had reached for it without thinking about it, the way you reach for a light switch in a room you grew up in.

“Twelve-thirty,” she said.

“Twelve-thirty. Okay. I’ll be there.”

“Danny.”

“Yeah?”

She had about four things and no idea which one to use.

“Drive safe,” she said.

“Yeah,” he said. “Okay. Bye, Mom.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

There was a period of about ninety seconds after the call where she did not move at all.

The tap was dripping. It had been dripping since Christmas and she had a washer in the drawer and had not done it, and she stood in her kitchen at nine twenty-four on a Saturday morning listening to it and doing absolutely nothing.

*Twelve-thirty. Bergstrom’s.*

She had said yes in under two seconds. That was the part she went back to. Two days of not being able to dial a phone, and then a man asks her to lunch and she says yes before he finishes the sentence, which meant the two days had never been about whether. The two days had been about the four seconds after he picked up, and she had been right to be afraid of them, because *Mom?* had gone through her like a nail.

She got the washer out of the drawer and fixed the tap. It took ten minutes and required going down to the basement twice, and when she was finished the kitchen was silent in a way it had not been since December, and she stood in the middle of it with a wrench in her hand feeling completely deranged.

Then she made a coffee cake, and got about four minutes into making a coffee cake before she stopped, put the bowl down, and said out loud, to nobody, “Absolutely not,” and put the flour back in the cupboard.

That was, she would tell Marisol later, the single most impressive act of self-control of her adult life.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She told Eli about the tap before she told him about the lunch, which was, she recognized afterward, an act of pure cowardice dressed up as chronology.

“You fixed the tap.”

“I fixed the tap.”

“That tap’s been dripping since Christmas.”

“I’m aware of how long the tap’s been dripping.”

He went over and turned it on and off, twice, with a look on his face she had come to think of as his inspection face.

“Where was the washer?”

“In the drawer. Where it’s been since December.”

“You’ve had the washer in the drawer since December,” Eli said, “and the tap’s been dripping since December, and this morning you did it in ten minutes.”

“Yes.”

“Why this morning?”

Deb wiped a counter.

“Because I had a phone call,” she said, “and afterward I needed to do something with my hands, and the tap was there.”

Eli dried his hands on the towel and hung it back on the bar, square, the way he did.

“That’s a good reason,” he said.

“It’s not a reason at all.”

“It’s a better reason than most of the reasons things get fixed,” said Eli, and got the plate out of the fridge, and did not ask about the phone call, and Deb stood there with a cloth in her hand and understood, not for the first time, that she was being given room by a seventeen-year-old and that she had done nothing whatsoever to earn it.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Marisol had known since Thursday, because Deb had texted her from the school lot at twenty past four, and her response, delivered Friday in the walk-in, was not what Deb expected.

“You haven’t called him.”

“I haven’t called him.”

“Deb.” Marisol had a case of frozen corn in her arms and did not put it down. “The hearing’s the third.”

“I know when the hearing is.”

“And the boy’s gone by then, one way or the other.” She shifted the corn. “So you’ve got about two weeks of him left and you’re about to spend them on a man you haven’t seen since 2019.”

“That’s not fair.”

“It’s completely fair, it’s just not kind.” She put the corn down finally. “I’m not saying don’t call him. You’re going to hear this wrong for about a day, so listen. Call him. You should absolutely call him, I’ve been telling you to for six years. I’m saying I’ve watched you across that table for eleven years and every time something big is about to happen to you, you find a second big thing to go stand in front of.”

Deb crossed her arms in the cold.

“He called me,” she said. “I didn’t do anything. He called me.”

“He called you Thursday,” Marisol said. “Two weeks before the worst day you’ve had since the divorce. That’s not you doing anything, that’s timing, and it’s rotten timing, and it’s also the first time in six years, so what are you supposed to do, tell him April’s bad for you?”

“That’s what I keep landing on.”

“Right. Because there isn’t an answer. That’s what I’m saying to you.” Marisol picked the corn back up. “There’s no clever version. You’re going to call that boy and you’re going to sit somewhere terrible with him and it’s going to be hard, and then you’re going to come home and it’s still going to be two weeks, and both of those things are just true at the same time. I’m not solving it. I’m putting a hand on it now so you don’t think you’re crazy afterward.”

Deb looked at her for a second.

“Sometimes you’re all right,” she said.

“I’m magnificent. Get the door, I’m carrying corn.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The thing about a call you have not made yet is that the two days in front of it still have to be got through.

Deb went to work. She ran four hundred and twelve trays on the Thursday and four hundred and eight on the Friday because of a field trip. She did the laundry. She paid the water bill. On the Friday evening she took her car to have the oil done and did not mention the windshield, and the kid at the counter mentioned the windshield, and she said she knew, and he said they could do it in about forty minutes, and she said maybe next time.

On the Friday morning the district sent a man to look at the walk-in compressor. He stood in front of it for nine minutes and told her it had four years left in it, which she had been told in January, and in 2019, about the same compressor.

She thought about Danny for approximately four hours a day, in pieces, at odd moments, mostly while doing something with her hands.

She thought about Eli approximately continuously, which was different, and which did not feel like thinking so much as it felt like weather.

And on the Friday afternoon, Renee came for the last routine home visit, which neither Deb nor Eli had thought to identify as the last one until Renee said, at the door, coat on, “That’s my last one of these, by the way. The next time I’m in this house it’ll be about paperwork.”

Deb stopped with her hand on the door.

“That’s today?”

“That was today. About twenty minutes ago. It was the same as all of them and I asked the same three questions and he gave the same answers.” Renee was looking for her keys. “I didn’t say anything at the time because if I’d said it at the table it would have become a scene, and he’d have hated that, and honestly so would I.”

“Renee.”

“Forty-seven home visits,” Renee said. “I looked it up this morning, which was a mistake, and I did it in a parking lot before I came in so I’d have it out of my system.”

She found the keys.

“Forty-seven,” Deb said.

“Give or take. There was a stretch in 2022 where I came twice a month because of the school thing.” She got the door open. “Anyway. That’s what I wanted to say, and I’ve now said it standing in a doorway, which is where every real thing in this job gets said, which is something they don’t tell you at the training.”

“Is the van going to make it?”

“The van has a new battery,” Renee said, with enormous dignity, “purchased at that store, at cost, by a young man who did the math for me on a piece of scrap paper and then would not accept a tip, and I have made my peace with all of it.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

It came back up at eleven on the Saturday night, at the table, which had become the hour at which things got said in that house.

“So it’s tomorrow,” Eli said.

“It’s tomorrow.”

He looked up from the laptop.

“Good,” he said.

“That’s it?”

“What do you want me to say?”

“I don’t know. Something.”

Eli considered it, actually considered it, in that way he had.

“You stood up,” he said.

“What?”

“When you called him. Did you stand up?”

Deb sat back in her chair.

“You are an extremely strange young man,” she said.

“That’s a yes.”

“That’s a yes.”

“Then that’s what I’d say,” Eli said, and went back to his laptop, and after about ten seconds added, not looking up, “Bergstrom’s is bad, though. Genuinely. Their coffee’s worse than the courthouse.”

“Everybody knows Bergstrom’s is bad. That’s not information.”

“I’m just saying, if you’re going to have a hard lunch, have it somewhere with edible pie.”

“Where would you have it?”

“The place on Chestnut with the counter. Behind the bank.”

“That’s a gas station, Eli.”

“It’s a gas station with a counter,” he said, “and their pie comes from a woman in Xenia who does it out of her house, and it is the best pie in this county, and every single person who works at that store knows it and nobody talks about it because we don’t want a line.”

Deb stared at him.

“There’s a secret pie situation,” she said, “at a gas station, on Chestnut, and you have known about it for how long?”

“About two years.”

“You’ve been sitting on a secret pie for two years.”

“You never asked me about pie,” Eli said.

“Who asks somebody about pie? That’s not a thing you ask a person. That’s a thing a person volunteers.”

“Well,” he said, “now you know,” and Deb laughed so hard she had to put her forehead on the table, and it was eleven-fifteen at night, and there were thirteen days left.


# Chapter 19

[S]{.dropcap}he was up at five-twenty on the Sunday and had seven hours to fill before a twelve-thirty lunch ten minutes from her house.

She did the laundry. She ironed a blouse and then decided against the blouse. She changed twice, which she had not done since a job interview in 2011, and ended up in the second thing she had tried on, which is how that always goes.

At nine-forty Eli came down and found her sitting at the kitchen table fully dressed with a cup of coffee and the newspaper she did not read.

“You’re going at twelve-thirty.”

“I’m aware of when I’m going.”

“It’s nine-forty.”

“I’m aware of what time it is, Eli.”

He got a bowl down and poured cereal and ate it standing up, and did not say anything else about it, and then, at the door with his keys, said, “The place on Chestnut does a coconut cream too. Everybody gets the coconut. Don’t get the coconut.”

“You’ve said.”

“I know I’ve said. I’m saying it again because you’re going to be in a state on the way home and you’ll default to coconut.” He got the door open. “Butterscotch. It’s not close.”

And he went, and Deb sat in her kitchen at nine-forty-five on a Sunday morning in a blouse she had changed into twice, having just been given pie instructions by a seventeen-year-old, and found she was in slightly better shape than she had been ten minutes earlier.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Bergstrom’s had not changed, which was the first thing, and which Deb had counted on without knowing she was counting on it.

Same brown vinyl booths with the split in the third one down that had been repaired with tape in approximately 2009. Same laminated menu with the photograph of a club sandwich that no longer bore any relationship to the club sandwich they served. Same handwritten sign by the register about the restroom being for customers, in a hand that had been getting shakier for two decades.

She got there at twelve-eighteen. She’d meant to get there at twelve-twenty-nine.

Danny came in at twelve-thirty exactly.

She saw him through the window first, crossing the lot, and had about four seconds to absorb it before he was inside. He was heavier. That was the first thing, and it was not a bad thing, it suited him, he’d been rangy and unfinished at twenty-three and now he was a man with shoulders who worked with his hands. He had a beard.
