She had not known about the beard. There was gray in it, at twenty-nine, on the left side of his chin, exactly where Frank’s had come in.

He saw her and did a small lift of the hand and she thought, absurdly, *he’s nervous about the walk from the door to the table*, because she was nervous about watching him do it.

“Hey,” he said.

“Hi.”

He sat. There was a moment where neither of them knew whether they had hugged, which they had not, and the moment passed and was gone and would not be available again.

“You look good,” he said.

“I look like a woman who ran a pancake supper nine days ago.”

“That’s still going?”

“That’s still going. Craig from the PTO tried to tell me my batter was thin.”

“Which Craig?”

“Quarter-zip Craig.”

“Oh, I know exactly who that is,” Danny said, “and he was a jackass in 2008 and I refuse to believe he’s improved,” and something in the booth loosened by about ten percent.

They ordered. The waitress was new, or new to Deb, maybe forty, and she called them both hon and did not linger.

They talked about his job for six or seven minutes. It was good talk, actual talk, he explained a thing about heat pumps and why everybody in Ohio was suddenly getting them and why half of those installs were being done wrong by people who didn’t size them properly, and he got animated about it, hands going, and Deb sat there and watched her son be competent about something and could have wept in a diner over a heat pump.

Then the food came, and there was a lull, and Danny put his fork down and said, “Okay. So I want to do the thing before I lose my nerve.”

Deb put her hands in her lap.

“Okay.”

“I’m not here to have a fight about 2019.”

“All right.”

“I mean that, I really do, I’ve had that fight about nine hundred times in my truck and it’s boring now.” He turned his water glass. “But I can’t sit here and do six years of catching up like it’s normal, either, so I’m going to say my piece about it once, and then I want to eat this extremely bad sandwich and hear about your life.”

“Say it.”

He looked at the table.

“You said I was going to end up like Dad,” he said.

Deb did not move.

“That’s the entire six years,” Danny said. “In case you’ve been wondering, and I figured you probably have. You said it in the kitchen. October, 2019. I’d asked you for four thousand dollars for the truck and you said no, which, fine, that’s fine, I’ve since worked out you didn’t have four thousand dollars, which I did not know at the time because you don’t tell anybody anything about money, ever, and that’s its own thing.”

“Danny.”

“Let me finish, I’ve got about nine seconds of momentum left.” He took a breath. “You said no, and I said something about Casey, that we were trying to get the deposit together, and you said, and I’ve got this word for word, you said, ‘You’re going to end up exactly like your father, chasing something with somebody else’s money and calling it a plan.’”

The diner made its diner noises. Somebody’s plate went down at the counter.

“And here’s the thing,” Danny said. “You were right about Casey.”

Deb looked up.

“You were completely right about Casey. She was gone by February. She took the deposit, which was mostly her mother’s money anyway, and she went to Florida with a guy from the gym, and I found out about it from her sister on Facebook.” He laughed, no humor in it at all. “So congratulations, I guess. You called it in October.”

“That’s not what I…”

“I know it’s not what you wanted.” He rubbed his face. “That’s not the part. The part is that you didn’t say ‘I don’t think she’s good for you,’ or ‘I don’t have it,’ or ‘I’m worried.’ You said I was going to end up like him. And I’ve been listening to you in that kitchen my whole life, Mom, I know what that sentence means in that house. That’s not a warning. That’s a verdict.”

Deb sat very still in a brown vinyl booth.

Everything in her wanted to explain. She could feel the explanation assembling itself, fully formed, with evidence: that she’d been eleven months out from Frank cleaning out the joint account, that she’d been working doubles, that she’d been terrified in a specific way about her son and a woman she’d met three times, that she had not slept properly in a year.

All of it was true. Every word of it was true and every word of it was, she understood with total clarity sitting in that booth, entirely beside the point.

“I did say that,” she said.

Danny’s head came up slightly.

“I’ve thought about it,” Deb said. “I want you to know that’s not me conceding a point. I’ve thought about that sentence for six years and I know exactly what I said and exactly how I said it, and there’s no version where it lands differently than it landed.”

“Okay.”

“And I’m not going to sit here and tell you why I said it, because I’ve got about nine reasons and every one of them is true and not one of them is an answer.”

Danny looked at her for a long moment.

“That’s more than I expected,” he said.

“Do you want me to apologize?”

And here he did something that surprised her completely: he thought about it. He actually sat and thought about it, for a solid five or six seconds, the way a man weighs a thing he’s been carrying and wants to put down correctly.

“Not today,” he said.

“Okay.”

“Not because I’m punishing you. Because if you do it now I’ll say it’s fine, and it isn’t fine, and then we’ll have used it up.” He picked his fork back up. “I’ve watched people do that. Somebody apologizes at a lunch and everybody feels good in the parking lot and then it’s gone and nothing’s different in six months. I’d rather have it later, when it’s not the main event.”

Deb sat with that.

“That’s very grown up of you,” she said.

“I’m twenty-nine, Mom.”

“I’m aware. It’s been mentioned.”

He almost smiled. Then he said, “Also, I’ve got a therapist, so, some of this is not organic,” and Deb laughed out loud in Bergstrom’s, one loud unlovely bark of a laugh, and the new waitress looked over.

“Since when?”

“Since about four months after Casey. It was that or become a guy who talks about his ex in a bar.” He shrugged. “It’s expensive and I’m bad at it.”

“You called me.”

“Yeah, well.” Danny picked up the sandwich. “She’s been on me about it for two years. So I want partial credit at best.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

“Can I ask you something about him,” Deb said. “About Frank.”

Danny’s eyebrows went up.

“That’s a swerve.”

“I’ve been thinking about it since you sat down and I’d rather ask it than think about it for another six years.”

“Go ahead.”

“Do you hear from him?”

Danny turned his water glass.

“Twice a year,” he said. “Birthday and Christmas. Text message. Same message, roughly. He’s in Florida, he’s got a girlfriend called Denise, I’ve never met her, they’ve got a boat that I’ve seen fourteen photographs of.”

Deb absorbed that.

“Twice a year.”

“Yeah.”

“Since when?”

“Since he moved out. Since 2018.” He shrugged. “It’s not a big thing, Mom. He’s not, like, hiding it. I assumed you knew.”

“I didn’t know.”

“Would you have wanted to?”

Deb thought about that honestly, which took a second.

“No,” she said. “I don’t think I would have.”

“Right.” He drank some water. “That’s why I never said.”

And there was a whole conversation available in that, and both of them looked at it, and neither of them opened it, and Deb understood that this was going to be one of about forty such doors they would walk past over the next few years, and that walking past them was not the same as pretending they weren’t there.

“Is he all right?” she asked. “That’s the last one, then I’ll stop.”

“He’s fine. He’s exactly who he was, except older and warmer, which is a thing that happens to men like that and it’s annoying.” Danny picked up half a sandwich. “He asked about you in 2021. Once. He said ‘how’s your mother,’ and I said ‘she’s good,’ and that was it. And I want to be honest, I said ‘she’s good’ and I hadn’t spoken to you in two years.”

Deb looked at her plate.

“Why’d you say that?”

“Because I wasn’t going to tell him,” Danny said. “That’s the whole reason. There was no version where I gave that man the information.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

They ate the extremely bad sandwiches.

And then something happened that Deb had not planned for at all, which was that the rest of the lunch was easy.

He asked about the school. He asked about Marisol, by name, remembering her, which floored Deb, and asked whether her sister ever got married again, which she had, twice. He told a story about a customer’s basement that involved a snake. He asked about the Escape and Deb mentioned the windshield and he said, “You still haven’t fixed that? That was cracked at Thanksgiving,” and then both of them stopped, because Thanksgiving meant Thanksgiving 2018, and the crack was seven years old, and the crack was older than the estrangement.

“It’s spread,” Deb said.

“Yeah, they do. Get it done, it’s like ninety bucks, insurance usually covers the whole thing.”

“I know.”

“So why haven’t you?”

“Danny, I don’t know,” Deb said. “I’ve got a list.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

“Can I say a thing about the four thousand dollars,” Deb said.

Danny put his sandwich down.

“I thought we were done with 2019.”

“We are. This isn’t about the sentence, it’s about the money, which is a different thing and it’ll take ninety seconds.”

“Go ahead.”

“I didn’t have it,” she said. “That’s the whole content. I had twelve hundred dollars in a savings account and a furnace that was making a noise, and your father had taken what was in the joint account in the November, which I never told you about, because you were twenty-three and there was nothing you could have done with the information except carry it.”

Danny sat very still.

“How much was in the joint account?”

“That’s not the point.”

“Mom.”

“Nine thousand,” Deb said. “It was nine thousand and it was most of what I had, and he was entitled to half of it and he took all of it, and I decided in about four days that I was not going to spend two years and fourteen thousand dollars in lawyer’s fees to get four and a half back, and I have never once been sure that was the right call.”

The diner made its noises.

“You never said.”

“No.”

“For six years I’ve thought you just wouldn’t give me the money.”

“I know.”

“That’s…” He rubbed the side of his face. “Mom, that’s a completely different situation. I sat in my truck for two years being angry that my mother had four thousand dollars and wouldn’t.”

“I know.”

“Why didn’t you say?”

And here it was, and Deb made herself say the true one instead of the good one.

“Because I was ashamed,” she said. “Not of him. Of being a forty-eight-year-old woman who let a man walk out of a bank with nine thousand dollars and decided it was cheaper to let him. And I would rather my son thought I was withholding four thousand dollars than know I didn’t have it, and that is an appalling thing to have chosen, and I chose it in about a second and a half in a kitchen and I’ve had six years to look at it.”

Danny didn’t say anything for a while.

“Yeah,” he said finally. “Okay. That’s…” He shook his head. “That’s actually worse than what I thought happened. I want you to know that. That’s worse.”

“I know it is.”

“But it’s also, like.” He picked the sandwich back up and put it down again. “It’s a reason. What I’ve had for six years is no reason. I’d rather have a bad reason than no reason.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

At one-forty he looked at his phone and said he should get back, and she said of course, and they stood, and the moment came around again, and this time he hugged her.

It was brief and slightly wrong, the way a first one is, and it was a hug from a man built like a man, and she got her arms up in time and got a hand on the back of his head the way you do to a child, which he was not, and which she did anyway, and neither of them said anything about it.

In the lot he said, “So. Can I call you?”

“Yes.”

“Like, on a Tuesday. Not, like, a thing. Just call.”

“Yes.”

“Okay.” He got his keys out. “It doesn’t have to be a whole…”

“Danny,” Deb said. “Call me on a Tuesday.”

“Okay.”

He got in the truck and backed out and lifted two fingers off the wheel on the way past, exactly like his father, and Deb stood in the parking lot of a bad diner on Route 4 at one forty-five in the afternoon with her keys in her hand and did not move for a while.


# Chapter 20

[S]{.dropcap}he sat in the Bergstrom’s lot for six minutes before she started the car.

The truck had gone left onto Route 4 and she had watched it go and had then stood there long enough that the woman coming out behind her had asked, kindly, whether she was all right, and Deb had said yes, thank you, and had gotten into her car.

What she felt, sitting there, was not what she had prepared for.

She had prepared for grief and had brought the equipment for it. What she had instead was a kind of vertigo, and it took most of those six minutes to identify, and when she identified it she did not like it at all.

It was relief.

Not that it had gone well, though it had. Relief that it was over, in the specific sense that the thing she had been dreading for four days had happened and could not happen again, and that whatever came next, she would never again have to sit in a car outside a diner not knowing what her son was going to say to her about 2019.

*You are relieved,* she thought, *that your son has finished telling you what you did to him.*

And underneath that, immediately, the correction, which was fairer and which she made herself take: *no. You’re relieved that he came.*

Both. As usual. Both, the whole time, in a parking lot, on a Sunday.

She started the car.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The drive home was ten minutes, which was not enough, so she drove the long way, out past the reservoir and around, which made it thirty-five.

The sky over that stretch was doing the thing it does in late March in Ohio, high and pale and moving, with the fields still flat brown and the treeline just barely going red at the tips, that first color that isn’t a color yet. Somebody’s dog was running a fence line. There was a hand-painted sign for eggs.

She had the radio off.

What she thought about, first, for about ten miles, was Casey. Not the girl herself, whom she’d met three times and had, if she was honest, disliked on sight for reasons she’d never had to examine because events had let her off the hook. What she thought about was standing in her kitchen in October of 2019 with a mug in her hand, saying a sentence.

She could see the mug. That was the horrible part. Six years and she could see which mug it was, the green one with the chip, and she could see where Danny had been standing, by the fridge, and she could hear the exact intonation she’d used, which had been tired and flat and final.

*You’re going to end up exactly like your father.*

She’d meant: *I’m frightened for you.*

She’d said: *I’ve already decided how you turn out.*

And her son, who was twenty-three and had asked his mother for four thousand dollars, had heard the second one, correctly, because that was the one she’d said.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Around the reservoir she started thinking about the timeline, and that was where it got bad.

October 2019: the kitchen. The sentence.

November 2019: he stopped calling. Not dramatically. He’d taken maybe three weeks to answer a text, and then a month, and then Christmas had been a phone call instead of a visit, and then the next Christmas had been a text.

And then.

Deb slowed down without meaning to, and a truck came up behind her and went around, and she put her hand up in apology and got a wave back.

*And then February 2020, she’d started the fostering classes.*

Not that year. That month. She had signed up for the ODJFS pre-service training four months after the sentence in the kitchen, and she had told everybody, including herself, including Marisol, including the woman who did her home study, a story about how she’d been thinking about it for years, and the empty rooms, and how she had a lot to give.

Which had all been true.

Which had also been, she understood on a state route outside Deerbrook Falls at two-fifteen on a Sunday afternoon, entirely beside the point.

She pulled into a gravel turnout by a field and put the car in park and sat there with the engine running.

Six years. Four kids. Six years of a thing she was good at, really good at, nobody could take that off her, four children who had been fed and driven and sat with and none of whom had been failed by her.

And underneath it, running the whole time, like a current under a boat: *if I can be irreplaceable to one of them, then the sentence in the kitchen wasn’t the end of anything, because look, look at all this, look how much I have to give, I am not a woman who says a thing like that in a kitchen.*

Marisol had been circling it for six years. *Getting somebody in the house before the sheets are cold is a different activity and it doesn’t have a name.*

It had a name. It just wasn’t a nice one.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

There was a woman she had not thought about in years and who arrived now, unbidden, on a state route: Pat Lindqvist, who had run the licensing training in the winter of 2020.

Six Saturdays in a conference room at the agency, nine people, bad chairs. Pat had been about sixty, had fostered for twenty-two years herself, and had opened the very first session by saying something Deb had written down in a notebook and had not looked at since.

*Most of you are here because of something. That’s fine. Almost everybody is. But you need to know what your something is before a child is in your house, because if you don’t, the child will find it, and the child will manage it for you, and that is not their job.*

Deb had written it down and had thought, at the time, that it was a good line and did not apply to her.

She sat in a gravel turnout at two-fifteen on a Sunday and understood that she had been told, plainly, in a conference room, in week one, by a woman with twenty-two years in it, and had heard it as advice for other people.

And that a fourteen-year-old boy had come into her house two and a half years later and had spent fourteen months looking for her something and had not found it, and had eventually concluded there wasn’t one.

Which meant one of two things. Either he was wrong and had simply not found it, or she had, somehow, without ever once naming it to herself, managed to keep it out of the room.

She sat with both possibilities for a while and could not settle it, and understood that she was probably not going to settle it, and that she was going to be checking her own kitchen for the shape of it for the rest of her life.

*That’s probably right,* she thought. *That’s probably what the job actually is.*

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She sat in the turnout for a while.

The thing she kept trying to do, and kept catching herself doing, was to make it a verdict. To turn it into a single clean sentence about what kind of woman she was, and file it, and carry it around. She was extremely good at that. She had a whole shelf of them.

But it didn’t fit.

Both things. The whole time. A woman doing an actual good thing for six years, for a reason she’d never once looked at directly, and the reason didn’t cancel the thing and the thing didn’t excuse the reason.

Marisol was going to be unbearable about this.

The thought arrived from nowhere and Deb laughed out loud, alone, in a car in a gravel turnout, one short honk of a laugh at the sheer predictable size of what Marisol was going to say when she heard about it.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She thought about the house, then, because she’d been avoiding it since the gym.

*Four for four. Plus Frank. Plus Danny.*

And she turned it over out there by the field and found, with some surprise, that it had gone flat. It didn’t have the same charge. It had been the loudest thought in her head for three months and now it sounded like what it was, which was a woman up at two in the morning doing arithmetic on herself.

Frank had left because Frank left; Marisol had been right about that in a gymnasium and Deb had known it was right at the time and had refused it out of some perverse loyalty to her own worst opinion of herself.

The four kids had left, or were leaving, because that is the entire and stated purpose of foster care, and if none of them had ever left she would have been an unimaginable failure at it.

And Danny had left because his mother had said a sentence to him in a kitchen in October 2019 and had never once, in six years, picked up a phone and taken it back.

That last one wasn’t the house. That was her, and it was one specific thing, on one specific night, and the difference between *there’s something wrong with this house* and *I said a thing and never fixed it* was the difference between a fog and a repair.

You cannot repair a fog. You can repair a sentence.

She put the car in drive.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

There was one more piece and it arrived four miles later, and it was the one she had been avoiding for three months without knowing she was avoiding it.

If she had started fostering in February of 2020 because of a sentence she said in October of 2019, then every kid who had come through that house had been standing in a room with a shape in it.

Malik. Alyssa. Jaylen. Eli.

She pulled over again. There was no turnout this time; she just stopped on the shoulder with the hazards going, which was not a safe thing to do on that road.

Four children. Six years. And underneath all of it, in a room they could not see and had never been told about, a woman conducting an argument with her own son.

She made herself go through it properly, one at a time, because the alternative was to let it stay a general accusation and general accusations were exactly what she had been feeding herself for three months.

Malik: seven months, age nine, and she had driven him to a speech therapist in Kettering forty times and had argued the agency into paying for it. That had been about Malik.

Alyssa: ten months, and the stomach bug, and the Pedialyte, and the day she had sat in a parking lot for twenty minutes so the girl could finish a conversation with her mother on the phone without an adult in the car. That had been about Alyssa.

Jaylen: eight months, and a school that wanted to move him down a track, and a woman who had gone in and sat in a chair and refused to leave until they didn’t. That had been about Jaylen.

Eli: three and a half years, and a boy who had spent fourteen months looking for the reason and had not found one.

She put her hands on the wheel.

*Both things,* she thought. *The whole time.*

It was possible for a woman to enter a room for a bad reason and then, once inside it, to be entirely present for the child in front of her. She could not make those two facts cancel and she was not going to be allowed to pick one. That was the actual finding, out on the shoulder of a state route with the hazards clicking, and it was not comfortable, and it was not the verdict she had been trying to write for three months, and it had the specific unglamorous texture of a thing that is simply true.

She turned the hazards off and pulled back onto the road.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She stopped at the gas station on Chestnut on the way home, went in past the coolers to the counter at the back, and there it was: a glass case with four pies in it and a handwritten card that said COCONUT CREAM and a woman at the register who said, “You want the coconut, everybody wants the coconut, but the butterscotch is what you want.”

Deb bought the butterscotch.

It was the best pie she had ever eaten in her life, and she ate a piece of it standing up at her own counter at four in the afternoon before she’d even taken her coat off, and when Eli came in at six he stopped in the kitchen doorway and looked at the box.

“You went.”

“I went.”

“And?”

“You were right,” Deb said. “About the pie. And I want to be clear that I’m only saying it about the pie.”

“How was the other thing?”

Deb thought about it.

“Bad and then all right,” she said. “He’s got a beard. He knows an enormous amount about heat pumps. He’s not ready for me to apologize yet and he told me so, which I think might be the single most impressive thing my son has ever done.”

Eli got a fork out of the drawer.

“Is he going to call you again?”

“He said he’d call on a Tuesday.”

“Will he?”

“I don’t know,” Deb said. “I think so. Yes.”

Eli nodded, and cut himself an unreasonable piece of the pie, and said, “You’re going to want to hide the rest of that from yourself, by the way. That’s a known problem with that pie.”

“I’ve been managing my own affairs for fifty-four years.”

“Sure,” Eli said, with his mouth full, and Deb put the pie in the fridge behind the milk, where she could not see it, and then took it out again about forty minutes later.

Twelve days.


# Chapter 21

[I]{.dropcap}t came out of her on a Wednesday night, and she heard it happening the way you hear a glass going off a counter, in the half second where there’s nothing to be done.

The week had been good. That was the setup, and she’d think about that afterward, that it hadn’t come out of a bad week at all. Danny had called on Tuesday, actually called, at seven in the evening, and they’d talked for twenty-two minutes about nothing, about a job he was on in Grandview where the ductwork had been installed by somebody with a personal grudge against physics, and he’d said “I’ll call you next week” at the end of it like a man stating a fact.

She’d hung up and stood in the kitchen feeling something she had not felt in a very long time, which was that a thing had gotten slightly better instead of slightly worse.

And then Wednesday.

Eli got home at six-forty, which was not one of the three, and they had the soup with the little pasta, and he was in a good mood because Gary had let him do a full inventory reconciliation on his own and he’d found a discrepancy in the alternator stock that had been sitting in the system since November.

“Four units,” he said. “Four. And nobody caught it because they’re expensive enough that people assume somebody knows where they are.”

“Where were they?”

“Two got sold as cores by mistake, which is a whole thing, and two are physically in the back behind a pallet of wiper fluid where they’ve been since Thanksgiving.” He was proud of it. “Gary said it’s the first clean reconciliation that store’s had in three years.”

“That’s a real thing, Eli.”

“It’s inventory.”

“It’s four hundred dollars of somebody’s alternators and a store that’s been wrong for five months, and you found it, and don’t do the thing where you make it small.”

He shrugged, but he was pleased, and she could see he was pleased.

And Deb, standing at the sink with a dish towel, full of a good phone call from a son and a good week and a boy in her kitchen being proud of himself, said:

“You know you could stay.”

The pipes ticked. Eli didn’t move.

“What?”

She should have stopped. She knew, in the quarter second available, that she should stop, and she did not stop, because the thing had a momentum of its own now and she was standing behind it.

“Bridges,” she said. “It’s a signature. Renee said it a hundred times, it’s not April third, it’s not April nineteenth, it stays open. You could sign it in June, you could sign it in September, and you’d be in this house and you’d have the same job and the same schedule and nothing about your life would change, except you’d have a room and food and you wouldn’t be spending four hundred dollars a month on Pryor Street.” Her voice had gotten faster. “You’d finish the certificate in two years instead of four. You’d have eleven thousand dollars at the end of it that you’re not going to have otherwise, and I did that math, I actually did that math on paper, and I’m not saying it to be clever, I’m saying it because it’s four hundred a month for twenty-four months, and your groceries on top of that.”

Eli set his spoon down very carefully.

“Deb.”

“I know what you said in February.”

“Okay.”

“I know exactly what you said and I’m not asking you to take it back, that’s not what this is.” She had the towel in both hands now, twisting it, and she was aware of that and could not stop that either. “This isn’t adoption. That’s the whole point. It’s not a family, it’s not a court, it’s not somebody writing down that anything worked. It’s a housing arrangement. It’s a signature on a form that means a young man stays in a bedroom he already lives in for two more years while he does a certificate. That’s all it is.”

“That’s not all it is.”

“It could be all it is.”

“Deb.”

“You’d be twenty and you’d have money and you’d have a certificate and you’d walk out of here on your own two feet with something behind you instead of a hundred and ten dollars in a line called slack.” She heard her voice crack on *slack* and hated it. “That’s not me needing anything from you. That’s just the math. It’s just the math, Eli.”

He was quiet.

The soup steamed on the table between them.

“Is it,” he said.

Deb stood at her sink with a twisted dish towel in her hands.

“No,” she said.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

“Can I ask you something,” he said.

“Yes.”

“How much of that did you have ready?”

Deb looked at the towel in her hands.

“All of it,” she said.

“The eleven thousand?”

“I did it on paper on the eleventh of February. I’ve got the paper. It’s in the drawer in the front room under the phone books nobody’s used since 2011.” She made herself keep going. “I did it the night after the letter came about the hearing, and I’ve had it for six weeks, and I’ve taken it out about nine times, and I’ve been waiting for a night where it wouldn’t land as pressure.”

“And tonight was the night.”

“Tonight you were pleased about four alternators,” Deb said, “and I thought, there, he’s pleased, and I got out a thing I’d been carrying for six weeks and put it on a boy who’d had a good day at work.”

Eli was quiet.

“That’s honest,” he said.

“It’s just late.” She folded the towel and put it on the counter.

Eli got up. He took both bowls to the sink, because that was the arrangement, and he stood next to her at the counter and ran the water, and Deb moved over half a step to make room out of pure muscle memory.

He rinsed one bowl. Set it in the rack. Rinsed the other one.

“I’m not going to answer that tonight,” he said.

“I didn’t ask you to.”

“You did, a bit. That’s all right.” He turned the water off. “I’m saying it out loud so it isn’t sitting in the room being a thing neither of us mentions. I’ve got an answer. I’ve had one since October. I’m not handing it over standing at a sink five minutes after finding out you had yours ready since February.”

Deb held on to the edge of the counter.

“Okay,” she said.

“That’s not me punishing you.”

“I know it isn’t.”

“You’ll decide it is at about two in the morning, and then you’ll be careful with me for a week and I’ll notice.” He dried his hands on the towel she had folded and hung it back on the bar, square, the way he did. “It’s that if I answer it now, it’s an answer to the towel. And you’d take it, and you’d be all right about it in front of me, and then you’d go up.”

“That is a fairly complete description of what I was planning to do.”

“Yeah,” Eli said. “I’ve had three and a half years.”

He went up at nine-forty.

Deb sat at the kitchen table for a long time with the light on over the stove.

Then she got out her phone and typed a message to Marisol that said *I did something stupid tonight* and looked at it for a while and deleted it, and typed *are you around tomorrow* instead, and sent that.

Marisol texted back four minutes later: *cant tomorrow. friday, walk-in, 1:15, bring the good coffee not the school coffee*

Deb put the phone down.

Nine days.


# Chapter 22

[T]{.dropcap}he tires had been a decision and he’d made it on the Wednesday, standing in the AutoWorks lot at a quarter past six looking at a gauge.

Not the tires themselves. The tires had needed doing and had needed doing since January.

The decision was about doing them on Thursday.

Because he had a thing to say and he had known for two days that he had a thing to say, and he had also known that there was no version of saying it across a kitchen table. Across a table she would look at him. Across a table it would be a conversation with two people facing each other and a silence in the middle, and she would try to be all right about it in real time, and he would watch her try.

So he had thought about where.

The driveway had three advantages and he had listed them, actually listed them, in his head, walking to his car: he would be crouched and facing away; there would be a compressor to fill the gaps; and there would be a reason for both of them to be out there that had nothing to do with the conversation, which meant that at any point either of them could stop and the other one could pretend it was about a tire.

He was aware that this was an enormous amount of engineering to apply to a conversation with somebody he trusted.

He was also aware that it was, in every practical respect, the same skill he’d used at eleven years old to work out which of two adults to ask for a thing, and that the difference was not the skill but what he was using it for.

He decided he could live with that. He got in the car and drove home and did not sleep especially well.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The thing Deb had not accounted for was that Eli had been in the house for the phone call.

He’d come in the side door on Tuesday evening, on his break, for the tire gauge he’d left in the hall closet, while she was standing in the kitchen with the phone against her ear, and she hadn’t heard him because the fan was on over the stove, and he’d stopped in the hallway the way you do when you realize you’ve walked into something.

He’d heard about four minutes of it. Not the words. The other thing.

He’d heard her laugh twice. He’d heard her say “no, don’t do that, they’ll charge you for the callback,” in a tone he had never heard her use, and he had stood in that hallway with his coat half off and understood that he was listening to a woman talk to her son.

Then he’d gone back out the side door, quietly, and come in again two minutes later with the door doing its stick-and-give, loud, and by the time he got to the kitchen she was off the phone, and he took the gauge and a heel of bread and went back to the store.

He hadn’t said anything about it. Not Tuesday. Not Wednesday, through all of it, the soup and the offer and everything after.

He said it Thursday.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

They were in the driveway, both of them, because the Escape’s rear tire had been sitting at twenty-six psi for a week and Eli had finally lost patience about it and made her drive it up onto the flat part of the drive so he could do all four with a proper gauge instead of the gas station one.

He had done the tires in his head first, which was how he did everything.

Four wheels, gauge, compressor, valve caps. Twelve minutes of actual work if nothing went wrong, and one of the front valve stems was original and might crack, in which case forty minutes and a trip to the store.

He had also worked out, walking down the drive with the gauge, exactly how far into it he would be when he said the thing.

Second wheel. Not the first, because the first wheel is where you find out whether the compressor is behaving; not the last, because the last wheel has an end to it and a person can walk away from a conversation that ends when a job does.

Second wheel gave him two more after it.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

It was thirty-nine degrees and going down. The light was that late-March six o’clock which is not quite evening. Two doors down, Mrs. Petrosyan’s porch light was already on.

He was crouched at the front passenger wheel with the gauge.

“Twenty-nine,” he said. “Should be thirty-five. That one’s been low since February, you’ve been driving on a tire that’s six pounds down for six weeks.”

“It drives fine.”

“Everything drives fine until it doesn’t, that’s what a tire is.” He worked the compressor. “That’s actually what all of it is. That’s the industry.”

Deb stood with her arms crossed against the cold and let him do it.

Then he said, without looking up, still crouched, “I heard you on the phone Tuesday.”

Deb’s arms came down a little.

“I came in the side door. I didn’t stand there listening, I got about a minute of it and then I went back out and came in again so you’d hear the door.” He checked the gauge, added a little more. “But I got a minute.”

It had been four.

“Okay.”

“You sounded different.”

“Different how?”

He stood up, moved to the rear wheel, crouched again.

“Like somebody who’s known a person their whole life,” he said.

Deb didn’t say anything.

The compressor ran. Somewhere down the block a car door shut.

“I’ve been thinking about it since Tuesday,” Eli said, “and then last night happened, and this is going to sound worse than I mean it.”

“Say it.”

“I’m not him.”

The words went out into the cold driveway.

He stayed crouched, working the valve, not looking at her, which she understood was the only way this was going to be possible for either of them.

“I know you know that,” he said. “I’m not accusing you of something. I want to be really clear, because if you think I’m accusing you of something you’ll get defensive and then we won’t get to the end of this and I need to get to the end of it.” He checked the gauge. “But last night, when you did the math at me. The eleven thousand dollars and the certificate and the two years.”

“Eli.”

“You’ve never done that before. Not once, not in three and a half years, not about anything. You’ve never once tried to get me to do a thing by making it about numbers so I couldn’t argue with it.” He straightened up, finally, and looked at her over the roof of the car. “That was a person trying not to lose somebody. And I don’t think it was about me.”

Deb stood in her own driveway at six in the evening.

“That’s not fair,” she said.

“No.”

“That’s not…” She stopped. Made herself do it properly. “Say the rest.”

Eli put the gauge in his jacket pocket.

“I’ve had four houses,” he said. “This is the fourth. And in every single one of them, there was a reason I was there that had nothing to do with me. Every one. The Bakers were doing it because their church did it, which is a fine reason, I’m not knocking it. The place before that was money, which is also a real reason, and she wasn’t cruel about it, she just needed the check and I was the check. And the first one, the first one was a woman whose kids had grown up and she was extremely lonely and I was twelve and I did not have language for it at the time but I could feel it in the room, all day, every day, this thing where I was supposed to fill something.”

“This isn’t that.”

“I know it’s not that. That’s why I stayed.”

He came around the front of the car.

“But last night you offered me two years,” he said, “three days after a lunch with your son. And I’m not stupid, Deb. And I’ve been in this house long enough to know there’s a picture on the stairs that nobody talks about, and now there’s phone calls, and the same week there’s phone calls you did a thing you’ve never done.”

Deb’s eyes were burning in the cold and she made a decision about that and won it.

“So here’s the thing I need to say,” Eli said. “And then I’m going to do the back tire, because it’s twenty-seven and I can hear it.”

“Okay.”

“I can’t be the proof.”

The porch light two doors down flickered and held.

“Whatever the thing is with him,” Eli said. “The six years, whatever happened. I don’t know what happened and I don’t want to know unless you want to tell me, and it’s not my business. But whatever it is, if I stay in this house another two years, some part of it is going to be about that. Not on purpose. You wouldn’t do it on purpose. It’d just be there, in the room, all day, every day, the same way it was in the first house.” He shook his head slightly. “And I’d be good at it. That’s what I said last night and it’s still true. I’d be so good at being the evidence that you’re not a woman who loses people.”

Deb put a hand on the cold roof of the car.

“That’s the cruelest thing you’ve ever said to me,” she said.

“I know.”

“You’re not wrong.”

“I know that too,” Eli said. “That’s why I said it and not the other version.”

Then, after a second: “I’m still not answering last night. Not out here.”

“I know you’re not.”

“I’m just saying it so it’s said.”

“It’s sitting there anyway,” Deb said. “But I know.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

“Can I say the thing about the first house,” Eli said. “I’ve never told anybody the whole thing and I’ve been sort of half-telling it for four months and it’s starting to annoy me.”

“Say it.”

He was still crouched, hands on the valve.

“Her name was Coreen. She was about sixty, her husband had died about four years before, and her two kids were grown and in Indianapolis and they called her on Sundays.” He worked the compressor. “And she was a completely decent woman. I want to say that first. She never once raised her voice at me, she fed me properly, she went to two parent-teacher conferences in eight months and she asked good questions at both of them.”

“Okay.”

“And every day I was in that house I could feel the size of the hole I was standing in.” He shook his head. “It wasn’t anything she did. It was the house. There was a room that had been somebody’s room and I was in it, and there was a chair at the table that had been somebody’s chair and I was in it, and every Sunday the phone would ring and she’d take it in the other room and come back different, and I’d sit there being twelve and doing math about how to be enough.”

Deb didn’t say anything.

“I lasted eight months,” Eli said. “And I want to be really clear that I didn’t leave. I never leave, that’s not a thing kids in care get to do. What happened was that I got quieter and quieter until the agency thought there was something wrong with me, and there was a review, and Renee moved me, and Coreen cried at the meeting.”

“God.”

“Yeah.” He stood up and moved to the last wheel. “And that’s the whole story except for one thing, which is that I’ve thought about her about six times a year for five years and I’ve never been able to work out whose fault it was. Because it wasn’t hers. She was lonely and she got approved for a placement and the state gave her a boy, and every single thing she did was correct.”

“It wasn’t yours either.”

“No,” Eli said. “That’s what took me five years. It wasn’t mine either. It was just a room with a shape in it, and I didn’t fit the shape, and there’s nothing in the paperwork anywhere for that.”

The compressor ran.

“So when I say I can’t be the proof,” he said, “that’s what I’m talking about. I’m not accusing you of being Coreen. You are nothing like Coreen. I’m saying I’ve been in a room with a shape in it before and I know what it does to me, and I’d rather have a small room with no shape in it than a good room with one.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

They stood there for a bit. He did the back tire. The compressor was loud and neither of them tried to talk over it.

“Do you want to know what happened with him?” Deb said.

Eli looked up from the wheel.

“Only if you want to tell me.”

“That’s not an answer.”

“It’s the only answer I’m giving you,” he said. “I’m not going to say yes and have you feel obligated, and I’m not going to say no and have you think I don’t care. You’re a person and you’ve got a thing, and if you want to put it down in this driveway you can.”

Deb leaned against the car.

“I said something to him in 2019,” she said. “One sentence, in that kitchen, and I never took it back, and he stopped calling, and it’s been six years.”

“What was the sentence?”

“I told him he’d end up like his father.”

Eli didn’t say anything for a second.

“Okay,” he said. “Yeah, that’ll do it.”

“That’s it? That’s your response?”

“What do you want me to say? That’s a bad sentence, Deb. That’s about as bad as they come.” He stood up and checked the last tire again. “I’ve had one of those said to me and I remember the room.”

“What was yours?”

“‘You’re a lot of work,’” Eli said. “Second house. She wasn’t being cruel, she was on the phone to somebody and I was on the stairs and she didn’t know I was there, and she wasn’t wrong, I was a lot of work, I’d been in three schools in a year.” He crouched. “I was thirteen. I’m seventeen and eleven months and I can still tell you what she was wearing.”

The compressor ran.

“So no,” he said, “I don’t think it’s strange that he left. I think six years is about right for that sentence, and I think the fact he’s calling you now is the surprising part, not the six years.”

Deb stood in the cold and found, unexpectedly, that this was the most comforting thing anybody had said to her about Danny since 2019.

“Everyone else keeps telling me it wasn’t that bad,” she said.

“Everyone else is being nice to you.”

“You’re not being nice to me?”

“I’m being accurate,” Eli said. “You can get nice anywhere.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

When he’d finished he coiled the hose the way he did everything, properly, and put it in the trunk, and shut the trunk, and then he said, in an entirely different voice:

“That’s not the whole thing, though.”

“What?”

“That’s the reason I can’t stay. It’s not the reason I’m going.” He leaned against the back of the car. “Those are different and I’ve been trying to work out how to say it since November.”

“Say it however.”

“I’m going because I want to.” He shrugged, once. “That’s it. That’s actually it. Not because of him, not because of the first house, not because of anything anybody did. I want to see what I’m like in a room I pay for. I’ve wanted it since I was about fifteen. It’s the only thing I’ve ever wanted that wasn’t about getting away from something, and I think that might be the entire reason it took me until this house to want it.”

Deb turned and leaned against the car next to him.

“You know what the worst part of that is,” she said.

“What?”

“It’s the best possible answer and it’s the only one I can’t do anything with.”

“Yeah,” Eli said. “I know. I checked.”

She laughed. Wetly, and once, and it turned into something else halfway through, and she got hold of it.

“Don’t,” he said, alarmed. “Deb.”

“I’m not.”

“You’re doing something.”

“I’m doing nothing. I’m standing in my driveway in thirty-nine degrees.”

“Okay,” he said, unconvinced, and then, after a second, “Your tires are all thirty-five now. All four. They were all wrong. Every single one, all winter, and you drove that car to work every day for four months.”

“Are you going to be like this about my car for the rest of your life?”

She heard it as she said it. *The rest of your life.*

Neither of them touched it.

“Yes,” Eli said. “Obviously.”

And he went inside, and she stood out there for another minute in the cold with her hand on the roof of a car with four correct tires, and then she followed him in.


# Chapter 23

[S]{.dropcap}he had spent the morning being extremely good at her job, which was what she did with things.

Four hundred and twelve trays. A delivery from Wes, correct for once, which he pointed out twice. The Monday menu rebuilt on the fly because the chicken patties she had found in February had gone out on the Wednesday and nobody had told her.

And a phone call at ten-forty from the district office about a form, taken standing in the office doorway, in which Charlotte from Nutrition Services asked Deb whether she’d ever consider coming to the district level.

Charlotte, who had sat in a meeting in November of 2024 and let the lunch program be described as an inefficiency, and who had never found out that it got back to Deb inside a day, because everything got back to Deb inside a day.

“Doing what?”

“Coordinator. Cindy’s retiring in December, it’d be the eleven elementaries and the two middles.” A pause. “It’s a bump. It’s a real bump, Deb, it’s about nine thousand more and it’s Monday to Friday, no six a.m.”

Deb stood in the doorway with a clipboard.

“I’d be in an office.”

“You’d be in an office.”

“I’d be writing the menus that other people cook.”

“You’d be writing the menus and doing the ordering for thirteen buildings,” Charlotte said, “and, frankly, fixing the ordering, because it’s a mess, and you’ve been sending me emails about it since 2018 and every single one of them has been right.”

Deb looked out at the kitchen. Bev at the dish pit. Andre with the racks. Marisol counting something.

“When would I need to say?”

“October.”

“Then I’ll tell you in October,” Deb said, and hung up, and stood there for a minute, and then went and got the walk-in inventory sheet, because it was Friday, and Friday was the walk-in, and had been for twenty years.

She did not tell Marisol at one-fifteen. She had four other things to say at one-fifteen and this one went in a drawer, and she told nobody about it through April and May and most of the summer, and it was still there in September when everything else was decided.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The walk-in at one-fifteen was thirty-eight degrees and smelled like cardboard and cold lettuce, and Marisol was already in it, sitting on an upturned milk crate with her coat on.

“You brought the good coffee.”

“I said I would.”

“People say a lot of things.” Marisol took the cup. “Sit down, there’s a crate.”

Deb sat on a crate in a walk-in cooler in her work shoes.

“So,” Marisol said. “Wednesday.”

“How do you know about Wednesday?”

“You texted me *are you around tomorrow*, which you have done four times in eleven years, and the last time was when your mother went into the hospital.” She blew across the cup. “And then you waited two days for me without saying one word about it, which is its own information. So. Wednesday.”

Deb turned her cup around in her hands.

“I offered him Bridges,” she said. “Wednesday night, at the sink, with the math. I dressed it up as a housing arrangement and I told him it wasn’t about me needing anything.”

“Was it about you needing something?”

“He asked me that. He said ‘is it,’ just like that, and I said no.” Deb looked at the floor. “And then I stood there for about two seconds and said no again, except the second no was the answer.”

Marisol nodded slowly.

“And what did he say?”

“Nothing. He hasn’t.” Deb turned the cup around in her hands. “He said he wasn’t going to answer it standing at a sink five minutes after I’d admitted I’d had the whole thing ready since February. Then he said goodnight and went up. That was Wednesday.”

“And it’s Friday.”

“And it’s Friday.”

Marisol let that sit.

“There’s a worse part,” Deb said. “Six weeks I had that paper in a drawer, and in six weeks I never once did the other math.”

“Which math.”

“What it costs him.” She was looking at a case of shredded cheese. “I did the money and the certificate and the eleven thousand dollars, in three columns, twice. I never sat down and worked out what two more years in a house where you’re checking every day actually costs a person. Because that column isn’t on paper. I’d have had to ask him for the numbers.”

“Would he have told you?”

“Yes,” Deb said. “That’s what makes it bad.”

Marisol did not tell her it wasn’t.

“And then Thursday he did the tires,” Deb said.

“The tires.”

“He did all four tires in the driveway and told me I couldn’t be the proof.”

Marisol’s cup stopped halfway.

“He said what?”

“He’d heard me on the phone with Danny. He put it together with the offer, and he told me that if he stayed, some part of it would be about Danny, and he’d spend two years being the evidence that I’m not a woman who loses people.” Deb’s voice was flat and steady. “And he was right.”

The compressor kicked on somewhere above them.

“That’s a seventeen-year-old,” Marisol said.

“That’s a seventeen-year-old.”

“That is an alarming seventeen-year-old, Deb.”

“He’s had four houses,” Deb said. “You get good at reading a room or you don’t survive it. He told me the first house was a woman whose kids had grown and she was lonely and he could feel it in the room all day, every day. He was twelve.”

Marisol sat with that in the cold.

“So now you tell me the rest of it,” she said.

“There’s no rest of it.”

“Deb.”

“There’s no…”

“You’ve been carrying something around since Sunday and you’ve had a face on you all week and don’t tell me it’s the boy, because you’ve had the boy face since Christmas and this is a different face.” Marisol set her cup down on the shelf beside her. “You went to Bergstrom’s and you came back different and you haven’t said one word about what he actually said to you. Six years and you came back and told me he had a beard and knew about heat pumps.”

Deb sat on a milk crate.

“February 2020,” she said.

“What about it?”

“That’s when I started the pre-service classes.” She was looking at a case of shredded cheese. “I said it to him in October of 2019. I did the paperwork in February. Four months.”

Marisol didn’t say anything.

“Every word of the story I told you was true,” Deb said. “The rooms were empty and I’d been thinking about it for years and I had a lot to give. All of it, true.” She looked up. “And it was also not why. Why is that I said a thing to my son in that kitchen and never took it back, and I’ve spent six years running a house full of children so I could be a woman who has a lot to give instead of a woman who says a thing like that.”

The compressor hummed.

Marisol picked her coffee back up and drank some of it.

“Okay,” she said.

Deb waited.

“That’s it?” she said. “That’s what I get?”

“What did you want?”

“I don’t know. Something.”

“Deb.” Marisol shifted on the crate. “You want me to argue with you. You’ve come in here to be argued out of it and I’m not going to, because you’re right.”

Deb sat back against the shelving.

“You’re right,” Marisol said again. “That’s what you were doing. I’ve known it for about six years, and I’ve said it to you sideways probably forty times, and you told me on Friday nights that I was being dramatic.”

“You never said it.”

“I said ‘getting somebody in the house before the sheets are cold is a different activity.’ I said it in a gymnasium two weeks ago and you looked at the floor.” She shrugged. “I’ve been saying it for years, in the only way you’d let me say it, which is with a joke on the end.”

Deb laughed, once, in the cold.

“So what am I supposed to do with it?”

“Nothing.”

“Marisol.”

“No, I mean it. That’s my whole answer and I’ve had four days to think of a better one.” Marisol leaned forward with her elbows on her knees. “Here’s what you’ve got. You’ve got a reason you did a good thing for six years. It’s not a nice reason. It’s not the reason you told people and it’s not even the reason you told yourself, and now you know it, and you’re going to know it for the rest of your life, and there is nothing to do about it. You can’t go back and re-do it with a better motive.”

“That’s bleak.”

Marisol picked a piece of lettuce off her sleeve. “You want the actual thing? Those four kids don’t care. Not one of them. Whatever you were doing it for, the girl with the braids had somebody sit up with her through a stomach bug in 2021, and I know that because you called me at four in the morning to ask about the Pedialyte. That happened. It happened whether or not your reasons were pretty.”

Deb looked at her.

“He said something like that.”

“Who, Eli?”

“He said the reason he stayed was that this house wasn’t like the first one. Which is true. Which was also true the whole time I was doing it for the wrong reason.” She shook her head slowly. “Both of them. The whole six years.”

“Yes,” Marisol said.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

“There’s one more piece,” Deb said.

“Go on.”

“Jaylen texts me on my birthday. Nine words, every year, since 2022.” She was looking at the floor of the walk-in. “And every single year I get that text and I think, *there, look, that’s evidence.* And then I have a very good day.”

“That’s not a crime.”

“No. But listen to what I just said.” Deb looked up. “A twenty-two-year-old man in Cleveland sends nine words to a woman who looked after him for eight months when he was a teenager, and the thing I do with it is use it as an exhibit.”

Marisol was quiet.

“That’s the bit I can’t get past,” Deb said. “Not the classes in 2020. That’s a decision I made once, badly, six years ago, and I can look at it. It’s the nine words. It’s that I’ve been doing something to those nine words every year for four years and I’ve never once just let them be a nice text from Jaylen.”

“So let them be a nice text from Jaylen.”

“How?”

“You just noticed you were doing it,” Marisol said. “That’s the entire mechanism, Deb, there isn’t another one. You’ll get the nine words in July and you’ll do the thing again, because four years is a groove, and you’ll catch yourself about four seconds later, and in about three years you’ll catch yourself first.” She shrugged. “That’s what changing something is. It’s not a decision. It’s noticing faster and faster until the noticing gets there first.”

Deb sat with that in the cold.

“That’s the most useful thing anybody’s said to me since Christmas.”

“I know,” Marisol said. “I’ve been saving it. I heard it in a church basement in 2015 and I’ve been looking for somewhere to put it for ten years.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

“Can I tell you the actual worst part,” Deb said.

“There’s a worse part.”

“There’s a worse part and I’ve had it since Sunday and I’ve not said it out loud.” She had her hands wrapped around the cup. “If I’d known in 2020 what I was doing, I don’t think I’d have stopped.”

Marisol was quiet.

“That’s the thing I keep arriving at,” Deb said. “I’ve been sitting in this cooler for twenty minutes doing a kind of confession, and underneath it there’s a woman who thinks: and if somebody had sat me down in February 2020 and told me exactly what I was up to, in these words, I’d have listened carefully and then filled out the form anyway.”

“Probably.”

“Doesn’t that make the whole thing worthless?”

“No,” Marisol said, “it makes you a person.”

Deb looked up.

“There’s a version of this you’ve been trying to get to all week,” Marisol said, “and I’ve watched you circle it since the gymnasium, and it’s the version where you find the one true fact about yourself and then everything reorganizes and you’re different afterward. That’s not going to happen. Not to anybody over forty.”

“So what happens?”

“You know a thing now that you didn’t know a month ago. That’s it. That’s the entire yield.” Marisol shrugged. “Nobody will ever congratulate you about it.”

Deb sat with that in the cold.

“That’s a very small return.”

“It’s the only size they come in,” Marisol said.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

They sat in the cold for a minute.

“Can I ask you something,” Deb said.

“You’re going to.”

“Are you going to be all right? When I don’t call Renee in May.”

Marisol’s eyebrows went up.

“Am *I* going to be all right?”

“You’ve eaten at my table every Friday for eleven years,” Deb said. “I’m asking a question about you. It’s allowed.”

Marisol thought about it, which Deb appreciated.

“I’ll survive,” she said. “I’ll have to eat my own cooking, which is a tragedy for both of us, but I’ll survive.” She stood, wincing at her knees. “You’re not calling Renee in May, then.”

“I don’t know.”

“That’s the second time you’ve said ‘I don’t know’ about that and both times it meant no.”

“It meant I don’t know.”

“It meant no, and that’s fine, and it’s about time.” Marisol collected both cups. “Do me one favor.”

“What?”

“Don’t decide it in April.” She put a hand on the door lever. “You’re going to be in that house on the fourth with all that quiet and you’re going to want to solve it by Thursday, and you’ll pick up a phone and be back in the training room by June and never look at what you’re doing, and it’ll be six more years.”

“So what do I do?”

“You be bored,” Marisol said. “You be bored and miserable in that house until at least the fall, and if in September you want to do it again, do it, and you’ll be doing it for a real reason for the first time, and God help whoever gets you then, because that kid’s going to have an unbelievable time.”

She got the door open. Warm air came in.

“That’s the nicest thing you’ve ever said to me,” Deb said.

“I’ve said nicer.”

“You have not.”

“I said you make a good chili.”

“You said it had too much cumin.”

“It does have too much cumin,” Marisol said, holding the door. “Both of those are true.”


# Chapter 24

[H]{.dropcap}e’d told her about the room on a Sunday in February and had been rehearsing the sentence for four days before that.

That was the pattern with everything, and Deb had begun to be able to read it: a thing would arrive already decided, in a flat voice, at a moment he had chosen, and behind every one of those flat sentences was a week of a seventeen-year-old walking around a school with it in his mouth.

*I’ve got a room.*

Four words on a Sunday over the ziti, and then, when she asked, the whole thing in about ninety seconds: the address, the rent, the landlord’s name, the date he could take it, the deposit already paid out of money he had saved specifically and had not mentioned.

She had said, “That’s good, Eli,” and had meant it, and had gone up at nine-forty and sat on the edge of her bed for ten minutes.

The deposit was the part that had done it. Four hundred dollars, paid in February, out of a boy’s own money, to a woman on Pryor Street, in a transaction that had been conceived and executed entirely outside the knowledge of anybody in this house.

That was not a secret. She had made herself be very clear with herself about that. He was not obliged to consult her about the disposition of money he had earned at a counter on Route 4.

It was just that somewhere in the fourteen months he had spent looking for her reason and not finding it, he had also, quietly, and without ever once being unkind about it, been building the version of his life in which she did not appear.

And she had been in the kitchen the whole time, making the ziti.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The car was a 2009 Civic with a hundred and sixty-one thousand miles on it, silver where it wasn’t primer, and Eli had bought it in September for fourteen hundred dollars from a man in Xenia who had been honest with him about all but one thing.

The one thing was the rear subframe, which had been repaired at some point by somebody with a welder and an attitude.

“It’s not unsafe,” Eli said, on his back on a creeper in Trevor Pham’s garage on the third Saturday in March, the afternoon of the phone call, the day before Bergstrom’s. “I want to be clear about that because you’re doing a face.”

“I’m not doing a face.”

“You’re standing in a garage doing a face.”

“I’m standing in a garage in a coat,” Deb said, “because it’s thirty-one degrees and my son’s under a car.”

Neither of them said anything for a second.

Eli slid out from under it, sat up, wiped his hands on the rag.

“Anyway,” he said. “It’s fine. It’ll pass inspection, it did pass inspection, it’s just ugly.”

“You have grease on your face.”

“That’s the job.”

Trevor Pham, who was seventeen and enormous and who had said perhaps forty words to Deb in four years, said from behind a lawnmower, “He’s been under that car most Saturdays since October,” and went back to what he was doing.

“Thank you, Trevor.”

“You’re welcome.”

Deb looked around the garage, because she had not been in it before.

Along the back wall, stacked neatly against the pegboard, were seven boxes.

She had known about them, obviously, since the empty drawer in the first week of March. She had done the arithmetic and arrived at Trevor’s garage in four seconds and never said a word. But knowing a thing and standing four feet from it in a cold garage on a Saturday were not, it turned out, the same activity.

They were labeled. In marker, in his handwriting, on the side of each one: KITCHEN. CLOTHES W. CLOTHES S. TOOLS. PAPERS. BOOKS/MISC. TOOLS 2.

She looked at KITCHEN for a while.

“That one’s mostly nothing,” Eli said, behind her. “It’s like four things.”

“I wasn’t going to say anything.”

“You were going to say something about the kitchen box.”

“I was going to say something about the kitchen box,” Deb agreed.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Trevor’s garage had been the venue for a great deal of Eli’s life and Deb had never been inside it.

She looked around while he worked. A pegboard with a shadow-outline of every tool, drawn in marker by Trevor’s father sometime in the previous decade, about sixty percent of which currently held the correct tool. A chest freezer. A dartboard with no darts near it. Four bicycles in various states, three of which were clearly nobody’s anymore. A refrigerator from approximately 1988 that hummed at a frequency Deb could feel in her back teeth and that contained, she would learn, exclusively soda.

And, taped to the wall above the workbench, a piece of paper.

She went over to it because it was the only paper in the room.

It was a printed sheet, laminated, headed TORQUE SPECS, and under that in three columns a list of bolt sizes and figures, and at the bottom, in ballpoint, in Eli’s handwriting: *if unsure, look it up. do not guess. nobody has ever been proud of guessing.*

“Did you write this?”

Eli slid halfway out from under the car.

“Trevor’s dad printed the chart. I did the bit at the bottom.”

“When?”

“Two years ago. Trevor rounded a bolt on a wheel hub because he guessed and it cost his dad four hours and a tap set.” He slid back under. “It’s not, like, a philosophy. It’s a note about bolts.”

Deb looked at it a while longer.

“Nobody has ever been proud of guessing,” she said.

“It’s about bolts, Deb.”

“Mm,” said Deb, and went and looked at the boxes some more.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

They went to Pryor Street after, because he wanted her to see it, which she had not expected and which he presented as a logistical necessity.

“You have to see it because you’re going to be driving to it. If you don’t see it, you’ll do the thing where you imagine it, and whatever you imagine will be worse, and then you’ll be weird about it for a year.”

“That is an extremely accurate assessment of my character.”

“I’ve had three and a half years.”

The house was a 1920s two-story on a street of 1920s two-stories, a block and a half off Route 4, with a porch that had been enclosed at some point in the seventies and a yard that had a genuine, aggressive rose bush in it that had gone feral.

The landlord was a woman named Bernice Okafor who was seventy-one and who came out onto the porch in a cardigan and told Deb, without being asked, that she’d been renting rooms in that house for twenty-six years, that she did not permit smoking in the house or in the yard, that the third stair was loose and would be fixed in April, and that Eli had been the only applicant in four years who had asked her about the water pressure.

“He asked about the water pressure,” Bernice said, with something like wonder.

“That sounds like him.”

“Nobody asks about the water pressure. They ask about parking.”

“How’s the water pressure?”

“Terrible,” Bernice said. “I told him it was terrible. He said as long as he knew.”

The room was upstairs at the back. Twelve by eleven, one window facing the yard and the feral rose bush, a closet, a bed frame that came with the room, hardwood that had been refinished badly in some previous decade. There were two other tenants: a man in his fifties who worked at the hospital and kept to himself, and a woman doing a nursing program at Sinclair who Bernice described as “very serious.”

Shared bathroom. Shared kitchen downstairs, which was large and yellow and had a table in it.

Deb stood in the middle of the room.

It was a good room. That was the thing she had not prepared for and could not do anything about. It was clean and dry and the window faced south and it was four hundred dollars a month in a house with a seventy-one-year-old woman downstairs who noticed who asked about water pressure.

“It’s a good room,” she said.

“Yeah.”

“I mean it. I’m not doing a voice. It’s a very good room, Eli.”

“I looked at nine.”

“Nine?”

“Since October.” He was standing by the window. “Two of them were fine. One was actually cheaper. This one’s got Bernice.”

“Bernice is worth thirty dollars a month.”

“Bernice is worth a hundred,” Eli said. “Bernice has run this house for twenty-six years and there’s a fire extinguisher on both floors and the smoke detectors have new batteries. I checked them. I checked them before I gave her the deposit and she watched me do it and I think that’s why I got the room.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

On the way back to the car Bernice called after them from the porch.

“April first,” she said. “You’re moving the first?”

“Saturday the twenty-eighth,” Eli said. “If that’s all right. I paid from the first, I’m not asking to change the rent.”

“Then move the twenty-eighth, why would I care.” Bernice pulled the cardigan tighter. “Bring your own bulbs. There’s one in the ceiling and it’s forty watts and you’ll go blind.”

“Okay.”

“And the boy before you left a lamp in the closet. It’s a good lamp. It’s yours if you want it or I’ll put it out.”

“I want the lamp,” Eli said.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Bernice walked them down the hall on the way out and stopped at a door.

“That’s the bathroom. There’s four of you on it and there’s a schedule on the back of the door and I did not write the schedule, they wrote the schedule in 2022 and it has survived three tenants, which I consider a miracle of civilization.”

“There’s a bathroom schedule.”

“There’s a bathroom schedule and you will keep to it,” Bernice said, “and I will tell you now that Martin ignores it entirely and nobody has ever confronted him and I have decided that this is the price of Martin, who has never been late with rent in six years.”

Deb looked at the schedule. It was laminated. Somebody had used four colors.

“Who made this?”

“A girl called Aneesa who moved out in 2023 and who I think about weekly,” said Bernice. “She also organized the recycling and put labels on the shelves in the pantry and left a note explaining the labels. That house has never been the same.” She shut the door. “Anyway. You’ll want to look at the schedule and then do whatever you want, like everybody else.”

At the top of the stairs she stopped again and said, to Deb, not to Eli, “He’ll be all right here.”

Deb had not asked.

“I’ve had thirty-one of them through this house,” Bernice said. “Some of them for a month, some of them for four years. You get so you can tell in the first conversation, and the tell isn’t the job or the deposit, it’s what they ask about.” She started down. “They ask about parking. They ask about whether they can have people over. And once every few years one of them asks about the water pressure, which is a question about whether the house works, which is a question about the house and not about them, and those ones are fine.”

She got to the bottom and turned around.

“That’s all,” she said. “You looked like a woman who wanted to be told.”


::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

In the car, Deb sat for a second before starting it.

“The twenty-eighth,” she said.

“Yeah.”

“That’s Saturday.”

“Yeah.”

“That’s six days before the hearing.”

Eli looked out the windshield, through the crack, at Pryor Street.

“I know,” he said.

“You did that on purpose.”

“Yes.”

Deb put her hands on the wheel and didn’t start the car.

“Say it, then,” she said. “You’ve clearly got it worked out and I’d rather hear the whole thing than get it in pieces over the next week.”

“Okay.” He kept looking out the window. “If I’m still living in your house on April third, then what happens in that room is that a magistrate makes a finding about where I live. Renee reads the report, Meehan asks me the questions, and whatever I answer, the answer goes in a file, and the file is what happened.”

“That’s what a hearing is.”

“That’s what a hearing is.” He nodded. “But if I move out on the twenty-eighth, then on April third I walk into 4B as a guy who already has an address. And she asks me the questions and I answer them and none of it is a decision anymore. It’s just her writing down a thing I already did.”

The car ticked.

“It’s the same outcome,” he said. “I know it’s the same outcome. It’s the exact same outcome, six days apart, and I’ve been over it about four hundred times and it makes no practical difference to anything.”

“But.”

“But one of them is the state ending it and one of them is me ending it,” Eli said. “And I get to pick which.”

Deb looked at the crack in her windshield.

Six years of this. Court dates and case plans and permanency hearings and reports with boxes on them, and four children who had all been moved by adults on days chosen by adults, and here was one who had found six days and taken them.

“Okay,” she said.

“That’s it?”

“What did you think I’d say?”

“I had about six versions,” Eli said. “The one I had most was that you’d say it was six fewer days.”

“It is six fewer days.”

“Yeah.”

“It’s six fewer days,” Deb said, “and you’re right, and I’d have done exactly the same thing at seventeen if I’d been smart enough to think of it, which I wasn’t.”

She started the car.

Halfway down Pryor Street he said, “You can come Saturday. To move it. I’m not asking, I’m saying you can if you want, and if you’d rather not I’ll get Trevor and it’s four hours.”

Deb kept her eyes on the road.

“I’ll be there at nine,” she said.

“It’s seven boxes and a bed.”

“I’ll be there at nine.”

“Okay,” Eli said, and then, after a block, “There’s a lamp in a closet on Pryor Street that belongs to me now, and I want that on the record as the first thing I’ve ever owned that somebody gave me for free without it being about anything.”

“Noted.”

“It’s a big day for me.”

“I can tell,” Deb said. “You’ve mentioned the lamp four times.”

“It’s a good lamp.”

“You haven’t seen the lamp.”

“Bernice said it’s a good lamp,” Eli said, “and I have decided to trust her about the lamp,” and Deb laughed all the way to the light at Fourth and Chestnut.


# Chapter 25

[T]{.dropcap}he last week had a texture to it that neither of them acknowledged and both of them noticed.

Small things got done. On the Sunday he changed the batteries in both smoke detectors, which had not needed doing, and Deb watched him do it from the hallway and said nothing at all. On the Monday he tightened the handle on the back door, which had been loose since 2023. On the Tuesday, before his shift, he spent forty minutes in the basement with a flashlight looking at the water heater and came up and said, “You’ve got about two years on that, maybe three. Start putting money aside in the fall, don’t wait for it to go, because when they go they go on a Saturday.”

“Is that a rule?”

“That’s a rule,” Eli said. “Everything expensive fails on a Saturday. Ask anybody in a parts store.”

She wrote *water heater, fall* on the pad by the phone.

He was doing an audit. She understood that on the Tuesday, standing in the kitchen with a pen in her hand: a boy with no other way to say it, going through her house one appliance at a time.

She did not find it sad. She found it, standing in her kitchen with a pen, funny, and she laughed out loud on her own in an empty room, and Eli called down “what?” from upstairs, and she called back “nothing,” and it was nothing.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

He told Renee on Monday, on the phone, standing up in the kitchen, which Deb noticed and said nothing about.

It took twelve minutes. Deb heard one side of it: *Saturday the twenty-eighth. Pryor Street. Bernice Okafor, she’s got the lease. No, I’ll still be at the hearing, I’m not skipping the hearing. Yes. Yes. I know. I know that.* A long pause. *Okay. Thanks, Renee.*

He hung up and put the phone on the counter.

“What’d she say?”

“She said the agency doesn’t have to approve it because I’m seventeen and eleven months and the placement’s ending anyway, so legally it’s a technicality.” He rubbed the back of his neck. “And then she said something else.”

“What?”

“She said, ‘You know you can call me after, right? Not as a caseworker. The case closes on your birthday and then I’m not anybody, but you can still call me.’” He looked at the counter. “And then she said, ‘People don’t, and I want you to be one of the ones who does.’”

Deb leaned against the doorframe.

“Are you going to?”

“I don’t know.” He was quiet for a second. “She’s had my file since I was twelve. She’s read things about me I’ve never read. That’s a strange thing to have coffee with somebody about.”

“It is.”

“But she poured a whole van’s worth of gratitude on me over a battery terminal, so.” He shrugged. “Maybe.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The week was five days long and both of them worked all of them.

That was the thing Deb had not seen coming, and she thought about it later with something like gratitude: there was no long slow farewell week, because he had shifts and she had four hundred and twelve trays and life did not stop to make room for it. Monday he closed. Thursday he was in the driveway with a tire gauge until the light went, and by the time either of them ate anything it was half past eight and neither of them wanted it.

They had the Sunday, which was one of the three. They had the Wednesday, which turned into the worst twenty minutes of the month. They had the Friday.

Three, in a week that had not been arranged to give them any, and only one of them the one that had been agreed.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

And they had the Tuesday, which was not a dinner and did not count as one, and which she thought about for a great deal longer than she thought about any of the three.

She was up at eleven because she had not managed to be asleep, and she came down when she heard the door, and he was at the table with his coat still on and a glass of water he had not drunk any of.

“You’re up.”

“I’m up.”

He didn’t say anything else for a while, and it was not the usual quiet. The usual quiet had a laptop in it.

“Did Gary send you home?”

“No.” He turned the glass a quarter turn on the table. “I did the till twice. It was out by forty cents and I did it again and it was out by forty cents again, and then Reggie did it and it was fine.” He looked at the glass. “It was fine the first time. I did it wrong twice.”

“That happens to everybody.”

“It doesn’t happen to me. That’s the thing I actually do.”

Deb sat down at her own table.

She had four or five sentences available and she used none of them, which was not restraint, because restraint has a decision in it somewhere, and she had simply looked at the top of his head and found she had nothing she was willing to spend.

“It’s March,” Eli said.

“I know what month it is.”

“No.” He rubbed his eye with the back of his wrist. “That’s the thing I’m telling you. It’s March.”

And then he stopped.

Deb got up and put the kettle on, because it was something to do with a hand, and because it put her back to him.

“There’s a date in it,” he said, behind her. “Second half of the month. It’s not the… it’s not a date anybody would know about, it’s not on anything.” A pause with the tap running in it. “My mother’s. When it got finalized. I was thirteen and they let me sit in and it took about twenty minutes and at the end a woman I’d never met before signed a piece of paper.”

The kettle started doing its thing.
