“And it comes round,” he said. “And I’m no good for about two weeks and I don’t always notice it’s started until it’s finished. I’ve had five of them now.”

Deb stood with her hand flat on the counter.

“Have you told anybody this?”

“No.”

“Trevor?”

“Trevor’s worked out that it happens. Not why. Just that it happens and roughly when, and he has never asked me about it, not one time in four years.” A breath. “In January he told me across a lunch table that I was two months early this year. And Amos said one word about it in a hallway. Amos says about twenty words a week. He spent one of them on me.”

The kettle got loud and then got quiet.

“Eli.”

“Don’t,” he said, and it came out badly, and both of them heard it come out badly. “Sorry. Don’t do a thing. If you do a thing I’ll have to be all right about it in front of you and I haven’t got it tonight.”

“I wasn’t going to do a thing.”

“No,” he said. “You weren’t. I know.”

He got up.

“I’m going up. That’s not storming out, that’s going up.”

“I know what it is.”

He went. She heard the fourth stair.

She stood in her own kitchen with a kettle that had boiled for nothing, and she did not follow him and did not call up after him and did not make him a plate, and each one of those was a decision, and she made all three of them in about ten seconds standing on the same square foot of floor.

She turned the kettle off at the wall.

Through the ceiling she could hear him counting.

She had heard it perhaps forty times in three and a half years and had never once been able to make out the numbers. She stood under it for about a minute. Then she went up herself and got into bed and lay there listening to a boy down the hall count something she could not identify, in the second half of March.

On Friday night, which Marisol had given up without being asked, he came in at six-forty and said, “Last one,” about the dinner, and she said, “Don’t do that,” and he said, “Sorry,” and meant it, and they ate the chicken with the lemon, and it was, they both agreed, the best pan of it she had ever made, which was almost certainly untrue and which neither of them examined.

Afterward he did the dishes and she dried, and at the end of it he said, “I’m going to do the room tonight. Get the bed apart, get the stuff in bags, so tomorrow’s just carrying.”

“Okay.”

“You don’t have to help with that part.”

“I know.”

He went up. She heard the sounds through the ceiling: drawers, the wardrobe door, the particular percussive clatter of a bed frame being taken apart by somebody who knew where the bolts were.

Deb sat at the kitchen table and did not go up.

She got the calendar down off the fridge instead. April third was still boxed in red. She had drawn that box in December with a pen from the school office, and it had been more than a hundred days out, and she remembered doing it and thinking that a hundred days was a very long time.

She looked at it for a while, and then she took the pen out of the drawer and drew a second box, around Saturday the twenty-eighth.

Then she put the calendar back on the fridge, because she was not, in the end, a woman who threw a calendar away.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

On the Monday, she had been in Dayton at the food safety recertification, four hours in a hotel conference room off the interstate with fifty other people from a dozen districts, being read a slide deck by a woman from the state.

Deb had done this recertification six times. She could have delivered it. She sat in the third row with a laminated handout and a cup of hotel coffee and let it go past her.

At the break, a woman she half-knew from the Northridge district said, “You’re Deb, right? From Deerbrook Falls?”

“That’s me.”

“You do the pancake thing.”

“I do the pancake thing.”

“My sister’s kid goes there. She says you’re the reason they still have the pancake thing.” The woman refilled her coffee. “She says the PTO tried to make it a catered event two years ago and it didn’t happen and everybody knows why.”

Deb had, in fact, killed that proposal, in a meeting, in under four minutes, using a spreadsheet.

“It’s a pancake supper,” she said. “You can’t cater a pancake supper. The whole point of it is that it’s bad and everybody’s there.”

“That’s what she said you said.”

They stood by the coffee urns in a hotel corridor in Dayton, two women in their fifties who ran school kitchens.

“How long have you been in it?” the woman asked.

“Twenty years in August.”

“God.” She said it with real feeling. “I’m at fourteen and I’ve got a countdown on my phone.”

“To what?”

“Fifty-seven. When I can go.” She laughed. “Don’t tell anybody.”

Deb drove back to work on the interstate at one in the afternoon with the sun coming in on the left side of the car and thought about the fact that she did not have a countdown on her phone, had never had one, and that in twenty years it had never once occurred to her to want one.

She thought: *I like the job.*

And then, four miles later: *I like the job and I have been treating it like a thing I do while the real thing happens in the house.*

She got home at five-forty. The kitchen light was on. Eli was at the table with the tape gun and three collapsed boxes before his closing shift, and he looked up and said, “How was Dayton?”

“A woman read me a slide about norovirus for four hours.”

“Was it a good slide?”

“It was the same slide,” Deb said. “It’s been the same slide since 2011,” and got the pan out.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Monday he closed and she was asleep before he got in, which had happened perhaps four times in three and a half years and which she was furious about in the morning.

She found evidence of him in the kitchen at six-fifteen: a plate in the rack, a mug upside down beside it, and the light over the stove left on. She had been leaving it on for him for two weeks. He had left it on for her.

She stood there and looked at a light switch for slightly too long, and then made her coffee and went to work.

She did the arithmetic once, on the Thursday, in the corner of a food order sheet, and then scribbled it out so completely that she went through the paper.

Neither of them had arranged any of it. That was the part she could not get over. There had been no withdrawal, no rationing, no farewell week; there had simply been a schedule, and a store that needed closing, and a car with four wrong tires on it, and the ordinary machinery of two working lives, and it would have run exactly the same way whether or not anybody was leaving.

She thought: *this is what it’s going to be like.*

Not the empty house. The empty house was coming and she had braced for it and it would be what it was. This was the other thing, the one nobody warned you about: that from here on, whatever they had would have to be scheduled, and would be subject to shifts and traffic and other people’s Thursdays, and that the days would eat it if she let them.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

At ten-forty he came down.

He had the tape gun in one hand and looked like a man who had been dismantling furniture for four hours, which he was.

“There’s a problem,” he said.

“What kind of problem?”

“The toaster.”

Deb looked at him.

“The what?”

“The toaster.” He put the tape gun on the counter. “It’s on the list for the kitchen box. I put it on the list in February and I have been carrying it around in my head since then and I need to resolve it before tomorrow or I’m going to think about it in the car.”

“Eli, that’s my toaster.”

“That is not your toaster.”

“I have used that toaster every morning for three and a half years.”

“You have used that toaster every morning for three and a half years,” Eli said, “and I bought it. In 2022. From the Meijer on Woodman with my own money because the old one caught fire, which you have never once acknowledged, and I have the receipt.”

“You have the receipt?”

“I have a photograph of the receipt.”

“You photographed a toaster receipt in 2022.”

“I photograph every receipt,” Eli said, “as you would know if you had ever asked me about anything,” and Deb put both hands flat on the table and laughed so hard she had to stop and start again twice.

“That’s the fire toaster.”

“That’s the fire toaster.”

“The one that came after the fire.”

“Yes.”

“And you’ve had it on a list since February and said nothing.”

“I’ve been building up to it,” he said. “It’s a load-bearing appliance in this house. I’m aware of the implications.”

Deb wiped her eyes.

“Take the toaster,” she said.

“I want to be clear I’m not taking it because I need it. I need it, but that’s not why. It’s a matter of principle.”

“Eli.”

“I’m just saying I could buy another toaster.”

“Take the toaster.”

“Okay,” he said.

He picked up the tape gun. Then he put it down again.

“I’m going to leave it,” he said.

“What?”

“I’m going to leave the toaster. Cross it off.” He was already halfway to the hall. “I’ve got a shared kitchen and there’s a toaster in it. Bernice has a toaster. She showed me the toaster.”

“Then why did we just do all that?”

“Because I needed the toaster to be mine,” Eli said, from the hallway. “I didn’t need to take it.”

And Deb sat at her kitchen table at eleven o’clock at night on the twenty-seventh of March, holding a dish towel.

She heard him upstairs, taping a box.

“Eli,” she called.

“What.”

“You get the toaster in the will.”

“That’s grim, Deb.”

“You get it in the will and I’m having it in writing, and it’s going to be the only line in the will, and my son’s going to read it out at the funeral and have absolutely no idea what it means.”

Upstairs, Eli laughed. She heard it clearly, through a ceiling, at eleven o’clock at night.

“That’s actually funny,” he called down. “That’s a genuinely funny thing to do to a guy.”

“I’m going to do it.”

“Do it.”

Then the tape gun again, and the sound of a box going down onto the floor, and Deb sat where she was for a while longer, and then got up and washed a pan that was already clean, and went to bed.


# Chapter 26

[S]{.dropcap}he had not slept, and she had known she would not sleep, and she had made a decision at about one in the morning that she was not going to lie there and manage it.

So at one-fifteen she got up and went down and sat in the front room in the dark.

She did not turn the light on. She sat in the chair by the window with the streetlight coming in and the house entirely quiet, and she looked at the wall for a while.

She thought about a Tuesday in September of 2022 and a boy coming up a driveway with a garbage bag.

She thought about eleven dollars in the second aisle at the Goodwill.

She thought about the first six weeks, which had been the hardest six weeks of the whole three and a half years and which almost nobody knew anything about, when he had been so unfailingly polite that she had lain awake worrying about it, and had called Renee twice, and had been told both times that this was normal and would pass and that the thing to do was nothing.

She thought about the fourteen months he had spent looking for her reason, which she had not known about until a month ago and which reorganized her memory of the entire first two years.

She thought about the day in the second summer when he had come down and asked, out of nowhere, at four in the afternoon, whether she wanted help with the yard, and had then worked in it for three hours without further comment, and how she had understood at the time that something had been decided upstairs.

And at about two-forty she thought, with great clarity:

*You did the job. Whatever you started it for, you did the job, and the job was to make a boy who did not need you, and you have made one, and he is upstairs in an empty room asleep on a mattress you bought him, and in six hours he is going to load it into a truck.*

She sat in the chair a while longer.

Then she went up at three and slept for ninety minutes, and woke at four-fifty, and got up at five.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Saturday the twenty-eighth came up clear and forty-four degrees, which was the warmest morning of the year so far.

She made coffee. She did not make anything else, which took a genuine act of will and which she counted, privately, as a victory.

At six she went down to the basement.

The box was where it had been since January, on the shelf under the stairs next to the Christmas things, with a folded towel over it. She took the towel off. Sixteen dollars of stoneware, off-white with a thin blue band, eight place settings, three of them chipped, in a cardboard box that somebody at the Goodwill had re-taped.

She carried it up. It was heavier than she remembered and she had to put it down twice.

She set it by the front door, next to the boots. Then she got the can opener out of the drawer where it had been living since January, the dollar one with the good handle, and put that on top of the box.

Then she went and got the index cards with the rubber band around them off the counter by the microwave, and put those on top of the can opener.

And then she went and made the coffee cake, because there was a limit to how much a person could be expected to do.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Eli came down at seven-fifteen.

He stopped at the bottom of the stairs.

“What’s this.”

“Dishes.”

He looked at the box. Then at her, in the kitchen doorway with a dish towel over her shoulder.

“Deb.”

“They’re from the Goodwill in January and they cost sixteen dollars and three of them are chipped,” she said. “There’s a can opener. You don’t have a can opener, and you’d have found out about that at about nine o’clock on Tuesday night.”

“That’s true. That’s actually true, I’ve got a list called WEEK ONE and there’s no can opener on it.”

“There’s never a can opener on it.”

He crouched down and looked at the box without touching it.

“How long have these been in the basement?”

“Since January the tenth.”

“You’ve had dishes in the basement since January.”

“Under a towel,” Deb said. “I’d like the towel included in the record.”

Eli picked up the index cards. Turned them over in his hand, the rubber band and the six of them, the chicken and the chili and the soup with the little pasta and the ziti and the enchiladas with the note about why they didn’t work and the coffee cake.

“You did the salt,” he said, reading.

“I did the salt. It’s two teaspoons. It’s been two teaspoons my whole life and I’d have told you one.”

He put the cards in his jacket pocket. Not in a box. In the pocket of the jacket he was wearing.

Then he stood up and picked up the dish box and carried it out to the car without saying anything else about it, and Deb stood in the doorway with a towel over her shoulder and let that be how it went.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

He came back in for the coffee cake, because he was seventeen, and stood at the counter eating a piece of it with his fingers.

“You made the cake.”

“I made the cake at six o’clock this morning because I got up at five and there is a limit to how long a person can sit in a chair.”

“That’s fair.”

They stood in the kitchen. Twenty past seven on the twenty-eighth of March, forty minutes before Trevor was due, with a house that had already had most of its furniture taken out of one room upstairs.

“I want to do the money thing,” Eli said, “and I want to do it now, before he gets here, because if I do it in front of Trevor it’ll be worse.”

“What money thing?”

He put down the cake and got his wallet out and took out four twenties and put them on the counter.

“Deb.”

“That’s for the mattress.”

“That’s not for the mattress.”

“That’s eighty dollars toward a mattress you bought in 2023 for two hundred and forty and I’ve worked out the depreciation and eighty’s about right, and I’d like you to take it, and I’ve been carrying it since Tuesday.”

Deb looked at the four twenties on her counter.

“No,” she said.

“Deb.”

“No. And I’ll tell you exactly why, and then I’d like you to put it back in your wallet and we’ll never mention it.” She folded her arms. “I got a check for you every month for three and a half years. That’s how it works. The state of Ohio paid me a maintenance payment on the fifteenth of every month, and it covered the food and a portion of the utilities and it did not begin to cover a mattress, and I bought the mattress anyway, out of my own money, because you were fifteen and your back hurt.”

“Which is why…”

“Which is why it’s not a debt,” Deb said. “There is no line item in this house where you owe me eighty dollars, and if you walk out of here today having paid me for something, then this becomes a place you rented, and it was not a place you rented.”

Eli stood there.

“I’ve had this planned for four days,” he said.

“I know.”

“I had a whole thing about depreciation.”

“I could see the whole thing about depreciation coming from the other side of the room,” Deb said. “Put it away.”

He put it away.

Then he said, “Can I buy the toaster?”

“Get out of my kitchen.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Trevor arrived at eight with his father’s pickup, which was a 2004 F-150 with a bed liner and a passenger seat full of fast food debris that he cleared by the simple method of putting it all on the floor.

“Mrs. Hale.”

“Trevor. It’s Deb.”

“Okay,” said Trevor, who would call her Mrs. Hale for years after that.

The seven boxes came out of Trevor’s garage. The bed frame, in pieces, went in the truck bed with a moving blanket over it. Eli’s mattress, which was his because Deb had bought it in 2023 and had lost that argument in advance by refusing to have it, went on top.

From the house: two duffel bags, one of them eleven dollars, second aisle, bought in his first week; it had spent March packed under his bed. A milk crate of books. A desk chair. A guitar he had owned for two years and could play four things on, badly, and which he had never played where she could hear him. A laundry basket with his coats in it. The dish box. A plastic tub of things from the bathroom. Two more boxes, taped that week.

That was all of it.

It took forty minutes to load and Deb kept expecting there to be more, and there wasn’t, and at eight-forty they were standing in the driveway with a full pickup and an empty afternoon.

“That’s it?” she said.

“That’s it.”

“Three and a half years.”

“I don’t have a lot of stuff, Deb, I’ve been moving my whole life. Stuff is the enemy.”

“That’s a horrible sentence.”

“It’s a practical sentence,” Eli said, and shut the tailgate.

Neither of them mentioned the Wednesday. It had been three days. The truck was loaded.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Deb went upstairs one last time before they left, on the excuse of checking the closet.

The room was a room. The bed gone, frame and mattress both, and a paler oblong of floorboard where it had stood, and the dresser against the wall with its drawers shut, and four wire hangers on the rail, and a rectangle on the wall where something had hung, and a window with the blind up.

On the dresser, squared to the edge, was a stack about eight inches high. He had stripped the bed himself at some point on the Friday night and folded all of it, sheets and pillowcase and the mattress protector, and left it where she would come across it.

She stood in the middle of it.

She had painted this room in 2020, before any of them, a color the paint chip had called Morning Linen and which was, in practice, white with an apology in it. She had painted it on a weekend by herself with the radio on because a licensing worker was coming in six weeks and the walls had been the color the previous owners left.

Malik had been in here for seven months and had put nothing on the walls at all.

Alyssa had covered that wall by the door in about nine days, entirely, edge to edge, and had taken all of it down herself the night before she went back to her mother, and had left the tape.

Jaylen had had one thing up: a poster of a rapper Deb had never heard of, which he had left behind on purpose, and which she had taken down in 2022 and put in the closet, and it was, she realized standing there, still in the closet on the shelf.

She checked. It was.

And Eli had put up one thing in three and a half years, in the second summer, a printed diagram of an engine that had come free with something, and it had hung in that rectangle until this week.

Four children. Four different relationships with one wall.

She went and got the poster off the closet shelf and looked at it and put it back. Then she picked the stack up off the dresser and carried it down and set it on top of the dryer, and did not say anything about any of it to anybody, then or ever.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Pryor Street took nine minutes.

Bernice Okafor was on the porch when they pulled up, in the cardigan, with a thermos and three mugs on the rail beside her, which she had clearly put there in advance.

“You’re early.”

“Twenty minutes,” Eli said.

“That’s the correct amount of early.” She poured. “Take that up first,” she said, pointing at the mattress. “Do the heavy things while you still like each other.”

They did the heavy things. The mattress went up the stairs sideways with Trevor at the bottom and Eli at the top and a section in the middle that involved the bannister and a great deal of profanity, and the bed frame came up in three trips, and the boxes came up one at a time.

Deb carried the dish box. She carried it up herself, both hands, resting it on the loose third stair on the way, and put it down in the middle of the room by the window.

The lamp was in the closet, exactly as Bernice had said.

It was a floor lamp, brass-colored, with a shade that had gone slightly yellow, and it was, in fact, a good lamp. Eli plugged it in immediately, before anything else was unpacked, before the bed was even assembled, and turned it on in a room with the morning sun coming in the south window, where it made no visible difference at all.

He looked at it for a second.

“It works,” he said.

“It’s a good lamp,” Deb said.

“It’s a good lamp.”

Then he turned it off and got on with the bed.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

By eleven-forty it was done.

The bed was together and made, because Deb had made it while the two of them were bringing up the last of the boxes and neither of them had commented. The desk chair was by the window with nothing to sit at. Four of the boxes were open and five weren’t. The dishes were in the shared kitchen downstairs, in the cupboard Bernice had cleared for him, next to the nursing student’s things.

The shared kitchen downstairs was the last thing they did.

Bernice had cleared a cupboard, the second from the left, and had put a strip of masking tape on the edge of the shelf with ELI on it in marker, which she had plainly done that morning and which she did not mention.

Deb unpacked the dishes into it. Eight place settings, three chipped, and the can opener.

There was a moment where she looked at the cupboard and understood that she was arranging a boy’s plates in another woman’s kitchen, and that this was the last domestic act she would perform for him, and that it was taking about ninety seconds and involved a cardboard box.

Bernice came in behind her with a dishcloth over her shoulder.

“There’s a shelf in the fridge, bottom left, and a bin in the freezer with his name on it. The rest is shared and it works out. Nobody in this house has ever stolen anything except milk, and that’s Martin, and I’ve decided about Martin.”

“Everybody’s decided about Martin.”

“Martin is a good man who takes other people’s milk,” said Bernice, “and I have made my peace and I buy an extra half gallon on Thursdays and we do not speak of it.”

Deb closed the cupboard.

“Thank you for the tape,” she said.

Bernice waved it off entirely.

“They come in with a box,” she said, “and the box goes in a cupboard, and if the cupboard doesn’t have their name on it they’ll eat out of the box for six weeks. I’ve watched it happen twenty times. It’s forty cents of tape.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Trevor left at eleven-fifty, because he had work at one. He shook Eli’s hand in the yard in a way that seventeen-year-olds do, with a kind of formality, and said “text me,” and to Deb said “Mrs. Hale,” and drove off in his father’s truck.

And then it was the two of them in a twelve-by-eleven room off Pryor Street with the sun coming in and boxes on the floor.

Neither of them had planned for this part. That was clear immediately. They had planned the loading and the driving and the carrying, and nobody had put anything after eleven-fifty.

“So,” Eli said.

“So.”

“There’s a thing I have to do at one.”

“What thing?”

“There’s no thing,” he said. “I’m saying that so you’ve got somewhere to go, because I can see you standing there working out how long you’re supposed to stay and I’d rather just hand it to you.”

Deb laughed. It came out of her sideways.

“That’s extremely rude,” she said.

“You’ve been doing the arithmetic since Trevor left. I could hear it.”

“I have been doing the arithmetic since Trevor left.”

“Yeah.”

She looked around the room.

The lamp. The bed she’d made. Nine boxes, four open. A window with a feral rose bush under it that was going to be enormous and terrible by July.

“Right,” she said.

“Deb.”

“I’m going. I’m going. I want to say one thing, and it’s short, and then I’m going and I’m not going to do it in the doorway with my hand on the frame like a woman in a film.”

“Okay.”

She turned around and faced him properly in the middle of the room.

“I’m not going to tell you to call me,” she said. “Everybody’s going to tell you that this week. Renee’s going to tell you, Mrs. Kadeem’s going to tell you, Gary’s going to say some version of it and it’s going to be about hours. So I’m not going to.”

“Okay.”

“But there’s a thing I want, and it’s small, and I’d like it.” She had her hands in her coat pockets and she left them there. “When something goes wrong in here. Not something big. When the water pressure’s terrible or the third stair gets you or you burn something in Bernice’s kitchen in front of a nursing student. Text me the stupid one. That’s it. That’s the whole ask. Everybody’s going to tell you to call when it’s serious, and you won’t, because you’ll have it handled, because you always have it handled.” She shrugged. “So text me the stupid one instead. That’s the one I want.”

Eli stood in the middle of the room.

“Okay,” he said.

“Okay.”

“That’s a good ask,” he said. “That’s a much better ask than the other one.”

“I’ve had a long winter to think about it.”

And then she went. She did not stand in the doorway. She went down the stairs and past the loose third one and out through the enclosed porch, and Bernice Okafor said “Mrs. Hale” from her chair without looking up from a crossword, and Deb said “Deb,” and Bernice said, “Mm,” and turned a page.


# Chapter 27

[S]{.dropcap}he stopped at the Kroger on the way home, which she had not planned to do.

She was three blocks past Pryor Street when she thought about the fact that there was nothing in the house for tonight, and she turned left instead of right, and by the time she was in the parking lot she understood what she was doing and did it anyway.

The Kroger on a Saturday at noon was full of people who had somewhere to be. She got a cart. She went up the produce aisle and down the dairy aisle and stood in front of the meat case for a period of time she could not afterward account for.

She bought: a chicken. Two pounds of ground beef. A bag of onions, which she did not need, because there were four onions at home. Milk. The good bread. A bag of the small pasta. Two cans of the tomatoes, the ones from Italy that were a dollar more. A pound of butter.

At the register the girl said, “Do you need bags?” and Deb said, “Yes,” and then, unprompted, “It’s just me tonight,” and the girl said, “Oh, nice, that’s the best,” and scanned the butter, and Deb stood there with her card in her hand feeling the floor tilt very slightly.

The girl at the register was maybe nineteen and had a name badge that said KENZIE and had been perfectly pleasant, and Deb thought about her on the drive home more than was reasonable.

She had said *it’s just me tonight* out loud, unprompted, to a stranger. There had been no conversational opening for it. The girl had asked about bags and Deb Hale had volunteered her domestic circumstances to a nineteen-year-old who was scanning butter.

She examined herself at the light on Fourth and found no injury at all, which surprised her, and decided at the next light to leave the whole thing open.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The house at one o’clock in the afternoon was full of sun.

That was the first thing, and it was outrageous. She had braced for a house that was dark and instead she opened the door into a hallway with a rectangle of light across the floorboards, because it was the twenty-eighth of March and the sun was further north than it had been in October and it came through the front window at one o’clock now in a way it hadn’t since last spring.

She put the groceries on the counter.

The kitchen was exactly as she’d left it that morning. Coffee cake on the rack, two slices gone. The pool catalogue on the microwave with a five-dollar bill under it. The calendar on the fridge with two red boxes on it, one around the twenty-eighth, which was today, and one around April third, which was Friday.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She put the milk in the fridge. The butter. The beef in the freezer, because there was no possible way she was cooking two pounds of ground beef this week.

She folded the bags and put them in the drawer with the other bags.

Then she washed her hands, and dried them on the towel, and stood at the sink.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She had thought, driving away from Pryor Street, that she would go home and that the house would be the event.

It was not the house. The house was fine. She got in the door and put groceries down and the house was a house, the same one she had left at eight-forty that morning, with a coffee cake on a rack and a calendar on the fridge.

What got her was the car.

Not on the drive. On the drive she was fine, competent, indicating properly, thinking about whether she had bought enough milk.

It was afterward, at about four o’clock, when she went out to bring in the recycling bin and walked past the Escape sitting in the driveway, and saw, through the rear window, on the back seat, a moving blanket folded in quarters that Trevor had handed her at eleven-forty and that she had put in the car without thinking about it.

She stood in her own driveway and looked at a folded blanket on a back seat for slightly too long.

Then she got it out and took it into the garage and put it on the shelf, and brought the recycling bin in, and went inside.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Here is what she did for the rest of that afternoon.

She wiped down the counters, which were clean. She ran the vacuum in the front room. She took the recycling out to the bin at the side of the house and put the lid on properly because of raccoons.

At one-forty the phone rang and it was Sheila Kowalczyk, of all people, about the school board thing in May, and Deb talked to her for ten minutes about a levy renewal and was, for the entire ten minutes, a completely normal woman having a completely normal conversation.

At one-fifty-five she hung up and stood in the hall.

The house did the thing houses do at that hour on a Saturday, which is nothing. The refrigerator. A car. Somewhere in the walls, the specific tick of a place that has been warmed by sun on one side.

She had lived alone in this house for two years before the licensing came through. She kept reminding herself of that, which she recognized as a technique and used anyway. Two years, 2018 to 2020, a woman in a house by herself, and she had managed it, and she had not thought about it as managing at the time.

What she could not make herself remember, standing in the hallway, was what she had done with the afternoons.

She could remember the job and the divorce paperwork and a summer where she painted the front room a color she immediately disliked. She could not remember a single Saturday afternoon from either of those years. Not one. Twenty-two months of Saturday afternoons and there was nothing there at all, and she stood in her hall at five to two and understood that she had not been living in that house so much as waiting in it, and that she had been waiting so continuously that it had not registered as an activity.

She had a thought then that she did not like and could not put down, which was that a person who fills a house with children might be a person who has discovered they cannot be alone in the afternoon.

She went and put the kettle on. It was something to do with a hand.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

At two-fifteen she went upstairs and stood in the doorway of his room.

It was very clean. He had swept it, which she had not seen him do and which he must have done at some point in the four hours after she went to bed on Friday. The bed was gone. The dresser was there, because it was hers, and the closet door was open and the rail was empty except for four wire hangers, and there was a rectangle on the wall where the light had not faded the paint behind something he’d had up.

She had expected to find something. That was what she caught herself doing, standing in the doorway: she was waiting for the moment where she found a thing he’d left behind, a sock, a book, some object that would arrive with a piece of him attached to it.

There was nothing. He had been in the system since he was twelve and he knew how to clear a room.

She closed the door. Then she opened it again, because a closed door in that house at the end of the hall was going to be a thing she’d have to walk past forty times a day, and an open door with an empty room in it was at least honest.

At three she cleaned out the fridge, which had needed it since February.

At four she sat down in the front room with a cup of tea and picked up her phone and looked at it and put it face down on the arm of the chair.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

At four-forty she picked it up again and there was nothing on it.

At five she called Marisol, who did not pick up, which was correct, because Marisol had a book club on the last Saturday of the month that she complained about continuously and never missed.

She left no message.

At five-fifteen she went out to the garage and looked at the gutters from below, and made a note about the gutters, and came back in.

At five-thirty she sat down in the front room again and got out her phone and typed a message to Eli that said *Hope the room is going okay,* and looked at it.

Then she deleted it.

She thought about why for a while afterward. It was a completely reasonable message. It was warm and undemanding and any mother in America could have sent it and no one would think anything of it.

But she had left that house at eleven-fifty and it was now five-thirty, which was five hours and forty minutes, and there was no version of that message that did not also say *it has been five hours and forty minutes.*

She put the phone down.

*He’ll text when there’s something,* she thought. *You asked for the stupid one. Wait for the stupid one.*

It was, she reflected, harder than the coffee cake, and she had spent that same afternoon reorganizing a refrigerator she had already cleaned at three.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

At six she made dinner.

That was the thing she would think about afterward, more than any of it: that she got up at six o’clock on the twenty-eighth of March and made dinner, because it was six o’clock, because that is what six o’clock is for.

She made the chicken with the lemon. She did not decide to make it. She took the chicken out of the bag and put the pan on and got the lemon out of the bowl on the counter and it happened the way it had happened four thousand times, and she was halfway through, standing at the stove, before she registered which dish it was.

She kept going. That felt important.

She did the whole thing properly. She got the pan hot and dried the chicken and salted it, two teaspoons, and got it in there and left it alone for the four minutes you have to leave it alone, and when the lemon went in at the end she scraped the bottom of the pan with the wooden spoon and got every brown bit up off the bottom, which was where forty percent of the flavor of that dish lives.

She thought about the fact that she had never once said that out loud in her own kitchen until ten days ago.

She plated it.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Then she went to the cupboard and got down two plates.

Her hand did it. She was not present for the decision in any meaningful way. She reached up and took two of the everyday plates the way she had reached up and taken two of the everyday plates every night since a Tuesday in September of 2022 when a fourteen-year-old had come into her house with a garbage bag and a duffel he did not yet own, and she turned around with them and got two steps toward the table before her arms understood.

She stood in the middle of her kitchen holding two plates.

There was a period of some seconds.

Then she put one of them back.

She did not put it in the cupboard. She put it on the counter, next to the sink, where it would be in the way, and she turned around and put the other one on the table and put the chicken on it and sat down and ate her dinner.

It was very good. That was not a small thing and it was not a comfort. It was the best pan of it she’d made in a year, and she ate all of it, at a table set for one, at twenty past six in the evening, with the window over the sink going the color it goes.

The house made its noises. The fridge. A car on the street. Somewhere out the back a dog was extremely upset about something and then wasn’t.

She did not cry.

She would, later. Not that night. Twelve days later she would be in the Kroger parking lot on a Thursday afternoon and would put her forehead on the steering wheel for four minutes over absolutely nothing, over a song on the radio she didn’t even like, and it would come out of her sideways in a place with no dignity in it at all, and that would be the shape of it.

But not that night. That night she ate a very good pan of chicken and did the dishes, both plates, the one she’d used and the one she hadn’t, and put them away.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

At eight-forty her phone went off on the arm of the chair.

*bernices kitchen has one (1) fork*

Deb read it three times.

Then she typed: *One?*

*ONE. shared kitchen. four people. bernice says there were six in january and she “doesnt want to talk about it”*

*You have eight forks. They’re in the box with the dishes.*

*i know. i found them. thats not the point deb. the point is i live in a house where four adults have been operating on one fork since january and NOBODY SAID ANYTHING*

*What are you going to do?*

*im going to say something*

*Don’t.*

*im saying something*

Deb sat in her front room in a house with the lights off except the one by the chair and laughed until she had to put the phone down on her knee.

Then she picked it up and typed, *Text me how it goes,* and he sent back a thumbs up, and that was all of it, the whole exchange, ninety seconds on a Saturday night about cutlery.

She put the phone face down and sat in the chair for a while longer with her tea gone cold.

Upstairs, the door to the empty room was open.

At ten she went to bed, and lay there in the dark listening to a house with one person in it, and thought, with a clarity that surprised her, that she was going to be all right, eventually, in the way people are, and that eventually was going to take a great deal longer than anybody was going to expect it to, including her.

She was asleep by eleven, which she had not managed in three weeks.


# Chapter 28

[T]{.dropcap}he house on the Saturday night had been survivable and she had gone to bed at ten and slept, which she had not expected and had taken as a good sign, and which turned out to have been a loan rather than a gift.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Sunday was the worst one, and she had not expected that either, because Sunday was the day she had braced for and the braced-for ones are never the ones that get you.

It got her anyway. She woke at five-forty out of habit and lay there for forty minutes doing nothing, and then got up and made coffee and stood in a kitchen with an entire day in it.

She cleaned the gutters. This was a job that had been on the list since October and that she had been told repeatedly by three separate people not to do herself at fifty-four with a ladder, and she did it on the last Sunday in March in work gloves, alone, and it took two and a half hours, and it was disgusting, and it was the best decision she made that week.

Mrs. Petrosyan came out onto her porch at some point and shouted, “You should not be up there.”

“I know.”

“Your husband should do it.”

“I don’t have a husband, Mrs. Petrosyan.”

“I know,” said Mrs. Petrosyan, with enormous satisfaction, and went back inside.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Monday she went back to work, which saved her, in the specific way work saves people.

Four hundred and twelve trays. The cooler threw a fit and she fixed it. Craig from the PTO emailed the entire staff a spreadsheet about the pancake supper’s profit margin and Marisol printed it out and drew a hat on it.

Dominic Reyes came through the line and asked for extra rice.

“No.”

“You look tired.”

“I am tired.”

“Is that why no rice?”

“No, Dominic, the rice is because of policy, the tired is a separate issue,” Deb said, and gave him extra rice, and he was so shocked that he stopped dead in the line and said, “Wait, what happened,” and she said, “Move along,” and he went off down the line looking at his tray like it might be a trap.

Marisol, at the second station, said nothing at all and put her hand flat on Deb’s back for about two seconds as she went past.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Monday afternoon she stayed late and did the walk-in inventory that was not due until Friday.

She did it properly, with the clipboard and the count sheets, alone in a cooler at four in the afternoon with the building emptying out around her, and it took ninety minutes and she found three discrepancies and a case of chicken patties that had been mis-shelved since February and would have gone past date in six days.

Marisol found her at five-fifteen, still in there, coat on over her apron.

“You know it’s Monday.”

“I’m aware.”

“The inventory’s Friday.”

“The inventory’s now.”

Marisol leaned on the door frame.

“Deb.”

“I’m going to do a thing,” Deb said, “and I’d like you to not make it a whole conversation, because if it’s a conversation I won’t do it.”

“Okay.”

“I’m going to stay late two days a week for a while.” She turned a page on the clipboard. “Not because I can’t be in the house. I can be in the house, I’ve been in the house, I ate a very good dinner in it on Saturday. It’s that there’s about nine things in this kitchen I’ve been doing badly for four years because I was leaving at five to get home, and it turns out I’ve got the time now, and I’d rather use it on the chicken patties than sit in the front room deciding whether I’m all right.”

Marisol was quiet for a second.

“Two days,” she said.

“Two days.”

“Not five.”

“Not five.”

“Because I’ve seen five, Deb. I’ve seen a woman do five and then it’s 2031 and she’s got a countdown on her phone and a spare set of clothes in the office.”

“Two days,” Deb said. “And you’re eating at my table on Wednesdays as well as Fridays, which is not negotiable, because I’ve already bought the chicken.”

Marisol pushed off the door frame.

“Fine,” she said. “Give me the other clipboard, we’ll do the dry store while we’re at it, and then I’m going home to my cat, who I resent.”

They did the dry store. It took another forty minutes. They found a case of the good rolls that had been ordered in error and would otherwise have gone to waste, and Deb put them on Wednesday’s menu on the spot, and four hundred and twelve children got a better roll than they were scheduled to get, and nobody ever knew.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Danny’s Tuesday calls would, over the weeks that followed, develop a shape.

He would call at seven-fifteen. That was not negotiated; it simply became a fact, and Deb would begin, by the third one, to be finished with dinner by ten past.

They would talk for between twenty and thirty-five minutes. They would talk about his work, which she could now follow in outline: he was on a commercial job in Grandview that had gone badly from the design stage and which he described with the specific relish of a man who had been proved right by a duct.

They would talk about the school. She would tell him about Dominic Reyes, which would take two calls, and Danny would laugh in a way she had not heard since he was a teenager.

They would not talk about 2019. They would not talk about Frank. They would not talk about Casey.

And on the fourth call he would ask whether she’d had the furnace looked at, and on the fifth how old the water heater was, and Deb would understand by then what was happening: that a twenty-nine-year-old man who installed heating systems for a living was conducting an audit of his mother’s house one item at a time, over the telephone, from an hour and forty away, and had no other available vocabulary in which to conduct it.

She would let him. She would answer every question and write two of them down.

It was, she would recognize, exactly what Eli had done to her water heater in the last week of March, and neither of them had any idea the other one was doing it. *I am being audited,* she would think, *by two men who love me, and neither of them can say a sentence.*

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Tuesday night Danny called.

He called at seven-fifteen, which was when he called, and they talked for thirty-one minutes, and it was the longest phone call they had had since 2019.

He talked about a job in Grandview. She talked about the gutters, and he was so angry about the gutters that she had to hold the phone away from her ear.

“You got on a ladder.”

“I got on a ladder.”

“Alone. Nobody there. At fifty-four.”

“I’ve been getting on ladders since before you were born.”

“That’s not a defense, that’s the problem,” Danny said, and then, after a second, in a different voice, “Call me next time. I’m an hour and forty. I’d have come up and done it in an hour with a leaf blower.”

Deb sat down on the arm of the chair.

“You’d drive an hour and forty to do my gutters.”

“Yeah.”

“Danny.”

“Don’t make it a thing.”

“I’m not making it a thing.”

“You’re making a face, I can hear the face.”

“There is no face,” Deb said, with a face.

There was a pause, and then he said, “So he moved out Saturday, right? The kid.”

She had told him. Last week, on a Tuesday call, in about four sentences, and he had said “huh” and asked a question about the auto parts store and they had moved on.

“He did.”

“How’s that.”

“It’s…” She looked at the ceiling. “It’s a very quiet house.”

“Yeah,” Danny said.

Neither of them said the obvious thing. Neither of them said any version of it, and Deb understood, sitting on the arm of a chair with her son on the phone, that they were not going to say it for a long time, possibly years, and that this was survivable, and that they had both just chosen not to say it at exactly the same moment.

“Anyway,” Danny said. “Don’t get on the ladder.”

“I’ve already been on the ladder.”

“Don’t get on it *again*.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Tuesday afternoon, before the call, she did a thing she had been putting off since Saturday, which was to wash the sheets.

They had been sitting on top of the dryer since eight-forty on the Saturday morning, folded in a stack eight inches high by a boy at some point on the Friday night and left squared to the edge of a dresser in an empty room. That was the only part of the whole business that had come close to undoing her on the day, and she had carried it down two flights and put it on the dryer and walked past it for three days.

She put all of it in the machine and ran it and it took an hour and ten minutes, and she sat downstairs for that hour and ten minutes doing nothing whatsoever, and when it was done she put the sheets in the dryer and then folded them again at nine o’clock at night on the kitchen table.

And then she had a decision, which was where to put them.

There was a linen closet. That was where sheets went, obviously, and there was a shelf in it with the spare sets on it and a gap where these ones lived.

She put them back in the gap.

Then she stood in the hallway with the closet open, looking at a folded set of sheets that fit a bed that was currently in a room off Pryor Street, and closed the door, and went downstairs, and did not think about it again for two months, at which point Marisol’s sister needed a spare set for a visiting nephew and Deb gave them away without hesitating and felt absolutely nothing about it, which surprised her.

That was the thing nobody told you either. You could not predict a single one of them. A folded stack of sheets on a dresser had nearly finished her on a Saturday. Giving the sheets away in June was nothing at all. And in November, for no reason, she would find a receipt from AutoWorks in a coat pocket and have to sit down on the stairs.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Thursday was April second, and the phone did not go off all day, and she noticed that, and made herself notice that she was noticing, and went to bed at nine-forty.

At ten-fifty it went off.

*i said something about the fork*

Deb sat straight up in bed.

*And?*

*so it turns out the nursing student (Priya) ALSO thought it was insane and had been buying plastic ones and hiding them in her room. for three months.*

*Three months?*

*THREE MONTHS DEB. she has a drawer. i have seen the drawer.*

*What did Bernice say?*

*bernice said and i quote “well nobody TOLD me” which i want to point out is not an explanation of where five forks went*

*Where did they go?*

*nobody knows. bernice will not be drawn on it. priya thinks it was the guy before me. bernice says he was “very good about the yard” which is not a denial*

Deb typed: *You’ve been there five days and you’ve solved a three-month standoff about forks.*

*i put my eight in the drawer. we have nine forks now. priya nearly cried. she said “you brought FORKS?” like id brought a horse*

*They were sixteen dollars.*

*i know. i told her. she didnt care.*

There was a pause of about a minute, and then:

*hearing’s tomorrow*

*I know.*

*9:40*

*I know. I’m bringing the thermos.*

*the coffee in there is genuinely a crime*

*There’s a petition.*

*i know about the petition. gary’s on the petition. gary told me about the petition today unprompted and got genuinely worked up about it*

*Gary’s on the petition?*

*gary’s on the petition TWICE*

Deb lay in the dark in her bedroom on the second of April holding a phone above her face and laughing in a way that made the bed shake.

Then she typed: *Do you want a ride tomorrow?*

The three dots came up. Went away. Came up again.

*no. im good. i’ll meet you there.*

*Okay.*

*not because of anything. i just want to drive there.*

*I know,* Deb typed. *Go to sleep.*

*you go to sleep*

*I was asleep.*

*no you werent*

*I was asleep and you woke me up with forks.*

*worth it,* he sent, and then, thirty seconds later, *goodnight deb*

She put the phone on the nightstand.

The house was very quiet, in the way it now was, and would be, and she lay there and looked at the ceiling and thought about a nursing student with a drawer full of plastic forks and about the fact that in eleven hours she was going to sit in the back of courtroom 4B with a thermos and watch a magistrate ask a boy a question he had already answered in a driveway with a tire gauge in his hand.

She slept badly, and it did not matter, and she was up at four-fifty anyway.


# Chapter 29

[S]{.dropcap}he had gotten the thermos out on the Tuesday night and washed it, which was unnecessary, and stood in the kitchen at nine-fifteen with a clean thermos on the counter.

Then she had made a decision about the coffee, which she had thought about for longer than a person should think about coffee.

She could take the good stuff. She had the good stuff, the bag Marisol brought at Christmas, three quarters gone. It was a hearing, it was the third of April, it was arguably an occasion.

She made the ordinary coffee. The same coffee she had made every morning since about 2016, out of the red can, which cost nine dollars for a tub that lasted six weeks and which nobody had ever described as good.

She was quite clear with herself about why. The good coffee would have been a gesture, and a gesture would have to be noticed, and being noticed was exactly what nobody in that hallway was going to need.

He noticed anyway. She would find that out four hours later, standing outside courtroom 4B, and it would take her most of the drive to work to get over it.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The Montgomery County Juvenile Court building was four stories of 1974 concrete with a flagpole out front and a parking situation that had been a known local grievance for fifty years.

Deb got there at nine-oh-five. Eli’s Civic was already in the lot, third row, backed into the space, because of course it was.

He was in the hallway outside 4B on one of the wooden benches that ran the length of it, in a collared shirt she had not seen before and had not bought him.

“That’s a new shirt.”

“It’s from Kohl’s. It was nineteen dollars.”

“It’s a good shirt.”

“Bernice ironed it,” Eli said. “I want to be clear that I did not ask her to iron it. I was ironing it in the kitchen and she came in and watched me for about ten seconds and then took the iron off me without saying anything.”

Deb sat down on the bench next to him and got the thermos out of her bag and two paper cups, and poured.

The hallway smelled like floor wax and old radiator. Down at the far end a woman in a suit was talking quietly to a man who was crying without making any noise about it, and the two of them were pretending it wasn’t happening, and so was everybody else, which was, Deb thought, the entire architecture of a building like this.

“Nine forty,” Eli said.

“They’re running late. They’re always running late. Marisol’s cousin worked here, they’ve never started a docket on time in the history of the building.”

“How late?”

“Twenty minutes. Maybe more.”

He nodded and drank the coffee.

Renee came down the hall at nine-twenty with a folder and her good coat on and a woman Deb didn’t know, in her thirties, who turned out to be the guardian ad litem, a lawyer named Ms. Ferris who shook Eli’s hand and said, “We spoke on the phone. You’re the one who had questions.”

“I had four questions.”

“You had four questions and you’d written them down,” Ms. Ferris said. “I’ve been doing this six years. Nobody writes them down.”

They went in at nine-fifty-one.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

They had forty-six minutes in the hallway and Deb spent most of them not talking, which took effort.

The bench was hard in the specific way of institutional wood, and the radiator under the window put out a heat that had nothing to do with the temperature outside, and people came and went. A man in work boots with a folder. Two women who were plainly a mother and a grandmother, sitting six feet apart and not speaking. A young father with a toddler who had brought an entire bag of small toys and was deploying them strategically.

Down at the far end, the woman in the suit and the man who had been crying went into a courtroom and the door closed behind them.

“That’s the other side of the building,” Eli said.

“What?”

“That’s a removal hearing or a permanent custody thing. That’s what those look like.” He said it without any weight at all, the way you’d identify a make of car. “You can tell by how many lawyers.”

Deb looked down the hallway.

“How do you know that?”

“Because I’ve been in that room,” Eli said. “Twice. Once when I was twelve, and once about a year later when it got finalized.”

The radiator ticked.

“I don’t remember much about the first one,” he said. “I remember there was a fish tank in the waiting area and there isn’t one now, and I remember somebody bought me a Sprite out of the machine, and I remember the sound the door made.” He was looking at his coffee. “And I remember it was March, because there was salt all down the hall from everybody’s boots and somebody had put cardboard down.”

He turned the cup a quarter turn on his knee.

“And the second one I remember all of, because I was thirteen and they let me sit in, and it took about twenty minutes, and at the end of it a woman I’d never met before signed a piece of paper and my mother wasn’t my mother anymore in the way the state means it.”

The radiator ticked.

Deb did not move.

“That’s the one,” she said. “From that Tuesday.”

“That’s the one.”

Neither of them went any further into it, in a hallway, with ten minutes to go.

“I wanted you in the building for this one anyway,” he said. “Because in about fifteen minutes we’re going into a room like that one and it’s going to be nothing, and I wanted you to know the difference.” He drank some coffee. “This one’s nothing. I want to be really clear about that. Today is an administrative event. The one when I was thirteen was the other kind, and I’ve been to both now, and I’d rather have a Friday with a form on it.”

The toddler down the hall dropped something plastic and shrieked with delight.

“Do you think about her?” Deb asked.

“Sometimes. Less than people assume.” He turned the cup. “She’s alive. She’s in Springfield, or she was in 2022. Renee’s told me what she’s allowed to tell me and I’ve never asked for the rest, and that’s a decision I’ve made about nine times and I keep making it the same way.”

“That’s allowed.”

“I know it’s allowed. That’s why I keep making it.” He looked at the door of 4B. “Maybe when I’m thirty. That’s what I tell myself, and I think it’s probably a lie I’ve agreed with myself about, and I’m fine with it for now.”

At nine-forty-one a woman came out of 4B and called a name that was not theirs, and a man with a folder stood up, and the morning went on.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Courtroom 4B was small and low-ceilinged and had eleven chairs in the gallery, and Deb sat in the back row of it with the thermos in her bag and her hands in her lap.

It took nineteen minutes.

Magistrate Meehan was maybe sixty, with reading glasses on a chain and a manner that Deb recognized immediately as belonging to a person who has done a thing ten thousand times and has decided, deliberately, not to let it become nothing. She called the case number. She confirmed who was present. She noted for the record that the youth was present and represented.

Renee read the report.

It was, as promised, a form. Placement stability. Educational progress. Employment. Independent living skills assessment. Medical and dental current. The words came out in the flat, careful register of a professional reading a document into a record, and it took perhaps four minutes, and there was one sentence in the middle of it that Deb did not hear coming.

*The youth has resided in the same placement since September of 2022, a duration of three years and seven months, which is the longest continuous placement in this youth’s case history and, in this caseworker’s assessment, the primary factor in the stability reflected throughout this report.*

Renee did not look up when she read it.

Meehan did. Meehan looked over her glasses at the back of the room, at a woman in her good coat with a thermos in her bag, for about a second and a half, and then looked back down at her file.

Nobody said anything about it. It went into the record and the record moved on.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Then the questions.

“Eli, I’m going to ask you some things directly. You can take your time.”

“Yes, ma’am.”

“You’ll be eighteen on the nineteenth of this month.”

“Yes.”

“You’ve moved out of your foster placement as of the twenty-eighth of March, to a room at an address on Pryor Street, which the agency has confirmed.”

“Yes.”

“Was that your decision?”

“Yes.”

“Was any pressure placed on you to leave that placement before your birthday, by anyone, including your foster parent?”

“No, ma’am.”

“You understand I have to ask that.”

“I do. The answer’s no. It was my idea and I picked the date.” A beat. “She didn’t like it.”

There was a small movement of air in the courtroom that on another day, in another room, would have been a laugh.

“I imagine not,” said Magistrate Meehan. “Next question. Do you consent to the filing of a petition for adoption?”

Eli said, “No, ma’am.”

“You understand that no adoption can proceed without your consent, and that this is entirely your decision, and that nothing that happens in this courtroom today can change that.”

“I understand.”

“And you understand that if you change your mind, at any point before your birthday, you can tell Ms. Ostrowski or Ms. Ferris or you can write to this court directly, and it will be heard.”

“Yes.”

“Is your answer no?”

“My answer’s no,” Eli said.

He did not look at Deb.

She had spent two months preparing a face for a moment she had been told, by a woman who knew about it, would arrive when he turned around. And he did not turn around, and did not look at her, not once, and she understood in that instant that he had decided that in advance, in a driveway or a kitchen or a car, and that not looking at her was the entire kindness, because looking would have made her into part of the answer.

She sat in the back row with a face she had practiced for nothing and was never so grateful for anything in her life.

“Thank you,” said Magistrate Meehan. “Next. You’re eligible for the Bridges program, which is voluntary extended care to age twenty-one. Ms. Ostrowski tells me you’ve been provided with the information and have declined to sign a voluntary participation agreement at this time. Is that correct?”

“That’s correct.”

“Do you want to sign one today?”

“No, ma’am.”

She had had one line’s warning, out of Renee’s report, in the flat voice you read a form in. It was not enough.

He had promised her in February that she would not hear the adoption answer for the first time in a room with a magistrate in it, and he had kept that, and he had kept it so completely that it had never once occurred to her she had not heard the other answer from him at all. Nine days. Left at a sink on a Wednesday night by a boy who would not hand it to a dish towel.

Deb sat in the back row and looked at the back of his head.

“You understand that door stays open until you turn twenty-one. Not today, not on your birthday. Twenty-one. You can walk into that agency in eighteen months and sign it.”

“I understand. Ms. Ostrowski’s told me four times.”

“Ms. Ostrowski is very good at her job,” said Magistrate Meehan, “and I’d like the record to reflect that I’m telling you a fifth time.”

“Understood.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Renee’s report had seven other paragraphs in it and Deb heard all of them.

Educational: on track to graduate, GPA, no attendance concerns. Employment: part-time, twenty-four hours weekly, employer contacted and confirmed. Independent living: assessed, transportation secured, banking established, documents obtained.

*Documents obtained.* Four minutes of a form and one line about a boy who had made a handwritten list headed DOCUMENTS I NEED AND WHERE THEY ARE when he was fifteen years old.

Medical and dental current. Behavioral: no concerns noted. Contact with biological family: none, at the youth’s stated preference, reviewed and documented at each visit since 2022.

That one went past in about two seconds and Deb felt it land in the room and go.

Placement stability, and then the sentence Renee had written in herself, and then the rest of the form.

And it occurred to Deb, sitting in the eleven chairs at the back with a thermos in her bag, that she was hearing the only account of those three and a half years that would ever exist in any permanent form anywhere.

There would be no other record. Nobody was writing this down. There were four photographs on her phone from three and a half years, because he did not like being photographed and she had respected that. There was no album. There were no report cards in a box in her basement, because his report cards went in a file the county kept.

What there was, was this. Four minutes of a woman reading a form in a small room, into a machine, for a file.

And one sentence in the middle of it that a caseworker had put there on purpose, at some risk, because there was no box for it.

Deb sat in the back row and did not move, and decided she would say something about it in the parking lot in about fifteen minutes.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The rest was housekeeping. Medicaid to twenty-six, confirmed. The education and training voucher, confirmed. Contact information for the independent living coordinator, provided and acknowledged. Jurisdiction to terminate on the youth’s eighteenth birthday, April nineteenth, at which point the case would close.

Meehan signed something. She had, Deb noticed, a fountain pen, which struck her as an act of enormous dignity in a building like that.

And then Magistrate Meehan took her glasses off.

“Off the record for a second,” she said, and the woman at the recorder’s station stopped typing.

She looked at Eli.

“I’ve been doing this nineteen years,” she said. “In this room, on any given morning, I see young people who have been failed by nearly every adult they have ever met. That’s the job. That’s most of the docket.” She set her glasses on the file. “You came in here with a room and a job and a certificate program and a lawyer whose questions you’d written down. That is not most of this docket, and I wanted to say so out loud in front of the people who came with you.”

Eli did not say anything.

“That’s all,” said Magistrate Meehan. “Back on the record. We’re adjourned.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She caught Renee by the vending machines while Eli was talking to Ms. Ferris.

“I want to say a thing and it’ll take ten seconds.”

“Okay.”

“You wrote that sentence.”

Renee shifted the folder.

“I wrote a sentence.”

“There’s no box for it. He’ll have noticed. He’s been in that room before.” Deb had her hands in her coat pockets. “You did that on a form that goes in a file, with your name on it, and there’s a version of this where somebody upstairs tells you the report’s not the place for editorializing.”

“There’s a version of that, yes.”

“So thank you.”

Renee looked at the vending machine for a second.

“Can I tell you what that sentence is actually for?”

“Please.”

“It’s not for you.” She said it without any unkindness. “It’s not for him either, though I’m glad he clocked it. That file follows him. Not far and not forever, but it follows him, and there are people who will read it in the next three years, at the college, at the agency if he ever comes back for Bridges, if he ever needs something.” She tapped the folder. “And every one of them will be reading a document that is otherwise entirely a list of things that were done to a child by institutions. Placement. Assessment. Review. Review. Review.”

“And now there’s a sentence.”

“Now there’s a sentence saying a person was there and it mattered,” Renee said. “In the middle of it. In the same font.” She got her keys out. “That’s the whole trick. That’s the only thing I’ve figured out in twenty-two years that I’d call a technique. You put one true sentence in the file and you make it survive the format.”

She started toward the doors.

“Renee.”

“Mm?”

“Twenty minutes behind the library in Miamisburg,” Deb said. “That’s not enough.”

“No,” said Renee. “It is not,” and went out into the parking lot.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

In the hallway afterward it was ordinary. That was the strangest part. Renee said something about paperwork and gave him a card he already had. Ms. Ferris shook his hand again and told him to call if anything came up before the nineteenth, and then said, “It won’t,” and left.

Eli and Deb stood in the hall by the wooden benches.

“So that’s it,” he said.

“That’s it.”

“That was nineteen minutes.”

“You told me it would be.”

“I said give or take. I didn’t think it would be exactly nineteen.” He looked back at the door of 4B. “Three and a half years. Nineteen minutes.”

“Yes.”

He was quiet for a second.

“She read the thing,” he said. “Renee. The sentence.”

“She did.”

“That’s not on the form. I’ve seen the form. Trevor’s cousin was in care, I’ve seen four of those reports.” He was still looking at the door. “There’s no line for that. She wrote it in.”

“I know,” Deb said.

Deb thought of a phone call in a school parking lot in February, and Renee saying *I’ll make it eleven.*

Eli put his hands in his pockets.

Then he said, “I owe you the other one.”

“You don’t owe me anything. I believe that’s more or less the whole title of the business.”

“Don’t do that.” He said it without heat. “I told you I had an answer and then I moved out and let a woman in a robe hand it to you. That’s not how I wanted it and I’ve known it since about Tuesday.”

A door opened down the hall and closed again.

“Everything you said at that sink was true,” he said. “The money’s true, the certificate’s true, the eleven thousand dollars is true. I know because I did that math in October, before you did, and about nine times since, and it comes out the same every time. The smart move was to sign it and stay in your house until I’m twenty.”

“So why didn’t you?”

“Because I’d take it.”

He was looking at the double doors at the far end.

“That’s the whole answer. If I’d signed that form I’d have stayed in that room and eaten at that table and had somebody’s chicken in a fridge with foil on it, and in two years I’d have been twenty and I’d have had no idea whether I could do any of it.” He shifted his weight off one foot. “I’d have fixed things and paid you rent you didn’t want, and every day of it there’d have been a thing running underneath where I’m checking whether I’m still worth the room.”

“You’d never have had to…”

“I know I wouldn’t have had to. That’s not something you’d do. That’s something I’d do.” He looked at her then. “That’s the part I need you to have. You never once made me feel like I was on trial in that house. Not one time in three and a half years. And I still check. Every day. It’s automatic, it’s like the counting. It doesn’t turn off because a person is kind to me.”

Deb stood in a hallway with an empty thermos in her bag.

“So it was never whether it was smart,” he said. “It was whether I wanted two more years of finding out. And every year I stay is a year I’m more sure I can’t.”

“That’s the most frightening thing anybody has ever said to me.”

“Yeah.”

“I’d have signed it. If you’d said yes that night I’d have called Renee at nine o’clock and I’d have been unbearable about it.”

“I know.”

“I had a good week,” Deb said. “That’s all that was. I had a good week and I got greedy.”

He almost smiled. Then it went.

“One more and then I’ll go.” He got his keys out of his pocket and held them. “The eleven thousand dollars is real. I want to say out loud that I’m walking away from eleven thousand dollars, because if I don’t say it, then in about four years I’ll be twenty-two and something will go wrong and I’ll be standing in a kitchen somewhere doing the math backwards and it’ll feel like a thing that happened to me.”

“And if you say it now?”

“Then it’s a thing I did.” He shrugged. “I’d rather be a guy who chose to be eleven thousand dollars poorer than a guy who missed out on eleven thousand dollars. Those are the same money and they’re completely different men.”

Deb put a hand on the back of one of the wooden benches.

“That’s going to be hard when it comes,” she said.

“Yeah.”

“You’ll be twenty-two and the transmission will go and there won’t be a slack line big enough.”

“Yeah.”

“And you’ll remember standing in this hallway.”

“That’s the idea,” Eli said. “I want to remember standing here, because the version where I don’t remember it is the version where I get bitter, ten years from now, when I can’t tell the difference between a thing I chose and a thing that was done to me, and there’s no way to go back and check.”

The radiator under the window came on.

“Okay,” he said. “I’ve got a one o’clock.”

“Is there a one o’clock?”

“There’s genuinely a one o’clock. Gary’s got me on inventory. It’s the alternators again, a different four.”

“Go.”

He got about six steps down the hall, and then turned around and came back, and said, “The coffee was fine, by the way. In the thermos. It was good coffee.”

“It’s the same coffee I make every day.”

“Yeah,” Eli said. “I know.”

And then he went, out through the double doors and into the parking lot, and Deb stood in the hallway of the Montgomery County Juvenile Court building with an empty thermos and watched him go through a window, and then went out to her car, and sat in it, and drove to work, and did the afternoon prep, and got home at five-forty.

Sixteen days until his birthday.

Nothing about the house was different, and everything in the file was closed, and both of those had happened in nineteen minutes on a Friday morning in April.


# Chapter 30

[A]{.dropcap}pril did the thing April does in that part of Ohio, which is nothing for two weeks and then everything in four days.

The first week was gray and forty-one degrees and could have been March. The second week the trees along Route 4 went from bare to that first green that isn’t a color yet, and the fields turned, and the whole town smelled like cut grass by the eleventh, and the light in the kitchen at seven in the evening was suddenly a different light entirely.

Deb noticed all of it, which she had not expected to.

She had assumed that a woman in an empty house would experience spring as an insult. It was, in fact, the opposite: the season arrived on schedule and was completely indifferent to her, and there was something restful in being of no interest to it. The rose bush on Pryor Street was doing whatever it was doing. The one in her own yard, which she had cut back badly in November, was coming anyway.

She had the windows open by the ninth, three weeks earlier than usual, because there was nobody in the house to say it was cold.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The empty house did not become a metaphor, which Deb was grateful for.

It became a set of small logistical facts, arriving one at a time over about three weeks, most of them stupid.

The hot water lasted twice as long. That was the first one, and she noticed it on the second Tuesday and stood in the shower an extra four minutes out of a sort of grim scientific interest. The laundry went from three loads a week to one, and she did that one on a Sunday and it was done by ten in the morning and then there was a Sunday. The milk went off. That happened twice before she adjusted, half a gallon poured down the sink on a Thursday, and the second time she stood there with the carton and said, out loud, in an empty kitchen, “Well, obviously,” and bought the small ones after that.

She got the windshield done. It took forty minutes, exactly as the kid at the counter had said it would in March, and it cost her nothing because the insurance covered the whole thing, and she sat in a waiting room with a magazine from 2023 and came out to a car she could see the road through. She did not think anything about it. She drove home and put the groceries away, and it was three days before she noticed she had stopped looking at the lower left corner of her own windshield at every red light on Route 4, the way you stop putting your tongue in the gap after a tooth comes out.

She still cooked too much. She had known that one was coming and had planned for it and it happened anyway, every single time, because it turned out that the amount of chicken a person buys is not a decision, it is a reflex located somewhere below the level of intention.

So she got containers. That was the solution she landed on, and it was not elegant, and it worked. Four portions in the freezer every time she cooked, labeled with masking tape and a marker, and by the third week the deep freeze in the basement had fourteen meals in it and she had begun, without quite admitting to it, to think of them as something other than leftovers.

She gave two of them to Mrs. Petrosyan, who received them with the air of a woman being handed a tax bill, and then, four days later, sent back the containers washed with a foil-covered plate on top of them.

That became a thing. Neither of them ever discussed it.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The first Monday back at work after the move, Bev cornered her by the dish pit.

“Somebody said your boy moved out.”

“He did.”

“That’s it, then? That’s the last one?”

“I don’t know yet.”

Bev was sixty-three and had worked in that kitchen since 1999 and had, over twenty-seven years, developed the specific tactlessness of a woman who has decided she has earned it.

“My sister did fostering,” she said. “Down in Hamilton. Six years, same as you. She stopped in about 2016.”

“Why’d she stop?”

“Her husband got sick, mostly. But she told me after that she’d been ready to stop for about two years and hadn’t known how, because there’s no way to stop.” Bev put a tray in the rack. “That’s what she said. There’s no retirement from it. Nobody gives you a card. You just don’t call them one year and then you’re not one anymore, and nobody notices except you.”

Deb stood with a clipboard.

“That’s grim, Bev.”

“It’s just true. You’re in a job with a leaving do at the end. I’ve got a leaving do in June, they’re doing it in the library and Marisol’s making the sheet cake and I’ve already told her not to do the frosting she does.” Bev shrugged. “You don’t get one for the other thing. That’s all I’m saying. So if you stop, you’ll have to do something for yourself about it, because nobody else is going to.”

She went off with the tray rack.

Deb wrote *chicken patties, three cases, Thursday* on her clipboard and did not think about it again until eleven that night, when she thought about it for a long time.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The Kroger happened on the ninth.

It was a Thursday afternoon and she had gone in for three things and come out with a bag, and she got into the Escape and put the bag on the passenger seat and started the car, and the radio came on partway through a song she had never liked, a song she would have changed the station on any other day of her life.

She got about four seconds into it.

Then she put her forehead on the steering wheel and cried in a way she had not cried since the divorce, in a parking lot, in daylight, with a bag of groceries on the seat beside her and her hands still on the wheel at ten and two.

It lasted about four minutes. It was extremely ugly and made a great deal of noise and there was no dignity in it anywhere. A woman two cars over got into her own car and very deliberately did not look, which Deb registered dimly and was grateful for.

Then it stopped, the way they do, and she sat back and looked at herself in the rearview mirror and said, “Oh, for God’s sake,” and found a napkin in the glovebox from a drive-through in 2022 and fixed her face with it.
