She drove home. She put the groceries away. She made a perfectly ordinary dinner and ate about half of it and put the rest in a container.

It happened again in June, once, on a Sunday afternoon, over nothing. And once in August, in the school kitchen, over a tray of rice, which she got a handle on in about ninety seconds and which Marisol pretended not to see.

Less after that. That was all it ever was: less after that.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The texts settled into a rhythm inside about two weeks, and the rhythm was not what she would have designed.

They were short. There were between one and four a day, and about ninety percent of them were stupid, and roughly none of them were about anything.

*bernice has opinions about the recycling*

*the rose bush is coming for the window*

*martin says the water pressure is better on the first floor and i think hes lying*

*priya has a night shift rotation starting in may. she says the house is loud in the daytime. deb is our house loud in the daytime*

She replied to all of them. That was her whole policy and she had arrived at it in the first week: reply to everything, ask for nothing, never be the one who starts it.

She kept to it for twelve days and then broke it on the thirteenth by sending him a photograph of a bird on the fence, which he replied to with *thats a starling. theyre awful,* and a small door opened in the policy and she permitted herself, thereafter, one unprompted stupid thing a week.

She was aware of the absurdity of a woman of fifty-four running a rationing system on her own text messages. She ran it anyway. It was the only mechanism available for the thing she was actually doing, which was learning, at speed, in real time, how to be in someone’s life without being the weather in it.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Marisol came Wednesdays and Fridays now. Fridays had been the arrangement for eleven years and the Wednesdays were new, and neither of them made anything of the fact that the two together now constituted the entirety of the week’s cooking-for-someone.

The third Wednesday, over the ziti, she said, “You haven’t called her.”

“No.”

“It’s been eighteen days.”

“I’m aware of how many days it’s been.”

“I’m not checking on you,” Marisol said. “I’m noting it out loud so that you have heard somebody notice, because you’ll have decided by now that nobody has.”

Deb pushed the ziti around.

“I got the folder out,” she said.

“What folder?”

“The licensing folder. The renewal’s in October. It’s in the file box in the front room and I got it out on Sunday and put it on the table and looked at it.”

“And?”

“And I put it back in the box,” Deb said. “And then I got it out again on Monday, and put it back, and I haven’t touched it since, and I’d like to be told that’s normal.”

“That’s completely normal.”

“Is it?”

“How would I know?” Marisol said. “I’ve never fostered anybody. I have a cat and I resent her.” She reached for the bread. “But you told me you’d wait till the fall, and it’s April, and you’ve got the folder in a box instead of in your hand, and that’s a woman doing what she said she’d do. That’s all I’ve got.”

“That’s not nothing.”

“It’s not nothing,” Marisol agreed. “It’s also not a lot. I’d like to be honest with you about the size of it.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The Sundays were the problem and she identified this on the second one.

Weekdays had a shape. She got up at five-forty and was at the school by six-thirty and the day did what it did and she got home at five-forty or, twice a week now, at seven-fifteen, and there was dinner and the light over the stove and a book she was actually reading for the first time in years.

Saturdays had errands in them and Marisol on alternate ones and a shape she could impose.

Sundays had nothing.

The second Sunday she cleaned the oven, which took two hours and was foul. The third Sunday she drove out to the reservoir and walked the loop and came back, which took ninety minutes and left five hours.

That same afternoon, the twelfth, she got out the sewing machine.

She had not touched it since 2016. It had belonged to her mother and it was a 1974 Singer in a cabinet, and it weighed what a small refrigerator weighs, and it had lived under a sheet in the corner of the front room for ten years.

She got it out and cleaned it and oiled it, which she remembered how to do without having to look it up, her hands doing it while her head was somewhere else, and she took the sheet outside and shook it and put it in the wash, and then she sat in front of a sewing machine at two in the afternoon with nothing to sew.

She hemmed four dish towels that did not need hemming.

It was, she reported to Marisol on the Wednesday, an extremely stupid afternoon and the best one she’d had in three weeks.

“Four dish towels.”

“Four dish towels that were already fine.”

“That’s the machine that was your mother’s.”

“That’s the machine that was my mother’s,” Deb said, “and I have not touched it in ten years and I don’t know why not, and I’d rather not turn that into a whole thing, so I’m telling you about the towels and not about the machine.”

“Understood,” said Marisol, and did not mention it again for two months, and then mentioned it exactly once, in June, when Deb made her a set of curtains.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

On the twelfth of April Deb took the pool catalogue down off the microwave.

It had been up there since January and it was, by that point, three months out of date and had been superseded by two subsequent issues, both of which had also gone up on the microwave, so that there was a small stack of them.

She took the whole stack down and put it on the kitchen table and sat with it.

The five-dollar bill was underneath, where it had been since a Wednesday in January when an eighty-one-year-old woman had forced money on a seventeen-year-old for shoveling her walk.

She looked at the Bayside Deluxe with the slide. Four thousand gallons. She had made approximately forty jokes about that pool over three months and had never, not for a second, considered buying it, and the reason she had never considered it was that the joke required a second person and the second person was the entire point.

She sat there for a while.

Then she got her phone and took a photograph of the Bayside Deluxe, the whole page, and sent it to him with no message.

Four minutes later: *absolutely not*

*It’s got a slide.*

*deb its 34 feet. your yard is 30 feet. i have measured your yard*

*When did you measure my yard?*

*2023. i was bored.*

*You measured my yard in 2023 out of boredom.*

*i measure a lot of things. anyway the meadowbrook fits. barely. youd lose the fence*

Deb sat at her kitchen table and typed: *The Meadowbrook’s filter is underpowered.*

The three dots came up immediately.

*THANK YOU. thats what ive been saying since january. nobody in that house ever took the filter issue seriously*

*I took it seriously.*

*you did not. you made a face.*

*I made a face because you were fifteen minutes into a filter argument with a catalogue.*

*someone has to be*

She put the phone down and looked at the stack of catalogues and then, because there was nobody in the house to see her do it, put the top one back on the microwave and threw the other two away.

The five dollars she put in the drawer with the string and the batteries, where it stayed for a number of years.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She did not call Renee in April.

That was, in the end, the whole content of the month: a woman in a house, not making a phone call, going to work, freezing meals, having a bad four minutes in a parking lot on the ninth, and getting the licensing folder out twice and putting it back twice.

Nobody would have been able to see any of it from the outside. Marisol saw about half. Danny, on the Tuesday calls, got a version that was accurate but not complete, and did not push, and Deb understood that not pushing was a thing he had learned to do from a therapist he was bad at and paying for.

On the seventeenth of April, a Friday, she came home from work and found the house entirely silent and stood in the kitchen doorway with her bag still on her shoulder and thought, clearly and without much drama:

*Two more days.*

And then she put her bag down and got the pan out, because it was five-fifty, and five-fifty is what five-fifty is for.


# Chapter 31

[T]{.dropcap}he week before, on the Tuesday call, they had had the first entirely ordinary conversation of the entire six years.

Nothing in it. He had talked about a supplier who had shorted him on a delivery of flex duct and about a coworker who had strong opinions about a truck. She had talked about Bev’s retirement and about the fact that Marisol was making the sheet cake and had been told, explicitly, not to do the frosting she did.

“What’s wrong with the frosting?”

“It’s the boiled kind. It’s her mother’s. It’s magnificent and it’s structurally unsound and it has failed at four consecutive events.”

“Failed how?”

“It slides,” Deb said. “Over the course of about two hours it slides off the cake and pools, and she will not accept this, and she has now been asked in writing by two separate people to use a different frosting.”

Danny laughed for a long time.

And that was the call. Twenty-eight minutes about a supplier and a frosting, and Deb had hung up and stood in her kitchen and thought: *there it is. That’s the thing I’ve missed for six years and I could not have told you what it was.*

Not the big conversations. Not resolution. Twenty-eight minutes of somebody telling you about flex duct.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Danny came up on the Saturday, the eighteenth, which neither of them had arranged as a significant date and which turned out to be one anyway.

He came because of the gutters.

“I’m not letting it go,” he’d said on the Tuesday. “You got on a ladder in March and I’ve thought about it every day since and I’m coming up Saturday with the blower and doing the whole thing and you can sit in a chair and watch me.”

“They’re already done. I did them.”

“You did the fronts. You didn’t do the back, because the back needs the extension and you don’t own the extension.”

Deb had opened her mouth and found nothing in it, because he was correct.

“Nine o’clock,” Danny said. “Don’t make food.”

She made food.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

He arrived at ten past nine in the truck with a leaf blower, an extension ladder strapped to the rack, and a case of water he had bought at a gas station out of some instinct she recognized as entirely his father’s.

The back gutters took him about fifty minutes. Deb did not sit in a chair and watch him, because that was a ridiculous thing to ask of a woman in her own yard, and instead she held the ladder, which he permitted after a negotiation, and handed things up.

Mrs. Petrosyan came out on her porch at some point and looked at Danny at the top of a ladder for a long moment.

“Who is that.”

“That’s my son.”

“Ah,” said Mrs. Petrosyan, in a tone that suggested a mystery of some years had just been solved to her satisfaction, and went back inside.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

While he was up there she went in and got the box.

She had not planned it. She was in the basement for the extension cord and the box was on the shelf where it had been since 2015, DANNY - SCHOOL in her own handwriting, and she stood there for a second and then took it upstairs and put it on the kitchen table and cut the tape.

Report cards. A lot of report cards, going back to second grade, in no order whatsoever. A construction paper turkey with a handprint on it. Three spelling tests, all of which he had done well on, kept for reasons she could no longer reconstruct.

A photograph of a science fair project involving a volcano, taken at an angle that made the volcano look enormous, which she remembered doing on purpose.

And at the bottom, a folded sheet of lined paper that turned out to be a letter he had written in fourth grade, addressed to his future self, an assignment, the teacher’s rubric stapled to it.

*Dear Danny. You are 24 now. I hope you have a truck. I hope you still like the Bengals. Mom says you should be a lawyer but I want to work outside. I hope you did that.*

Deb sat at her kitchen table with a letter written by a nine-year-old in 2005.

*Mom says you should be a lawyer.*

She had no memory of ever saying that. She would have sworn under oath she had never said any such thing. And a nine-year-old had heard it enough times to put it in a letter to himself, and had put next to it what he actually wanted, and had then, at twenty-nine, become a man who installed heating systems for a living and was at that moment on a ladder outside her house.

She folded it and put it back and taped the box and took it down to the basement before he came in.

She would give it to him. Not that day. She had thought about it for about four minutes and decided that handing a man that letter on the same afternoon she apologized to him would be doing two things at once, and she had done enough things at once that year.

She gave it to him in July. He read the letter standing in her kitchen and did not say anything for a while and then said, “I did work outside, though,” and had to go out to the truck for a minute.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

They ate at twelve-thirty. She had made the chicken with the lemon, and she had made it right, and he had two helpings and then stood at the counter picking at the pan the way he had done since he was fourteen years old and which she had forgotten about entirely until she watched him do it.

Afterward they sat in the front room with coffee. The window was open, because it was sixty-one degrees on the eighteenth of April and the whole street smelled like cut grass for the first time that year.

And Deb said, “I want to do the thing now, if that’s all right.”

Danny put his cup down.

“Okay.”

“You said not at the lunch. You said we’d use it up.” She had her hands around her cup. “So I’ve waited four weeks and I’ve thought about it about four hundred times and I’d like to do it now, in this room, and I’d like you to let me get through it.”

“Okay.”

“I said a terrible thing to you in this house in October of 2019.”

“Mom.”

“Let me do it.” She looked at him. “I said you’d end up exactly like your father, chasing something with somebody else’s money and calling it a plan. I said it in the kitchen and I was holding the green mug and you were standing by the fridge, and I remember all of that, which I want you to know, because I think you’ve spent six years assuming I said it and forgot it.”

Danny didn’t say anything.

“It wasn’t a warning,” Deb said. “You were right about that at Bergstrom’s and you were right about it that night, which is why you left. It was a verdict. I had been frightened of you turning into him since you were about nineteen and I had never once said that out loud in a way you could do anything with, and then one night I was tired and I was frightened and I put four years of it into one sentence and handed it to you.”

“You didn’t mean it like that.”

“That doesn’t matter,” Deb said. “That’s the part I’ve worked out. I’ve had a lot of practice this winter at working out what doesn’t matter.” She set her cup down. “I meant *I’m frightened for you.* I said *I’ve already decided how you turn out.* And you were twenty-three and you’d asked your mother for four thousand dollars, and what you got was your mother telling you who you were going to be. And then I didn’t take it back. That’s the actual thing, Danny. Not the sentence. Six years of not taking it back.”

The window was open and somebody down the street was using a saw.

“I’m sorry,” Deb said. “I’m not asking you to do anything with it. I’m not asking you to say it’s all right, because it wasn’t all right, and I’d rather you didn’t.”

Danny sat with his elbows on his knees, looking at the carpet.

It went on long enough to be uncomfortable, and Deb let it, and did not fill it.

“I don’t forgive you yet,” he said.

“Okay.”

“That’s not…” He rubbed his face with both hands. “That’s not a punishment. You’ll take it as one, though. No, listen. You’ll take it as one, and then you’ll go quiet, and in about six weeks you’ll say something like ‘well, I understand’ in a voice, and I’ll hear the voice, and then we’ll be back.”

Deb almost laughed.

“That’s uncannily accurate.”

“I’ve had a lot of years to study it.” He sat back. “I believe you. That’s the first thing. I believe every word you just said and it’s more than I ever expected to get, and it’s landed, and I want you to know it landed.”

“Okay.”

“But I’ve been carrying that sentence since I was twenty-three, and it’s been the thing I think when a job goes wrong, and it’s been the thing I thought when Casey left, which was, by the way, the single worst four months of my life, and I sat in an apartment in Columbus with my mother’s voice in my head telling me she’d called it.” His jaw was tight. “So it’s in there pretty deep. And an apology in a front room on a Saturday doesn’t get it out, and I’d be lying if I told you it did, and I’m not going to lie to you to make a nice afternoon.”

Deb nodded slowly.

“That’s fair,” she said.

“Is it?”

“Danny, it’s more than fair. It’s the correct answer.” She picked her cup back up. “I’d have been suspicious of anything else. If you’d said ‘it’s fine, Mom,’ I’d have known we were back where we were.”

“Yeah.” He exhaled. “Yeah, that’s what my therapist said.”

“You talked to your therapist about this.”

“I talked to my therapist about this for four sessions, which at a hundred and forty a session means this conversation has cost me five hundred and sixty dollars,” Danny said, “and I’d like that noted.”

Deb laughed out loud in her front room.

“It’s noted.”

“I’ve got a receipt.”

“You people and your receipts,” she said, and had to explain the toaster, which took ten minutes and which Danny found so funny that he had to put his coffee down.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

They sat with it for a while, and then Danny said, “Can I ask you about the kid?”

“You’ve never asked about him.”

“I know. I’ve been working out how.” He turned his cup. “Because there’s a version where I ask about him and it’s me being generous, and there’s a version where it’s me checking something, and I’ve been trying to get to a third one.”

“What are you checking?”

“Whether he got the version of you I didn’t.”

Deb put her cup down on the side table.

“That’s an honest question,” she said.

“It’s the only one I’ve got.”

She thought about it for long enough that a lesser man would have filled the silence.

“He got a version of me that was fifty,” she said. “That’s most of it. He got a woman who’d already been through a divorce and a bad year and who had, by then, learned to keep her mouth shut about a great many things, which she had not learned at forty.”

“That’s not an answer.”

“It is, though. That’s the answer.” She looked at him. “You got the version of me who thought she was building something. That’s what I was doing with you, Danny, from the day you were born, and I was completely sincere about it and I would not have described it that way at the time. And when you did something I hadn’t planned for, I said a sentence about what you were going to become, because I thought I was still building.”

Danny didn’t say anything.

“He got a woman who’d given up on building,” Deb said. “That’s the whole difference and it’s not a virtue. I didn’t become wiser. I got beaten out of it. And what was left over was a woman who could feed a boy for three and a half years without a plan for him, and that turned out to be exactly what he needed, and I want to be very clear that I did not arrange it.”

“That’s grim.”

“It’s not grim.” She picked her cup back up. “The one it’s hard on is you. Because you got the earlier one and there’s no way to give you this one retroactively, and I’ve thought about that a great deal since Bergstrom’s, and I don’t have anything for it except that I’m here now and I’m the one that’s left.”

Danny sat with his elbows on his knees.

“That’s more than I’ve had,” he said.


::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

At the door, at four, he said, “So I’ll call Tuesday.”

“Okay.”

“And I’ll come up when the AC needs doing. June, probably. You’ve never had that unit serviced and I’ve been thinking about it since I got here.”

“There’s nothing wrong with the unit.”

“There’s twelve years wrong with the unit.” He got his keys out. “And, Mom.”

“Yeah.”

“The thing I said. About not forgiving you yet.” He was looking at the truck, not at her. “The word’s *yet.* I picked that one. On purpose, I mean. I want you to have the whole word and not just the front half of it.”

Deb stood in her doorway.

“Okay,” she said.

“Okay.”

He went down the steps. At the bottom he turned around.

“Is it tomorrow? The kid’s birthday.”

She had told him. On a Tuesday, in four sentences.

“Tomorrow,” she said.

Danny nodded.

“Text him from me,” he said. “Just, like, happy birthday. From the guy whose room he had.”

And he got in the truck and backed out and lifted two fingers off the wheel on the way past, and Deb stood in the doorway for a minute after he’d gone, and then went inside and washed two cups and put them both away.


# Chapter 32

[T]{.dropcap}he eighteenth had ended with her standing in the kitchen at nine at night looking at a calendar on the fridge with two red boxes on it.

She took it down.

Not to throw away. She had established in March that she was not a woman who threw a calendar away. She took it down and looked at it properly for the first time since December: the box around the twenty-eighth, the box around April third, and the whole four months of small pencil notes in her own hand that she had made without noticing she was making them. *E closing. E off. Dayton 9-1. Renee 4pm.*

Four months of a boy’s schedule in a woman’s handwriting on a calendar on a fridge.

She turned the page to May, which was blank, and hung it back up on the nail.

Then she stood there for a second, and got a pencil, and wrote on the nineteenth, which was tomorrow, and which was on the previous page and therefore already effectively in the past:

*E - 18.*

And on the fifth of June, in the same small hand: *Bev - leaving do.*

And on the seventh of June: *E graduation?* with the question mark, because she had not been told and had not asked and had decided she was not going to ask.

That was the whole of the new calendar. Three entries in seven weeks, one of them with a question mark on it, on a page that had held a dozen a week since 2021.

She looked at it for a while, and then turned the light off over the stove and went up.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The nineteenth of April was a Sunday, and it was seventy-one degrees, and the whole town went outside.

Deb woke at five-forty out of habit and lay there and did not get up for a while, which was new.

At some point in the night, or possibly weeks earlier, in a way she could not locate, she had stopped doing the countdown. She noticed it that morning, lying in bed with the light coming up: that she had not, on waking, produced a number. She had produced numbers every morning since Christmas, without asking for them, the way her hand had produced two plates.

Today the number would have been zero, and she had not gone and got it.

She got up at six-thirty.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She had decided in March that she was not going to text first on the nineteenth.

It had taken about a week of deciding and she had re-made the decision four times, most recently at eleven the night before, lying in bed, with the phone in her hand and a message typed out that said *Happy birthday* and nothing else, which she had looked at for two minutes and deleted.

The reasoning was not complicated and she could state it clearly.

If she texted at eight in the morning, he would reply within about four minutes, because he was scrupulous and would not leave her sitting there. And then the day would contain a thing he had done for her.

If she waited, then either he texted her or he didn’t, and both of those were information, and both of those were his.

She had also known, with a certainty she could not have justified, that he would text.

She was awake at five-forty and it was seventy-one degrees by nine and she had the windows open and she did not look at her phone between six-fifteen and eight-fifty, which she achieved by leaving it face down on the arm of the chair in the front room and doing the laundry.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The text came at eight-fifty.

*case closed*

She was standing at the counter with her second coffee, and she read it and put the cup down.

Two words. He had been in the custody of the state of Ohio since he was twelve years old, and at eight-fifty on a Sunday morning that had ended, and he had sent two words in lowercase and no punctuation.

She looked at it for a while.

Then she typed: *How does it feel?*

The three dots came up. Went away. Came back.

*weird. its just a sunday.*

*Yes.*

*i keep checking my phone like somethings going to happen. nothings going to happen. renee texted me at 8. thats it. thats the whole event.*

*Renee texted you?*

*she said happy birthday and that shes not my caseworker anymore and that the offer about coffee stands. she sent it at 8:01. i think she was waiting.*

*She was absolutely waiting.*

*yeah,* Eli sent. *i think im going to go.*

Deb stood in her kitchen with the sun coming through the window over the sink.

*Danny says happy birthday,* she typed. *He told me to tell you. He said “from the guy whose room he had.”*

There was a longer pause on that one.

*tell him thanks. and that the fourth stair still creaks and he shouldve fixed it*

*He was nineteen when he left, Eli. He didn’t fix anything.*

*sounds about right*

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

She did not ask him what he was doing that day.

She thought about it. She composed it twice, once at nine-ten and once at nine-forty, and both times deleted it, and the reason was not restraint exactly. It was that she had spent four months learning the difference between a question you ask because you want to know and a question you ask because you want to be told you’re still on the list.

At eleven-fifteen he sent her a photograph.

It was a birthday cake. It was a bad one, visibly a grocery store cake, with white icing and blue writing that said ELI in a hand that had clearly not been trained for it, and it was sitting on a table in a large yellow kitchen she recognized from a Saturday in March.

*bernice*

*BERNICE?*

*bernice bought a cake. priya wrote on it. the hospital guy came down. deb i have lived here for THREE WEEKS*

*How did she know it was your birthday?*

*ITS ON THE LEASE*

*She read the date of birth on the lease and bought you a cake.*

*she says “its on the paperwork, im not a stupid woman.” then she made everyone sit down. the hospital guy is martin. ive lived above martin for three weeks and today i found out he does dialysis intake and he has a daughter in cincinnati*

Deb sat down at her kitchen table.

*Did you have the cake?*

*i had two pieces. its not good. its got that icing thats mostly air*

*The whipped kind.*

*the whipped kind. priya says its the worst cake shes ever had and she ate three pieces*

*Eli.*

*yeah?*

She looked at the photograph again. The yellow kitchen, the bad cake, the corner of somebody’s hand at the edge of the frame.

*That’s a page of people,* she typed.

The dots came up. Stayed. Went away. Came back.

*kadeem told you about that?*

*Kadeem told me about it in January. She tells me everything, she’s told me everything for four years, you’ve never once figured that out.*

*unbelievable*

*Did you ever add it?*

A long pause.

*yeah. in march. its got seven names on it now*

*Seven’s good.*

*seven’s good,* he agreed. And then, thirty seconds later: *youre on it*

Deb sat at her kitchen table with her hand over her mouth for a second.

Then she typed: *Obviously.*

*i just thought id say it out loud since its on a page and everything*

*Go eat more bad cake.*

*im going to. priya’s cutting it wrong and i have to intervene*

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

At two in the afternoon she drove out to the cemetery road, which was not a thing she did and which she wanted to be clear with herself about: she did not go to the cemetery. Her mother was in it and she went twice a year and had never found the place useful for anything.

She drove past it. That was the point. The road past it went out to the reservoir and then turned north and came back around by the fairgrounds, and it was ten miles of nothing, and it was seventy-one degrees, and she drove it with the windows down for the first time that year.

The fields had gone from flat brown to that first green that comes up in April in a week and then never stops. Somebody had a hand-painted sign out for eggs, the same sign that had been there in March, with a coffee can on a stump.

She stopped and bought eggs. Two dollars in the can, one carton off the stump, nobody in sight.

They were, she discovered later, extremely good eggs, and she bought them from that stump for the next nine years.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

At three-thirty she got out the licensing folder for the third time.

She sat with it at the kitchen table with the back door open. The renewal was due in October. There was a form for continuing education hours, which she had, and a form for the fire inspection, which was current, and a page about the home study update, which was routine at this point.

Twenty minutes.

She could fill it in inside twenty minutes and drop it at the county office and by June there would be a bedroom upstairs with somebody in it, and Marisol would come Wednesdays and Fridays, and the whole apparatus would resume, and everybody who knew her would say it was good that she was keeping busy.

She read the continuing education page twice without taking any of it in.

And then, because the renewal form had a line on it for the year and the year was sitting there in her own handwriting, she thought about Charlotte.

October. That was what Charlotte had said, standing in a doorway with a clipboard in Deb’s hand: *when would I need to say*, and *October*, and Deb had put it in a drawer for three weeks without once taking it out to look at. Nine thousand more. Monday to Friday. No six a.m. Thirteen buildings’ worth of ordering that was a mess and that she had been right about in every email since 2018.

She sat at her kitchen table on the nineteenth of April and understood, for the first time, that the two pieces of paper were the same piece of paper. She could not do the office and the bedroom. Not properly, not both, not at fifty-four, and anybody who told her she could had never placed a produce order nine days out for a building she was not standing in. One of them was a woman who ran a house. The other was a woman who ran thirteen kitchens and came home at five to a room with a sewing machine in it.

She had never in her life held two futures at once and been allowed to want either.

She did not decide. She sat with both of them on the table in front of her, in the sun, with the back door open, for slightly under four minutes.

Then she closed the folder, and got up, and put it back in the file box in the front room, and did not get it out again until the fourteenth of September, which was a Monday.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Marisol came at five.

She came because it was a Sunday and because Deb had texted her on Friday and said *come Sunday*, and Marisol had texted back *why*, and Deb had texted *because I said so*, and that had been the end of the negotiation.

She brought wine, which she never did, and a bag of the good bread from the place on Fourth, and put both on the counter and said, “What are we doing.”

“We’re eating.”

“We eat constantly. What are we doing?”

“Marisol.” Deb had the oven on and the pan out and the chicken already dried on the board. “Sit down.”

She sat down.

And Deb Hale, in her own kitchen, on a Sunday evening in April, went to the cupboard and got down two plates.

She counted them. She stood there for a second with her hand on the stack and counted out two, deliberately, the way you count out change, and put them on the table.

Two.

Not one and one left on the counter. Not four out of habit with two going back. Two, for two people, because there were two people in the house and one of them was sitting at the table complaining about the wine she herself had brought.

“This is a bad Riesling,” Marisol said. “I want to say that up front. I bought it because it was nine dollars and I’ve now read the label.”

“Then why did you buy it?”

“Because it was nine dollars, Deb, I’ve just said.”

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

They ate at six.

The chicken was very good. The bread was excellent. The Riesling was, as advertised, bad, and they drank most of it anyway.

At some point Marisol said, “How is he?”

“He’s had two pieces of a grocery store cake and he’s got a landlady who reads lease paperwork.”

“Good.”

“And a nursing student who cuts cake wrong, apparently, and a man called Martin who does dialysis intake.”

“He knows the neighbors’ jobs after three weeks.”

“He knows the neighbors’ jobs after three weeks,” Deb said, “and he’s put seven names on a piece of paper, and he told me one of them without being asked, which I think might be the single largest thing that’s happened to me this year.”

Marisol picked up the bad wine.

“Well,” she said.

“Don’t.”

“I’m not doing anything.”

“You’re going to do a thing.”

“I’m going to raise a glass in a kitchen, which is a normal thing that people do,” Marisol said, “and if you make it weird, that’s on you.”

She raised the glass.

Deb raised hers.

Neither of them said anything, because there was nothing available that wasn’t either too small or too large, and they drank the bad Riesling, and that was the whole ceremony.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

“So I’m going to say a thing,” Marisol said, over the last of the bad wine, “and then we’re going to talk about something else for the rest of the evening, and I want that clearly understood as a condition.”

“Understood.”

“It’s April nineteenth.”

“It is.”

“And three weeks ago I told you not to decide anything until the fall.” Marisol turned her glass. “I want to withdraw that.”

Deb looked up.

“Withdraw it?”

“Not the timing. The timing’s right, you should do nothing until September. I want to withdraw the way I said it, because I said it like it was medicine and I’ve thought about it since.” She set the glass down. “I made it sound like you were being punished. Sit in the empty house, be bored, don’t fill it. Like the point was the suffering.”

“Wasn’t it?”

“No. The point was that you’ve never in eleven years found out what you’re like with nothing in your hands.” Marisol shrugged. “And now you are going to, whether either of us likes it, and I’d rather you went into it thinking of it as finding something out than as serving a sentence.”

Deb turned that over.

“I don’t know what I’m like with nothing in my hands,” she said.

“Nobody does. That’s not a diagnosis, that’s just a woman who’s been busy since 1994.”

“What if I don’t like her?”

“Then you’ll know that,” Marisol said, “and you’ll call Renee in September and you’ll do it on purpose, which is a completely different thing from doing it in May because the house is loud.” She reached for the bread. “Either way you’ll have found out. That’s what I’m proposing. It’s not a program.”

Deb looked at her kitchen, which had two plates in it and a bad Riesling and the last of the bread.

“You’ve been thinking about this.”

“I’ve been thinking about it in the car for three weeks, Deb. You’ve got me driving over here twice a week now, I’ve had nothing else to do in that car.”


::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

Afterward, Marisol dried and Deb washed, which was the reverse of the arrangement and which neither of them commented on.

At the door, coat on, Marisol said, “Friday?”

“Friday.”

“I’m bringing nothing.”

“You never bring anything.”

“I brought wine.”

“You brought bad wine and drank most of it.”

“That’s still bringing,” Marisol said, and went down the steps, and got into her car, and did the thing she always did where she started the engine and then sat there for a minute looking at her phone before pulling out, and Deb stood in the doorway and watched her do it and did not go inside until the car had gone.

::: scene-break
\* \* \*
:::

The house was quiet.

She washed the two plates and put them in the rack, and then, because they were dry from the rinse, she picked them up and put them away in the cupboard properly, which took four seconds and which she did without thinking about it at all.

Upstairs, the door to the empty room was open. It had been open since the twenty-eighth of March. Sometime that summer she would move the sewing machine in there, and sometime the summer after that she would take the dresser out and put a chair by the window, and it would become, gradually and without ceremony, a room in her house.

She turned off the light over the stove.

On the counter sat a toaster, and on the microwave beside it, a pool catalogue.

She went to bed at ten and lay there listening to a house with one person in it, and thought about a boy in a yellow kitchen a mile and a half away who had been in the custody of the state of Ohio that morning and now wasn’t, and who was, at this exact moment, almost certainly in an argument about how to cut a cake.

The phone went off on the nightstand at ten-twenty.

*bernice has ONE cake knife*

Deb lay in the dark and laughed out loud in an empty house.

Then she typed: *Tell me everything.*

And he did.

# The End {.unnumbered .unlisted .the-end}
